A Better-Than- 5 / 4 5/4 -Approximation for Two-Edge Connectivity
Felix Hommelsheim, Alexander Lindermayr, Zhenwei Liu

Abstract
The 2-Edge-Connected Spanning Subgraph Problem (2ECSS) is a fundamental problem in survivable network design. Given an undirected 2 2 -edge-connected graph, the goal is to find a 2 2 -edge-connected spanning subgraph with the minimum number of edges; a graph is 2-edge-connected if it is connected after the removal of any single edge. 2ECSS is 𝖠𝖯𝖷 \mathsf{APX} -hard and has been extensively studied in the context of approximation algorithms. Very recently, Bosch-Calvo, Garg, Grandoni, Hommelsheim, Jabal Ameli, and Lindermayr showed the currently best-known approximation ratio of 5 / 4 \nicefrac{{5}}{{4}} [STOC 2025]. This factor is tight for many of their techniques and arguments, and it was not clear whether 5 / 4 \nicefrac{{5}}{{4}} can be improved. We break this natural barrier and present a ( 5 / 4 − η ) (\nicefrac{{5}}{{4}}-\eta) -approximation algorithm, for some constant η ≥ 10 − 6 \eta\geq 10^{-6} . On a high level, we follow the approach of previous works: take a triangle-free 2 2 -edge cover and transform it into a 2-edge-connected spanning subgraph by adding only a few additional edges. For ≥ 5 / 4 \geq\nicefrac{{5}}{{4}} -approximations, one can heavily exploit that a 4 4 -cycle in the 2-edge cover can “buy” one additional edge. This enables simple and nice techniques, but immediately fails for our improved approximation ratio. To overcome this, we design two complementary algorithms that perform well for different scenarios: one for few 4 4 -cycles and one for many 4 4 -cycles. Besides this, there appear more obstructions when breaching 5 / 4 \nicefrac{{5}}{{4}} , which we surpass via new techniques such as colorful bridge covering, rich vertices, and branching gluing paths.



1
Introduction

Survivable network design studies how to compute cost-effective networks with connectivity properties
that are resilient to edge or node failures. One of the most important and fundamental problems
in this area is the 2-Edge-Connected Spanning Subgraph Problem (2ECSS). Here, we are given an
undirected 2-edge-connected (2EC) graph
G
, and the goal is to compute a 2-edge-connected spanning
subgraph (2ECSS)
H
of
G
with a minimum number of edges.

2ECSS is
\mathsf{APX}
-hard
[
Fer98
,
CL99
]
, and admits a simple
2
-approximation by augmenting a DFS tree. Substantial research has been devoted to prove smaller approximation ratios using non-trivial techniques.
After two early better-than-2 approximations
[
KV92
,
CSS01
]
, until recently, the best known approximation ratio remained
\nicefrac{{4}}{{3}}
, which was achieved independently with different techniques by
[
SV14
]
and
[
HVV19
]
.
In a breakthrough result,
[
GGJ23
]
improved the
ratio to
\nicefrac{{118}}{{89}}+\varepsilon<1.326
, which was quickly tuned to
1.3+\varepsilon
by

[
KN23
]
by replacing a 2-edge cover, which is used as a lower
bound in
[
GGJ23
]
, with a triangle-free 2-edge cover.
While this already reduced the technical complexity of
[
GGJ23
]
, very recently,

[
Bos+25
]
made a substantial leap forward to a
\nicefrac{{5}}{{4}}
-approximation via an even simpler proof. Their key idea is to always glue smaller 2EC components to a large 2EC component, which eventually becomes a 2ECSS.
[
Bos+25
]
is the result of merging the best parts of two independent works, by
[
BGJ24
]
and
[
GHL24
]
, who achieved this approximation ratio with slightly different techniques.
1
1

1

[
GHL24
]
showed an approximation ratio of
\nicefrac{{5}}{{4}}+\varepsilon
.

This state of affairs strongly indicates that
\nicefrac{{5}}{{4}}
is a natural barrier for
many
known techniques used for 2ECSS.
Despite this, we breach the factor of
\nicefrac{{5}}{{4}}
and show the following result.
We did not optimize the constant
\eta
too much and aimed for a simple approach whenever possible.

Theorem 1
.

There is a deterministic
(\nicefrac{{5}}{{4}}-\eta)
-approximation algorithm for 2ECSS that runs in polynomial time for some
\eta\geq 10^{-6}
.

While the quantitative improvement over
[
Bos+25
]

is only small, our result gives qualitative insights to approximating 2ECSS.
Many techniques and arguments in
[
Bos+25
]
are tight for the approximation ratio
\nicefrac{{5}}{{4}}
,
and it was not clear whether this factor can be improved by
building up on the framework used
in
[
GGJ23
,
BGJ24
,
GHL24
,
KN23
,
Bos+25
]
. This approach broadly consists of the following four steps:

1.

Preprocessing:
Reduce the input graph to a structured graph with additional properties.

2.

Canonical 2-edge cover:
Compute a subgraph
H
with
|H|\leq\mathsf{opt}
(a minimum triangle-free 2-edge cover) and impose additional structural properties on it.

3.

Bridge covering:
Cover the bridges of
H
so that
H
is composed of 2EC components.

4.

Gluing:
Glue the components of
H
together to a 2ECSS.

Bridge covering and gluing may also happen simultaneously.
As our main technical contributions, we introduce new techniques to overcome the following challenges that appear in this framework for approximation ratios
\alpha<\nicefrac{{5}}{{4}}
:

•

For bridge covering and gluing, we keep track of the cost of our solution via a credit scheme where each edge of the initial 2-edge cover receives a credit of
\alpha-1
. For
\alpha\geq\nicefrac{{5}}{{4}}
, even
4
-cycles, which are the smallest 2EC components that might appear, have a total credit of at least
1
and thus can “buy” one edge to glue themselves to another component—a crucial argument that was extensively used in previous work. This immediately fails for
\alpha<\nicefrac{{5}}{{4}}
. We address this by a careful case distinction on the number of
4
-cycles in the initial 2-edge cover, and then combine two very different approximation algorithms.

•

In the preprocessing, we contract constant-sized subgraphs for which any solution must contain many edges. Intuitively, these
contractible
subgraphs can be trivially included in an
\alpha
-approximation. For
\alpha\geq\nicefrac{{5}}{{4}}
,
5
-cycles are contractible if any solution must include at least
4
edges, which streamlines many arguments. If
\alpha<\nicefrac{{5}}{{4}}
,
5
-cycles are only contractible if every 2ECSS includes all
5
edges. This is much less likely, and makes this technique much weaker.

•

As a consequence of the previous point, we cannot enforce as much structure on complex components (connected component with bridges) that appear in intermediate steps compared to
[
Bos+25
,
GGJ23
]
. This leads to several complications when covering bridges. To fix this, we allow complex components initially to charge loans to satisfy invariants, which we carefully repay during bridge covering using a
color encoding
. Moreover, we allow uncovered (isolated) vertices in our 2-edge cover during bridge covering, which can glue themselves “on their own” to a 2EC component later (
rich vertices
).

We give a walk-through of our approach in
Section

2
, and describe how we address and solve these challenges in more detail.

As a byproduct of our approach, we also give the following conditional result.

Theorem 2
.

If there exists a polynomial-time
\mu
-approximation algorithm for computing a minimum
\{3,4\}
-cycle-free 2-edge cover of a 2-edge-connected graph, then there exists a polynomial-time
(1.24\mu+\varepsilon)
-approximation algorithm for 2ECSS for every
\varepsilon>0
.

It is known that a minimum triangle-free 2-edge cover can be computed in polynomial time
[
KN23
]
, as it is equivalent to computing a triangle-free 2-matching
[
Har24
,
Pal23
]
. For both, a PTAS is also available
[
BGJ23a
,
KN25
]
. On the other side, the problem of computing a minimum
\{3,4,5\}
-cycle-free 2-matching is known to be NP-hard
[
CP80
]
. The complexity status of a minimum
\{3,4\}
-cycle-free 2-matching and its cover variant are still open.

1.1
Related Work

In network augmentation problems, we are given a graph with a specific structure
(e.g., spanning tree, matching, forest, cactus, or disjoint paths) along with a set
of additional edges (called links). The task is to add a minimum-sized set of links to
the structure so that the resulting graph becomes spanning and 2-edge connected;
in 2ECSS we start with an empty graph.
For the (spanning) Tree Augmentation Problem, several better-than-
2
-approximations are
known
[
Adj19
,
CTZ21
,
CG18
,
CG18a
,
CN13
,
Eve+09
,
Fio+18
,
GKZ18
,
KN16
,
KN18
,
Nag03
,
Nut21
,
TZ21
,
TZ22
]
, where the currently best-known approximation ratio is 1.393
[
CTZ21
]
.
For the Forest Augmentation Problem, the first non-trivial approximation was obtained by
[
GJT22
]
with a ratio of 1.9973, which recently has been slightly improved
[
Hom25
]
.
For the Matching Augmentation Problem,
[
GHM23
]
gave a
\nicefrac{{13}}{{8}}
-approximation after several other non-trivial results
[
BDS22
,
Che+18
,
Che+23
]
.
In the
k
-Connectivity Augmentation Problem (kCAP), we are given a
k
-edge-connected graph and a set of links with the goal of adding as few links as possible to make the graph
(k+1)
-edge connected.
The k-Edge-Connected Spanning Subgraph Problem for
k\geq 2
is another natural generalization of 2ECSS (see, e.g.,
[
Cha+22
,
CT00
,
GG12
,
Gab+09
,
HKZ24
]
).

All the above problems have a natural weighted version.
A general result by Jain
[
Jai01
]
yields a
2
-approximation. For the Weighted Tree Augmentation Problem, the current best approximation ratio is
1.5+\varepsilon
by
[
TZ22
]
.
Recently,
[
TZ23
]
also obtained a
(1.5+\varepsilon)
-approximation algorithm for the weighted version of kCAP.
For Weighted 2ECSS, obtaining a better-than-2-approximation is a major open problem.

The
2
-Vertex-Connected Spanning Subgraph Problem (2VCSS) is the vertex-connectivity version of 2ECSS.
Here, we are given a 2EC graph
G
, and the goal is to find the 2-vertex-connected
2
2

2

A graph is
k
-vertex-connected (kVC) if it remains connected after the removal of an arbitrary subset of
k-1
vertices.

spanning subgraph
H
with minimum number of edges. There are various trivial 2-approximations.
[
KV92
]
gave the first non-trivial
\nicefrac{{5}}{{3}}
-approximation, which was improved to
\nicefrac{{3}}{{2}}

[
GVS93
]
and
\nicefrac{{10}}{{7}}

[
HV17
]
. Recently,
[
BGJ23
]
gave a
\nicefrac{{4}}{{3}}
-approximation.

Closely related is also the Traveling Salesperson Problem (TSP).
The best-known approximations for metric TSP
[
Chr22
,
KKG24
]
make use of the Help-Karp LP-relaxation of the problem, which has an integrality gap of
\nicefrac{{4}}{{3}}
. The example of this integrality gap is actually an instance of graphic TSP:
given a graph
G
, the task is to find an Eulerian multigraph with a minimum the number of edges. 2ECSS is a relaxation of graphic TSP.
Sebö and Vygen
[
SV14
]
proved the currently best approximation guarantee of
\nicefrac{{7}}{{5}}
for graphic TSP. Interestingly, they use very similar techniques to also obtain a
\nicefrac{{4}}{{3}}
-approximation for 2ECSS, which highlights the close connection between both problems.
Thus, advancements for 2ECSS may lead to better bounds for graphic TSP.

1.2
Preliminaries

We use standard graph theory notation. For a graph
G
,
V(G)
and
E(G)
refers to its vertex and edge sets, respectively. For a graph
G
, and a vertex set
S\subseteq V(G)
,
G[S]
refers to the subgraph of
G
induced on the vertex set
S
. We use components of a graph to refer to its maximal connected subgraphs.
A
k
-vertex cut in a graph for
k\geq 1
refers to a set of
k
vertices such that deleting them results in increasing the number of components of the graph.
We call the single vertex of a 1-vertex cut a cut vertex.
A graph with no cut vertices is called 2-vertex connected (2VC).
A bridge in a graph refers to an edge in the graph whose removal results in increasing the number of components in the graph. Note that a connected graph without bridges is 2-edge connected (2EC). A cycle refers to a simple cycle.
We use
\mathcal{C}_{i}
to represent a cycle on
i
vertices.
Given a graph
G
and a vertex set
S\subseteq V(G)
,
G|S
denotes the graph obtained from contracting the vertex set
S
into a single vertex. Graph contraction may give rise to parallel edges and self-loops. The edges of
G
and
G|S
are in one-to-one correspondence. For a graph
G
and a subgraph
H
, we use
G|H
to denote
G|V(H)
.
For a graph
G
, we use
|G|
to denote
|E(G)|
.
For a 2EC graph
G
, we use
\mathsf{OPT}(G)
to denote a 2ECSS of
G
with the minimum number of edges. We define
\mathsf{opt}(G)\coloneq|\mathsf{OPT}(G)|
(when
G
is clear from the context we may just say
\mathsf{opt}
and
\mathsf{OPT}
instead).

2
Overview of Our Approach

In this section, we present our algorithm and give an outline of our analysis. As an input to the problem, we are given a 2EC graph
G
.

2.1
Reduction to Structured Graphs

The first step of our approach is to reduce the input graph
to a graph with more structural properties while only sacrificing little in the approximation ratio. We will later use these structural properties extensively to prove a good approximation for 2ECSS on structured graphs.

In
[
GGJ23
,
GHL24
,
Bos+25
]
,

(\alpha,\varepsilon)
-structured graphs have sufficiently many vertices and
do not contain
\alpha
-contractible subgraphs, irrelevant edges, non-isolating 2-vertex cuts, or large 3-vertex cuts.
We defer definitions of these previously considered structures to
Appendix

A
.
In this work, we impose additional connectivity properties of structured graphs by preprocessing
\mathcal{C}_{k}
cuts and large
4
-vertex cuts. A
\mathcal{C}_{k}
cut
is a set of vertices
\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{k}\}
that forms a cycle in
G
whose removal increases the number of connected components in
G
.
We define structured graphs as follows.

Definition 1

((
\alpha,\varepsilon)
-structured graph)
.

Given
\alpha\geq 1
and
\varepsilon>0
, a graph
G
is
(\alpha,\varepsilon)
-
structured
if it is simple, 2VC, it contains at least
\nicefrac{{8}}{{\varepsilon}}
vertices, and it does not contain irrelevant edges,

1.

\alpha
-contractible subgraphs of size at most
\nicefrac{{8}}{{\varepsilon}}
,

2.

non-isolating 2-vertex cuts,

3.

large 3-vertex cuts (each side has
\geq\lceil\frac{2}{\alpha-1}\rceil-1
vertices; in this paper, this is equal to
8
),

4.

large
\mathcal{C}_{k}
cuts for
k\in\{4,5,6,7,8\}
(each side has
\geq\lceil\frac{k}{\alpha-1}\rceil-1
vertices; in this paper, this is equal to
4k
), and

5.

large
4
-vertex cuts (each side has
\geq\lceil\frac{6}{\alpha-1}\rceil-1
vertices; in this paper, this is equal to
24
).

[
GGJ23
]
gave an approximation-preserving reduction for approximation ratios
\alpha\geq\nicefrac{{6}}{{5}}
to instances without parallel edges, self loops, irrelevant edges, cut-vertices, small
\alpha
-contractible subgraphs, and non-isolating 2-vertex cuts. This reduction was also used in
[
Bos+25
]
.
Afterwards,
[
GHL24
]
significantly extended this reduction to instances without
large 3-vertex cuts for approximation ratios
\alpha\geq\nicefrac{{5}}{{4}}
, which loses a factor of
1+\varepsilon
in the final
approximation ratio.

Our contribution for this step is twofold. First, we show that large 3-vertex cuts can also be avoided for approximation ratios
\alpha\geq\nicefrac{{6}}{{5}}

by adapting the requirement on the number of vertices on each side of the cut. Second, we show that we additionally reduce to instances without large
\mathcal{C}_{k}
cuts for
k\in\{4,5,6,7,8\}
and large
4
-vertex cuts for approximation ratios
\alpha\geq\nicefrac{{6}}{{5}}
.
The proof of the following reduction is given in
Appendix

A
.

Lemma 3
.

For all
\alpha\in[\nicefrac{{6}}{{5}},\nicefrac{{5}}{{4}}]
and
\varepsilon\in(0,\nicefrac{{1}}{{100}}]
, if there exists a deterministic polynomial-time
\alpha
-approximation algorithm for 2ECSS on (
\alpha,\varepsilon)
-structured graphs, then there exists a deterministic polynomial-time
(\alpha+4\varepsilon)
-approximation algorithm for 2ECSS.

For the proof of
Theorem

1
, we will use
\alpha\coloneq\nicefrac{{5}}{{4}}-10^{-5}
and
\varepsilon\coloneq 10^{-6}
.
We will show in the following the existence of a deterministic polynomial-time
\alpha
-approximation algorithm for 2ECSS on structured graphs. Thus, from now on we simply write contractible and structured instead of
\alpha
-contractible and
(\alpha,\varepsilon)
-structured, respectively.

A crucial benefit of structured graphs is the existence of large matchings between vertex bipartitions. In the analysis of our algorithm for structured graphs, we will make generous use of the following matching lemmas, similar to
[
GGJ23
,
GHL24
,
Bos+25
]
.

Lemma 4

(
3
-Matching Lemma
[
GGJ23
]
)
.

Let
G
be a structured graph.
Consider any partition
(V_{1},V_{2})
of
V
such that for each
i\in\{1,2\}
,
|V_{i}|\geq 3
, and if
|V_{i}|=3
, then
G[V_{i}]
is a triangle.
Then, there exists a matching of size
3
between
V_{1}
and
V_{2}
.

Lemma 5

(4-Matching Lemma
[
GHL24
]
)
.

Let
G
be a structured graph.
Consider any partition
(V_{1},V_{2})
of
V(G)
such that for each
i\in\{1,2\}
,
\lvert V_{i}\rvert\geq\lceil\frac{2}{\alpha-1}\rceil+2=11
.
Then, there exists a matching of size
4
between
V_{1}
and
V_{2}
in
G
.

2.2
Canonical 2-Edge Cover

From now on, we assume that we are given a structured input graph
G
.
Our next step is to compute a lower bound on the size of a minimum 2ECSS of
G
.
For this, we follow the approach of
[
KN23
,
Bos+25
]
, and compute a minimum triangle-free 2-edge cover
H
of
G
, which can be done in polynomial time
[
KN23
,
Har24
]
.
Formally, a 2-edge cover is a set of edges of a graph
G
such that every vertex of
G
is incident to at least two edges of the cover.
A 2-edge cover is triangle-free if it does not contain a triangle as a component. We have
|H|\leq\mathsf{opt}(G)
, because every feasible solution to 2ECSS is a 2-edge cover and triangle-free, because it is connected and contains at least
4
vertices.

Given an arbitrary minimum triangle-free 2-edge cover
H
, we next transform it in polynomial time into a
canonical
minimum triangle-free 2-edge cover
H_{0}
of
G
to impose the following properties.

Definition 2

(complex)
.

Let
H
be a
2
-edge cover of some graph.
We call
H

bridgeless
if it contains no bridges, i.e., all the components of
H
are
2
-edge connected.
A component of
H
is
complex
if it contains a bridge.
Inclusion-wise maximal
2
-edge-connected subgraphs of
H
are
blocks
.
A block
B
of some complex component
C
of
H
is called
pendant
if
C\setminus V(B)
is connected.
Otherwise it is non-pendant.
Note that by contracting each block of a complex component to a single vertex, we obtain a tree whose leaves correspond to pendant blocks.

Definition 3

(canonical)
.

A
2
-edge cover
H
is
canonical
if

•

each non-complex component of
H
is either a
\mathcal{C}_{i}
, for
4\leq i\leq 8
, or contains at least
9
edges,

•

for every complex component, each of its pendant blocks
B
either

–

consists of a
\mathcal{C}_{5}
, labeled clockwise as
{b_{1},b_{2},b_{3},b_{4},b_{5}}
, with
b_{1}
incident to the bridge, and
b_{2},b_{5}
have degree
2
in
G
. We call such a
\mathcal{C}_{5}
a
nice

\mathcal{C}_{5}
,

–

or has at least
6
vertices,

•

for every complex component, each of its non-pendant blocks
B
has at least
5
edges,

•

there are no two non-complex components of
H
that are both
\mathcal{C}_{4}
’s and can be merged into a single
\mathcal{C}_{8}
by removing two edges of
H
and adding two edges, and

•

H
contains a component that has at least
32
vertices.

(a)

A canonical 2-edge cover
H
(solid edges) of a graph
G
(solid and dashed edges). Vertices of
G
are black-filled small circles, 2EC components of
H
with at least
9
vertices and blocks of complex components are white-filled big circles.

(b)

The component graph
\bm{\hat{}}{G}_{H}
of the canonical 2-edge cover
H
shown in
Figure

1(a)
. Here,
\mathcal{C}
denotes the node of a complex component,
\mathcal{L}
of a large component, and
\mathcal{H}
of a huge component. The left
5
nodes and the
3
right nodes each form a segment.

Figure 1
:
An example of a 2-edge cover and its component graph.

Our definition of canonical is similar to
[
GGJ23
,
Bos+25
]
, except that in this paper components with
8
edges have to be a
\mathcal{C}_{8}
, each pendant block of a complex component that is a
\mathcal{C}_{5}
has to be nice, and we cannot further merge two
\mathcal{C}_{4}
’s into a single
\mathcal{C}_{8}
.
Merging two
\mathcal{C}_{4}
’s whenever possible is new and crucial to later steps of our pipeline.
In
Appendix

B
, we show that we can compute a canonical 2-edge cover from a minimum triangle-free 2-edge cover in polynomial time.

Lemma 6
.

Given a structured graph
G
, we can compute a canonical
2
-edge cover
H_{0}
of
G
in polynomial time, where
H_{0}
is also a minimum triangle-free
2
-edge cover of
G
.

2.3
Credit Schemes, Bridge Covering, and Gluing

There are two reasons why a canonical 2-edge cover
H_{0}
of a structured graph
G
is not already a 2ECSS of
G
: First,
H_{0}
might have multiple components. Second, some of these components might have bridges.
In the following two subsections, we will present two algorithms that transform
H_{0}
into a 2ECSS of
G
by covering bridges and gluing components together.
Throughout these transformations, we will keep the property that the intermediate solution is a canonical 2-edge cover.

To keep track of the size of our solution throughout these modifications, we use a
credit-based argument
.
Initially, we assign each edge in
H_{0}
a certain credit, and then redistribute the credits to the
(sub)-components according to a
credit scheme
, which we maintain throughout the algorithms. We use different credit schemes for each algorithm. For a 2-edge cover
H
, we define
\mathrm{cr}(H)
as the sum of all credits assigned to
H
, and the
cost
of
H
as
\mathrm{cost}(H)=|H|+\mathrm{cr}(H)
.
If we can modify
H_{0}
to a 2ECSS
H
in polynomial time such that (i)
\mathrm{cost}(H_{0})\leq\alpha\cdot|H_{0}|
and (ii)
\mathrm{cost}(H)\leq\mathrm{cost}(H_{0})
,
we have that
H
is a
\alpha
-approximation on structured graphs, since the credits are non-negative.

To modify
H_{0}
to a 2ECSS, we mainly work on the component graph
\bm{\hat{}}{G}_{H}
of
G
.

Definition 4

(component graph and segments)
.

The
component graph

\bm{\hat{}}{G}_{H}

w.r.t.
G
and
H
is the multigraph obtained from
G
by contracting the vertices of each 2EC component of
H
into a single node.
We call the vertices of
\bm{\hat{}}{G}_{H}

nodes
to distinguish them from the vertices in
G
. There is a one-to-one correspondence between the edges of
\bm{\hat{}}{G}_{H}
and
G
. Furthermore, there is a one-to-one correspondence between the nodes of
\bm{\hat{}}{G}_{H}
and the components of
H
. We will denote the component corresponding to a node
A
by
C_{A}
. We say a
segment
of
\bm{\hat{}}{G}_{H}
is an inclusion-wise maximal 2-vertex-connected subgraph of
\bm{\hat{}}{G}_{H}
.

For ease of exposition, we remove all self-loops from the component graph
\bm{\hat{}}{G}_{H}
.
Observe that the component graph
\bm{\hat{}}{G}_{H}
is 2EC, because
G
is 2EC and contracting a set of vertices or deleting self-loops in a 2EC graph results in a 2EC graph. We can break
\bm{\hat{}}{G}_{H}
into segments in polynomial time such that two segments only overlap at a cut node of
\bm{\hat{}}{G}_{H}

[
Wes01
,
KV02
]
.

Definition 5

(large and huge components)
.

We say that a component
C
of a canonical 2-edge cover is
huge
if
|V(C)|\geq 32
, and
large
if it is 2EC and
|V(C)|\geq 9
.

2.4
Case Distinction on the Number of
\mathcal{C}_{4}
s in
H_{0}
(Proof of
Theorem

1
)

Next, we will present two different algorithms that convert a canonical 2-edge cover
H_{0}
into a 2ECSS of
G
. Each algorithm handles a different case depending on the number of
\mathcal{C}_{4}
’s in
H_{0}
. To this end, let
\beta\in[0,1]
such that the number of edges of
\mathcal{C}_{4}
’s in
H_{0}
is
\beta|H_{0}|
.
We distinguish two cases:

•

Case

FEW
(
Lemma

7
, see
Section

2.5
): If
\beta\leq 1-\frac{1}{1000}
, there is an algorithm that computes in polynomial time a 2ECSS
H
of
G
such that
|H|\leq\left(\frac{5}{4}-(1-\beta)\frac{1}{100}\right)\cdot|H_{0}|\leq 1.24999\cdot\mathsf{opt}
.

•

Case

MANY
(
Lemma

13
, see
Section

2.6
): If
\beta>1-\frac{1}{1000}
, there is an algorithm that computes in polynomial time a 2ECSS
H
of
G
such that
|H|\leq\left(\frac{5}{4}-\frac{1}{28}\right)\cdot\beta|H_{0}|+5(1-\beta)\cdot|H_{0}|+\frac{1}{35}\cdot\mathsf{opt}\leq 1.24999\cdot\mathsf{opt}
.

Here we use that
|H_{0}|\leq\mathsf{opt}
. Given these two algorithm, we can easily prove
Theorems

1
and
2
.

Proof of
Theorem

1
.

Combining
Lemmas

7
and
13
yields a polynomial-time
(\nicefrac{{5}}{{4}}-10^{-5})
-approximation algorithm for 2ECSS on structured graphs.
We now apply
Lemma

3
with
\alpha=\nicefrac{{5}}{{4}}-10^{-5}
and
\varepsilon=10^{-6}
to conclude
Theorem

1
.
∎

Proof of
Theorem

2
.

We first preprocess the input graph
G
using
Lemma

3
using
\alpha=1.24
. In particular, we do not exclude large
4
-vertex cuts and large
\mathcal{C}_{k}
cuts for
4\leq k\leq 8
, because we do not need them for
FEW
. Then, we use the given algorithm to compute a
\mu
-approximation
H_{0}
of a minimum
\{3,4\}
-cycle-free 2-edge cover of
G
.
Note that a minimum
\{3,4\}
-cycle-free 2-edge cover of a 2-edge-connected graph
G
is a lower bound on a feasible 2ECSS of
G
.
Hence,
|H_{0}|\leq\mu\cdot\mathsf{opt}
.
Now, we make
H_{0}
canonical (
Lemma

6
) and apply the algorithm for
FEW
with the starting solution
H_{0}
and
\beta=0
. Hence, we obtain a 2ECSS
H
with
|H|\leq\left(\frac{5}{4}-\frac{1}{100}\right)|H_{0}|=1.24\mu\cdot\mathsf{opt}
(cf.
Lemma

7
).
∎

The next two subsections give an outline on the proofs of
Lemma

7
and
Lemma

13
, respectively.

2.5
Algorithm for
FEW

First, we present an approach that gives a good approximation ratio for
FEW
, i.e.,
\beta\leq 1-\frac{1}{1000}
, and prove the following lemma. We defer proofs and further details to
Section

3
.

Lemma 7
.

Given a structured graph
G
and a canonical
2
-edge cover
H_{0}
, in polynomial time we can compute a 2EC spanning subgraph
H
of
G
such that

|H|\leq\frac{5}{4}\beta|H_{0}|+\left(\frac{5}{4}-\frac{1}{100}\right)(1-\beta)|H_{0}|=\left(\frac{5}{4}-(1-\beta)\frac{1}{100}\right)|H_{0}|\ ,

where
\beta\in[0,1]
such that the number of edges of
\mathcal{C}_{4}
’s in
H_{0}
is
\beta|H_{0}|
.

On a high-level, we use a similar framework as in
[
Bos+25
]
:
We start from a canonical
2
-edge cover
H_{0}
and first modify it to a bridgeless one.
This is particularly challenging due to insufficient credits on some complex components and
\mathcal{C}_{4}
’s.
Finally, we glue the 2EC components together.

A crucial innovation over
[
Bos+25
]
is that
we do not distribute credits uniformly among the edges of
H_{0}
; otherwise we could not give a credit of
1
to each
\mathcal{C}_{4}
, which is important for later arguments.
Instead, in
FEW
we can afford to give each
\mathcal{C}_{4}
a credit of 1
initially: we give each edge
e
in
H_{0}
a credit of
\nicefrac{{1}}{{4}}-\delta
if
e
is not in a
\mathcal{C}_{4}
of
H_{0}
, and a credit of
\nicefrac{{1}}{{4}}
if
e
is in a
\mathcal{C}_{4}
of
H_{0}
, where
\delta=\nicefrac{{1}}{{100}}
. Hence, the total credit that we initially distribute to
H_{0}
is
\left(\nicefrac{{1}}{{4}}-\delta\right)|H_{0}|+\delta\cdot\beta|H_{0}|=\left(\nicefrac{{1}}{{4}}-(1-\beta)\delta\right)|H_{0}|
, as desired.

Moreover, we slightly relax the condition that the current solution
H
is a canonical
2
-edge cover by allowing some isolated vertices, which we will later give enough credits to buy edges. We call them
rich vertices
.
We call such a
2
-edge cover a
weakly canonical
2
-edge cover.

Our goal is to redistribute the initial credits on the edges according to the following credit scheme:

Definition 6

(credit scheme for
FEW
)
.

Let
H
be a weakly canonical
2
-edge cover of a structured graph
G
. We keep the following credit invariants:

•

Let
C
be a 2EC component of
H
that is a
\mathcal{C}_{4}
. Then
\mathrm{cr}(C)=1
.

•

Let
C
be a 2EC component of
H
that is a
\mathcal{C}_{i}
, for
5\leq i\leq 8
. Then
\mathrm{cr}(C)=(\nicefrac{{1}}{{4}}-\delta)i
.

•

Let
C
be a 2EC component of
H
with at least
9
edges. Then
\mathrm{cr}(C)=2
.

•

Let
C
be a complex component of
H
. Then
\mathrm{cr}(C)=1
.

•

Let
B
be a block of a complex component
C
of
H
. Then
\mathrm{cr}(B)=1
.

•

Let
e
be a bridge of a complex component
C
of
H
. Then
\mathrm{cr}(e)=\nicefrac{{1}}{{4}}-\delta
.

•

Let
v
be a rich vertex (component) of
H
. Then
\mathrm{cr}(v)=2(\nicefrac{{5}}{{4}}-\delta)
.

Ideally, we want each bridge to keep its credits, and each block and each component to get a credit of
1
.
However, for complex components, this may not be possible by only using the credits of the edges.
Hence, we take some
loans
that we will pay back later.
The key for this is that we may not need all the credits of the bridges to perform the bridge covering: we will buy edges using the credits of some bridges, while the unused credits of the remaining bridges will be used to pay back the loans. We next give more details on the bridge covering and how we repay the loans.

2.5.1
Bridge Covering

To make the loan repayments transparent, we maintain information about which bridges need to pay back which amount of loans via a color encoding:
We initially create a distinct color
\mathrm{col}(C)
for each complex component
C
, and give each bridge
e
of
C
the same color
\mathrm{col}(e)=\mathrm{col}(C)
, which is fixed and does not change. Let
\mathcal{F}
be the set of all colors.
For each color
f\in\mathcal{F}
, and for each 2-edge cover
H
that we consider in the process of bridge covering, we will maintain
\mathrm{loan}_{f}(H)
. We write
\mathrm{loan}(H)=\sum_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\mathrm{loan}_{f}(H)
.
Now
\mathrm{cr}(H)
has
2
parts:
\mathrm{loan}(H)
credits that are borrowed and
\mathrm{cr}(H)-\mathrm{loan}(H)
credits that are self-owned.
We call a color
f

deficient
for
H
if
\mathrm{loan}_{f}(H)>0
.

Throughout the bridge covering process, we maintain the following invariants.

Invariant 7

(color and loan invariant)
.

Let
H
be a weakly canonical 2-edge cover of a structured graph
G
.
We always maintain the following invariants:

(i)

For each color
f\in\mathcal{F}
, the bridges of color
f
are in the same complex component
C
of
H
.

(ii)

Let
f
be a deficient color and
F
be the set of bridges in color
f
.

–

F
induces a connected subtree
T_{C}(F)
of
T_{C}
, where
T_{C}
is obtained from
C
by contracting its blocks.

–

T_{C}(F)
has at most
4
leaves and its leaves are block nodes.

–

If
T_{C}(F)
has
4
leaves, then
\mathrm{loan}_{f}(H)\leq 20\delta
.

–

If
T_{C}(F)
has
3
leaves, then

\mathrm{loan}_{f}(H)\leq\nicefrac{{1}}{{4}}+11\delta
.

–

If
T_{C}(F)
has
2
leaves, then
\mathrm{loan}_{f}(H)\leq\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}+10\delta
. Additionally, if
\mathrm{loan}_{f}(H)>2(\nicefrac{{1}}{{4}}-\delta)
, i.e.,
\mathrm{loan}_{f}(H)
is larger than the credits of
2
bridges, then the 2 leaf blocks are nice
\mathcal{C}_{5}
’s.

(iii)

For each color
f
,
\mathrm{loan}_{f}(H)
is at most the total credit of bridges of
H
of color
f
.

One can check that
H_{0}
does not satisfy
7
(iii) if there is a complex component
C
consisting of exactly two blocks (which are nice
\mathcal{C}_{5}
’s) and exactly two bridges.
For this case, to satisfy
7
(iii), we slightly adapt
H_{0}
as follows.
Whenever there is such a complex component, we remove the two bridges and treat their intersection vertex as a component; we will call it a
rich
vertex.
Since we remove two edges from
H_{0}
and each bridge had a credit of
\nicefrac{{1}}{{4}}-\delta
, we give each rich vertex a credit of
2(1+\nicefrac{{1}}{{4}}-\delta)
.
For ease of presentation, we still call the resulting solution
H_{0}
, which is now a weakly canonical
2
-edge cover without the specific type of components as mentioned above.
All isolated vertices of
H_{0}
are obtained by this operation.

Lemma 8
.

There are valid colors
\mathcal{F}
and loans for
H_{0}
such that
7
is satisfied and

\mathrm{cost}(H_{0})-\mathrm{loan}(H_{0})\leq(\nicefrac{{5}}{{4}}-(1-\beta)\delta)|H_{0}|
.

The above invariants imply that for each color
f
, the credits of at most
3
bridges are enough to pay back the loans.
Hence, we can perform the bridge-covering process similarly as in
[
Bos+25
]
when there are enough bridges in color
f
.
When the number of bridges in color
f
is small, we carefully cover the remaining bridges without too much cost so that we can pay back the loans using the credits of some bridges before all bridges in color
f
are covered.

We will apply the following lemma to iteratively adapt
H_{0}
into a canonical 2-edge cover without bridges while maintaining the above invariants.
In each iteration, we add several paths to reduce the number of bridges, while not increasing the real cost of the current solution, i.e.,
\mathrm{cost}-\mathrm{loan}
.

Lemma 9
.

Given a weakly canonical 2-edge cover
H
of a structured graph
G
and a complex component, there is a polynomial-time algorithm that outputs a weakly canonical 2-edge cover
H^{\prime}
and a valid loan
\mathrm{loan}(H^{\prime})
of
G
with fewer bridges such that

\mathrm{cost}(H^{\prime})-\mathrm{loan}(H^{\prime})\leq\mathrm{cost}(H)-\mathrm{loan}(H)
.

By exhaustively applying the above lemma, we obtain a bridgeless weakly canonical 2-edge cover
H^{\prime}
of
G
such that

\mathrm{cost}(H^{\prime})-\mathrm{loan}(H^{\prime})\leq\mathrm{cost}(H_{0})-\mathrm{loan}(H_{0})\leq(\nicefrac{{5}}{{4}}-(1-\beta)\delta)|H_{0}|
.
Since
H^{\prime}
is bridgeless, we can conclude that
\mathrm{loan}(H^{\prime})=0
by
7
(iii).
Thus,
|H^{\prime}|\leq\mathrm{cost}(H^{\prime})=\mathrm{cost}(H^{\prime})-\mathrm{loan}(H^{\prime})\leq(\nicefrac{{5}}{{4}}-(1-\beta)\delta)|H_{0}|
as desired.

To get an intuition for
Lemma

9
, consider a complex component
C
of
H
. We denote by
G_{C}
the multi-graph obtained from
G
by contracting each block of
C
and each connected component
C^{\prime}\neq C
of
H
into a single node.
Let
T_{C}
be the tree in
G_{C}
induced by the bridges of
C
. We call the vertices of
T_{C}
corresponding
to blocks
block nodes
, and the remaining vertices of
T_{C}

lonely nodes
.
Observe that the leaves of
T_{C}
are block nodes. As for the component graph, we call vertices of
G_{C}
that arise from components as
nodes
and refer to vertices of
G_{C}
that correspond to vertices in
G
as vertices.

At a high level, we will transform
H
into a new solution
H^{\prime}
containing a component
C^{\prime}
that contains the vertices of
C
such that no new bridge is created and at least one bridge
e
of
C
is not a bridge of
C^{\prime}
; we say
e
is
covered
. During this process, we maintain that
H^{\prime}
is weakly canonical.

A crucial tool for proving
Lemma

9
are
bridge-covering paths
.
A bridge-covering path
P_{C}
is a path in
G_{C}\setminus E(T_{C})
with its distinct endpoints
u
and
v
in
T_{C}
, and the remaining internal vertices outside
T_{C}
.
Notice that
P_{C}
might consist of a single edge, possibly parallel to some edge in
E(T_{C})
.
Augmenting
H
along
P_{C}
means adding the edges of
P_{C}
to
H
to obtain
H^{\prime}
.
Notice that
H^{\prime}
is again a weakly canonical
2
-edge cover and has fewer bridges than
H
: all the bridges of
H
along the
u
-
v
path in
T_{C}
are no longer bridges in
H^{\prime}
as they were covered, and the bridges of
H^{\prime}
are a subset of the bridges of
H
.
Note that a bridge-covering path, if one exists, can be computed in polynomial time; specifically, one can use breadth-first search after truncating
T_{C}
.

If we are able to show that we can find a bridge-covering path such that the resulting solution satisfies
\mathrm{cost}(H^{\prime})-\mathrm{loan}(H^{\prime})\leq\mathrm{cost}(H)-\mathrm{loan}(H)
, we are done (cf.
Lemma

9
).
In particular, the inequality is satisfied if
P_{C}
involves at least
2
block nodes or
1
block node and at least
5

bridges.
During bridge-covering, we ensure that bridges only pay back the loan of their own color, even if we are currently covering bridges of a different complex component.
In this way, we can carefully argue that we satisfy
7
.

2.5.2
Gluing

After bridge covering, we have a bridgeless weakly canonical 2-edge cover
H_{1}
of a structured graph
G
with
\mathrm{cost}(H_{1})\leq\left(\nicefrac{{5}}{{4}}-(1-\beta)\delta\right)|H_{0}|
such that
H_{1}
is composed of

•

large components, each with a credit of
2
,

•

rich vertices, each with a credit of
2(\nicefrac{{5}}{{4}}-\delta)
,

•

\mathcal{C}_{4}
’s with a credit of
1
, and

•

\mathcal{C}_{i}
’s with a credit of
(\nicefrac{{1}}{{4}}-\delta)i
for
5\leq i\leq 8
.

Note that every component receives a credit of at least 1.
Our main contribution in this section is to prove the following lemma.

Lemma 10
.

Given a structured graph
G
and a bridgeless weakly canonical 2-edge cover
H_{1}
of
G
, we can transform
H_{1}
in polynomial time into a 2ECSS
H_{2}
of
G
such that
\mathrm{cost}(H_{2})\leq\mathrm{cost}(H_{1})
.

With the above lemma, we can prove
Lemma

7
.
We first compute a canonical
2
-edge cover
H_{0}
using
Lemma

6
.
We then transform this into a bridgeless weakly canonical 2-edge cover
H_{1}
such that
\mathrm{cost}(H_{1})\leq\mathrm{cost}(H_{0})-\mathrm{loan}(H_{0})
by exhaustively using
Lemma

9
.
Then, by exhaustively applying
Lemma

10
, we can transform
H_{1}
into a 2ECSS
H_{2}
such that
\mathrm{cost}(H_{2})\leq\mathrm{cost}(H_{1})
.
Since by
Lemma

8
,
\mathrm{cost}(H_{1})\leq\left(\nicefrac{{5}}{{4}}-(1-\beta)\delta\right)|H_{0}|
,
we can conclude
Lemma

7
.

Hence, the remaining section is dedicated to proving
Lemma

10
.
Recall that an inclusion-wise maximal 2-vertex-connected subgraph of
\bm{\hat{}}{G}_{H}
is a segment, and we can break

\bm{\hat{}}{G}_{H}
into segments such that two segments can potentially overlap only at a cut node. Hence, in a segment
S
with
|S|=2
, each node is either a cut node or a pendant node of
\bm{\hat{}}{G}_{H}
.
Moreover, a cut node of
\bm{\hat{}}{G}_{H}
cannot correspond to a rich vertex, because otherwise there is a cut vertex in
G
, contradicting that it is structured.

We roughly follow the approach of
[
Bos+25
]
.
Consider a segment
S
that contains a node
L
corresponding to a huge component. By the definition of a canonical 2-edge cover (
Definition

3
), such a segment must exist. The benefit of having a huge component is that there exists a matching of size
4
between
L
and the other vertices if those are at least 11 (
Lemma

5
).

Our approach is the following:
If we can compute a cycle
F
in
S
through
L
and another node
A
, such that
F
is incident to two distinct vertices
u
and
v
in
C_{A}
, and there is a Hamiltonian path
P
between
u
and
v
in
G[V(C_{A})]
,
then we set
H^{\prime}\coloneq(H\setminus E(C_{A}))\cup F\cup P
. Note that
H^{\prime}
is a canonical bridgeless 2-edge cover of
G
with fewer components than
H
. Furthermore,
\mathrm{cost}(H^{\prime})-\mathrm{cost}(H)=(|H^{\prime}|-|H|)+(\mathrm{cr}(H^{\prime})-\mathrm{cr}(H))=(|F|-1)+(2-|F|-1)\leq 0
, since we added
|F|
edges, effectively removed
1
edge as
|C_{A}|-|P|=1
,
\mathrm{cr}(C_{L})=2
, and
\mathrm{cr}(C_{A})\geq 1
in
H
, whereas in
H^{\prime}
those form a single component with credit 2.
We do not need the existence of such a Hamiltonian path in
C_{A}
if
A
is large, as this component already has a credit of
2
. Hence, such a cycle also meets our goal.
We call such cycles
good
cycles.

In most cases, we can simply find such a good cycle as above. However, this is not always possible.
We sometimes need to find two particular cycles in
\bm{\hat{}}{G}_{H}
that intersect at a single node.
In the following, we distinguish whether
|S|=2
or
|S|\geq 3
.

Lemma 11
.

If
|V(S)|=2
, then in polynomial time we can compute a weakly canonical bridgeless 2-edge cover
H^{\prime}
of
G
such that
H^{\prime}
has fewer components than
H
,
H^{\prime}
contains a huge component, and
\mathrm{cost}(H^{\prime})\leq\mathrm{cost}(H)
.

In this case,
S
contains two components: a huge component
L
and another component
C
.
We can find a good cycle between
L
and
C
if
C
is either large or if
C
is a
\mathcal{C}_{i}
for
4\leq i\leq 7
and there is a
3
-matching or
4
-matching between
L
and
C
, which already deals with most cases.
In the remaining cases and in particular if
C
is a
\mathcal{C}_{8}
, we show that we can find two cycles in
\bm{\hat{}}{G}_{H}
that intersect at
C
such that adding this cycle to
H
and removing a specific set of edges from
H
results in a canonical
2
-edge cover
H^{\prime}
such that
\mathrm{cost}(H^{\prime})\leq\mathrm{cost}(H)
and
H^{\prime}
has fewer components than
H
.

In the remaining case, we have
|S|\geq 3
.

Lemma 12
.

If
|V(S)|\geq 3
, then in polynomial time we can compute a weakly canonical bridgeless 2-edge cover
H^{\prime}
of
H
such that
H^{\prime}
has fewer components than
H
,
H^{\prime}
contains a huge component, and
\mathrm{cost}(H^{\prime})\leq\mathrm{cost}(H)
.

Here, we first use a lemma from
[
Bos+25
]
, which essentially says that if
S
contains the huge component
L
and another component
C
that is a
\mathcal{C}_{4}
, then we can find the desired
H^{\prime}
.
If there is no
\mathcal{C}_{4}
, any component in
S
has credit of at least
5(\nicefrac{{1}}{{4}}-\delta)\geq\nicefrac{{6}}{{5}}
.
Now, if there is a cycle
K
in
S
through
L
of length at least 6, we have that
H^{\prime}=H\cup K
is a canonical
2
-edge cover
H^{\prime}
,
H^{\prime}
has fewer components than
H
, and

|H^{\prime}|\leq|H|+|K|
and
\mathrm{cr}(H^{\prime})\leq\mathrm{cr}(H)+2-2-(|K|-1)\cdot\nicefrac{{6}}{{5}}\leq\mathrm{cr}(H)+|K|
for
|K|\geq 6
.
Otherwise, we make a case distinction on the longest length of a cycle in
S
through
L
, which can only be of length 3, 4, or 5. We show that in either case we can find a cycle through
L
such that we can find the desired
H^{\prime}
.
This finishes the gluing part and the algorithm for case
FEW
.

2.6
Algorithm for
MANY

Next, we present our algorithm for
MANY
, i.e.,
\beta>1-\frac{1}{1000}
.
We show the following lemma and defer proofs and further details to
Section

4
.

Lemma 13
.

Given a structured graph
G
and a canonical
2
-edge cover
H_{0}
, in polynomial time we can compute a 2EC spanning subgraph
H
of
G
such that

|H|\leq\left(\frac{5}{4}-\frac{1}{28}\right)\cdot\beta|H_{0}|+5(1-\beta)|H_{0}|+\frac{1}{35}\mathsf{opt}\ ,

where
\beta\in[0,1]
such that the number of edges of
\mathcal{C}_{4}
’s in
H_{0}
is
\beta|H_{0}|
.

To prove this lemma, we use the following credit scheme.

Definition 8

(credit assignment for the case of many
\mathcal{C}_{4}
’s)
.

Let
H
be a
2
-edge cover of
G
and let
\delta\coloneq\nicefrac{{1}}{{28}}
. We keep the following credit invariant for blocks, components, and bridges:

•

Each
\mathcal{C}_{4}
receives a credit of
\mathrm{cr}(C)=4\cdot(\nicefrac{{1}}{{4}}-\delta)
.

•

Each 2EC component
C
of
H
that is a
\mathcal{C}_{i}
, for
5\leq i\leq 8
, receives credit
\mathrm{cr}(C)=4i
.

•

Each 2EC component
C
of
H
that contains 9 or more edges receives credit
\mathrm{cr}(C)=2
.

•

Each block
B
of a complex component
C
of
H
receives a credit
\mathrm{cr}(B)=1
.

•

Each bridge
e
of a complex component
C
of
H
receives credit
\mathrm{cr}(e)=4
.

•

Each complex component
C
of
H
receives a component credit
\mathrm{cr}(C)=1
.

In a nutshell, the credit for edges not contained in
\mathcal{C}_{4}
’s is very large so that any
short
cycle covering a bridge or involving a
\mathcal{C}_{i}
for some
5\leq i\leq 8
can be used to transform
H
into a canonical
2
-edge cover
H^{\prime}
with fewer components or bridges such that
\mathrm{cost}(H^{\prime})\leq\mathrm{cost}(H)
. This enables us to mainly focus on components that are
\mathcal{C}_{4}
, large, or complex.

Using this credit scheme, it is easy to show that the initial canonical
2
-edge cover satisfies the invariant of the cost of the partial solution that we maintain throughout our algorithm.

Lemma 14
.

Given a canonical
2
-edge cover
H
, we have
\mathrm{cost}(H)\leq(\nicefrac{{5}}{{4}}-\delta)\cdot m_{4}+5\cdot m_{r}
, where
m_{4}
and
m_{r}
are the number of edges of
H
contained in a
\mathcal{C}_{4}
or not contained in a
\mathcal{C}_{4}
, respectively.

Our approach for
MANY
is different from the previous approach for
FEW
. Instead of first covering all bridges and afterwards gluing the different 2EC components together, we do both at the same time.
Therefore, throughout most of the algorithm, we also have complex components.

Unfortunately, we cannot turn the initial 2-edge cover
H_{0}
into a 2ECSS
H
such that
|H|\leq\left(\nicefrac{{5}}{{4}}-\nicefrac{{1}}{{28}}\right)\cdot\beta|H_{0}|+5(1-\beta)|H_{0}|
, i.e., we never increase the cost of the solution and only bound
H
in terms of
|H_{0}|
.
The reason for this is the following:
Consider the case that
H_{0}
has one large (but constant size) component
L
and a large number
k
of
\mathcal{C}_{4}
’s, such that each
\mathcal{C}_{4}
is only adjacent to
L
.
Then any feasible solution must include at least
5k\approx\nicefrac{{5}}{{4}}|H_{0}|
edges to be connected; hence
\mathsf{opt}\geq 5k
and therefore we cannot find a solution
H
such that
|H|\leq\nicefrac{{5}}{{4}}\cdot c\cdot|H_{0}|
, for some
c<1
.
However, we can find the following
core-square configuration
.
Then, we use a generalized version of the above improved bound on
\mathsf{opt}
to obtain our final approximation guarantee of
|H|\leq\left(\nicefrac{{5}}{{4}}-\nicefrac{{1}}{{28}}\right)\cdot\beta|H_{0}|+5(1-\beta)|H_{0}|+\nicefrac{{1}}{{35}}\cdot\mathsf{opt}
.

Definition 9
.

A canonical
2
-edge cover
H
is in a
core-square configuration
if

(i)

there is exactly one component
L
in
H
with
|V(L)|\geq 32
that is either complex or
2
EC,

(ii)

every component of
H
except
L
is a
\mathcal{C}_{4}
,

(iii)

L
is only contained in segments of
\bm{\hat{}}{G}_{H}
of size at most
2
, and

(iv)

if
C\neq L
is a cut component of
H
, then it is in exactly
2
segments of
\bm{\hat{}}{G}_{H}
and one of the segments contains
L
.
Let
S
and
S^{\prime}
be the
2
segments of
\bm{\hat{}}{G}_{H}
containing
C

and
L\in S
.
Then
S^{\prime}
contains at most
4
components and
C
is the only cut component in
S^{\prime}
.

Finally, we say that
H
is a
bridgeless
core-square configuration if
L
is
2
-edge connected.

(a)

(b)

Figure 2
:
An example of a core-square configuration (
Definition

9
) in
Figure

2(a)
, and its component graph and segments in
Figure

2(b)
.

A similar notion of core configuration together with the use of the different lower bound on
\mathsf{opt}
from above has been used in
[
GGJ23
]
.
The main goal of this section is to compute a bridgeless core-square configuration, summarized in the following lemma.

Lemma 15
.

Given a canonical
2
-edge cover
H
of
G
, in polynomial time we can compute a bridgeless core-square configuration
H^{\prime}
of
G
such that
\mathrm{cost}(H^{\prime})\leq\mathrm{cost}(H)+2
.

In the remainder of this section, we outline the proof of
Lemma

15
.
Similar to before, we know that our canonical
2
-edge cover
H
contains a huge component
L
.
We consider different cases on the sizes of segments
S
of the component graph
\bm{\hat{}}{G}_{H}
and the position of
L
within
\bm{\hat{}}{G}_{H}
.
In a nutshell, in most cases we can argue that the size of the current segment
S
is either at most
4
, there is a segment containing either a short cycle involving a bridge of a complex component or a
\mathcal{C}_{i}
for some
5\leq i\leq 8
, or we can conclude that there is a large
\mathcal{C}_{i}
cut, which is a contradiction to
G
being structured.
If we only have such cases, it is not too difficult to show that we can find
H^{\prime}
as required in
Lemma

15
.

The difficult case is if
L
is contained in a segment of size at least
5
, which is our main technical contribution and summarized in the following lemma.

Lemma 16
.

Given a canonical
2
-edge cover
H
of a structured graph
G
such that the component graph
\bm{\hat{}}{G}_{H}
contains a segment
S
with at least
5
components where at least one is huge, then we can compute in polynomial time a canonical
2
-edge cover
H^{\prime}
with fewer components than
H
and
\mathrm{cost}(H^{\prime})\leq\mathrm{cost}(H)
.

To prove
Lemma

16
, we use the definition of a
gluing path
. A similar concept in a weaker form has been used in
[
GGJ23
]
. In particular, unlike in
[
GGJ23
]
, we will later consider so-called
branching
gluing paths.

Definition 10
.

Given a canonical
2
-edge cover
H
of a structured graph
G
and a segment
S
, a
gluing path
is a simple path
P
in the component graph
\bm{\hat{}}{G}_{H}
satisfying the following conditions:

(i)

Every node of
P
is a node of
S
.

(ii)

If
e_{1}
and
e_{2}
are two edges of
P
incident to a component
C
corresponding to a
\mathcal{C}_{4}
, then the endpoints of these edges in
V(C)
are adjacent in
C
(w.r.t. the edges in
H
).

(iii)

If
e_{1}
and
e_{2}
are two edges of
P
incident to a complex component
C
, and both endpoints
u_{1}
of
e_{1}
and
u_{2}
of
e_{2}
in
V(C)
are not contained in some block of
C
, then
u_{1}\neq u_{2}
.

To illustrate the benefit of a gluing path, consider one of length
k\geq 4
.
If there is some edge
e
from a node
v
of
P
to some node
u
of
P
such that their distance on
P
is at least
4
, then consider the cycle
C
formed by the subpath of
P
from
u
to
v
and the edge
e
.
Clearly, adding the edges of
C
to
H
turns the
|C|
components of
C
into a single 2EC component
C^{\prime}
. Let
H^{\prime}
be the new
2
-edge cover.
Furthermore, due to the definition of a gluing path, each component of
C
that is a
\mathcal{C}_{4}
except
C_{u}
and
C_{v}
can be shortcut. That is, we can remove one edge for each such component such that
C^{\prime}
remains
2
-edge connected.
These edges pay for most of the cost of adding
|C|
.
Therefore, it can be easily checked that
H^{\prime}
is also a canonical
2
-edge cover such that
\mathrm{cost}(H^{\prime})\leq\mathrm{cost}(H)
.

Of course, such edges may not always exist.
Hence, our goal is the following:
Given some canonical
2
-edge cover and some gluing path
P
of length
k
, either we want to make progress by turning
H
into some canonical
2
-edge cover
H^{\prime}
such that
H^{\prime}
contains fewer components than
H
and
\mathrm{cost}(H^{\prime})\leq\mathrm{cost}(H)
, or we can extend
P
to a longer gluing path
P^{\prime}
. Since the length of a gluing path can be easily bounded by
|V(G)|
, after polynomially many extension we must find the desired
H^{\prime}
.
We then show that we can always make progress, which proves
Lemma

15
.

An important innovation over
[
GGJ23
]
are branches. Informally, a gluing path
P
has a branch
P^{\prime}
if
P
and
P^{\prime}
are identical for a large prefix and then “branch” out. By distinguishing structural properties of branches, we can prove the existence of more involved cycles
C
with which we can make progress; sometimes we even add two intersecting cycles. An example is illustrated in
Figure

3
.

Figure 3
:
An example of a gluing path
P=z_{5}-\ldots-z_{1a}
and a branch
P^{\prime}=z_{5}-\ldots-z_{1b}
. We show the existence of the orange edges. Then we can add the two orange edges and the four blue path edges, and remove the four red edges to make progress.

2.7
Conclusion

We give a polynomial-time
(\nicefrac{{5}}{{4}}-\eta)
-approximation algorithm for 2ECSS
for some
\eta\geq 10^{-6}
, breaching the natural barrier of
\nicefrac{{5}}{{4}}
that has been achieved very recently
[
Bos+25
,
BGJ24
,
GHL24
]
.
Our main contribution is the introduction and novel combination of techniques that allow
approximation factors below
\nicefrac{{5}}{{4}}
.
We focused on a clean and qualitative exposition, and did not optimize the approximation ratio much; we
believe that
\eta\geq 10^{-4}
should be easily possible by using more fine-grained constants.
Furthermore, we show a more substantial improvement to
1.24+\varepsilon
for every
\varepsilon>0
conditioned on the existence of a polynomial-time algorithm
that computes a minimum
\{3,4\}
-cycle-free
2
-edge cover on structured graphs.
The complexity status of this problem is open. In the light of our result, finding an optimal polynomial-time algorithm (or a good approximation algorithm) for this problem seems to be an interesting open question with strong implications.
Other future directions that build up on our work are better approximation algorithms for (special cases of) Forest Augmentation and graphic TSP.
Since current lower bounds on the approximation ratio are close to 1, one can only achieve tight or almost tight approximation ratios for 2ECSS if we improve upon these lower bounds. This is another promising direction for future work.

3
Algorithm for
FEW

This section is dedicated to the proof of
Lemma

7
, which we restate for convenience.

See
7

To prove the lemma, we first transform the initial canonical
2
-edge cover
H_{0}
into a bridgeless canonical
2
-edge cover. This step is done in
Section

3.1
.
Afterwards, we glue the different 2EC components together into a single 2EC component. This step is done in
Section

3.2
.

3.1
Bridge Covering

The key ingredient for covering the bridges is finding good bridge-covering paths, which we have already introduced in
Section

2.5
. We restate the credit scheme and the loan invariant and give some more details here.

See
6

See
7

One can check that
H_{0}
does not satisfy
7
(iii) if there is a complex component
C
consisting of exactly two blocks (which are nice
\mathcal{C}_{5}
’s) and exactly two bridges.
For this case, to satisfy
7
(iii), we slightly adapt
H_{0}
as follows.
Whenever there is such a complex component, we remove the two bridges and treat their intersection vertex as a component; we will call it a
rich
vertex.
Since we remove two edges from
H_{0}
and each bridge had a credit of
\nicefrac{{1}}{{4}}-\delta
, we give each rich vertex a credit of
2(1+\nicefrac{{1}}{{4}}-\delta)
.
For ease of presentation, we still call the resulting solution
H_{0}
, which is now a weakly canonical
2
-edge cover without the specific type of components as mentioned above.

We remark that each component of the initial (canonical)
2
-edge cover
H_{0}
that is not 2EC contains at least
12
vertices, and we only possibly merge together components in further stages of the construction.
As a consequence, the new solution is also canonical.

Recall the definition of a
bridge-covering path
.
A bridge-covering path
P_{C}
is any path in
G_{C}\setminus E(T_{C})
with its distinct endpoints
u
and
v
in
T_{C}
, and the remaining internal vertices outside
T_{C}
.
Notice that
P_{C}
might consist of a single edge, possibly parallel to some edge in
E(T_{C})
.
Augmenting
H
along
P_{C}
means adding the edges of
P_{C}
to
H
to obtain a
2
-edge cover

H^{\prime}
.
Notice that
H^{\prime}
has fewer bridges than
H
: all the bridges of
H
along the
u
-
v
path in
T_{C}
are no bridges in
H^{\prime}
(they are covered), and the bridges of
H^{\prime}
are a subset of the bridges of
H
.
If we are able to show that we can find a bridge-covering path such that the resulting solution satisfies
\mathrm{cost}(H^{\prime})-\mathrm{loan}(H^{\prime})\leq\mathrm{cost}(H)-\mathrm{loan}(H)
, we are done (cf.
Lemma

9
).

Hence, it remains to analyze
\mathrm{cost}(H^{\prime})-\mathrm{loan}(H^{\prime})
.
Let
P_{C}
be a bridge-covering path of

T_{C}
from
u
to
v
. We consider the following quantities.

•

br
is the distance between
u
and
v
in
T_{C}
(i.e., the path contains
br
many bridges).

•

bl
is the number of blocks on a path in
T_{C}
between
u
and
v
(including
u
and
v
)

Then the number of edges w.r.t.
H
grows by
|E(P_{C})|
.
The number of credits w.r.t.
H
decreases by at least
(\frac{1}{4}-\delta)br+bl+|E(P_{C})|-1
since we remove
br
bridges,
bl
blocks, and
|E(P_{C})|-1
components (each one having at least one credit).
However, the number of credits also grows by 1 since we create a new block
B^{\prime}
, which needs
1
credit, or a new 2EC component
C^{\prime}
, which needs
1
additional credit w.r.t. the credit of
C
.
Altogether
\mathrm{cost}(H)-\mathrm{cost}(H^{\prime})\geq(\frac{1}{4}-\delta)br+bl-2
.
That is, we can afford to buy the extra edges on
P_{C}
using the credits if
(\frac{1}{4}-\delta)br+bl-2\geq 0
.

Definition 11

(Cheap and Expensive Bridge-Covering Paths)
.

We say that a bridge-covering path
P_{C}
is
cheap
if
(\frac{1}{4}-\delta)br+bl-2\geq 0
, and
expensive
otherwise.

In particular,
P_{C}
is cheap if it involves at least
2
block nodes or
1
block node and at least
5

bridges.
Notice that a bridge-covering path, if at least one such path exists, can be computed in polynomial time; specifically, one can use breadth-first search after truncating
T_{C}
.
Observe that in the resulting solution
H^{\prime}
,
7
(i) is maintained.
However, to maintain
7
(iii), we have to carefully use a bridge-covering path that involves only
1
block.
For example, if there are only
6
bridges in color
f
and
\mathrm{loan}_{f}(H)
is larger than the credits of one bridge, then adding such a bridge-covering path would violate
7
(iii).

Now we show if such a path exists that the resulting solution
H^{\prime}
satisfies
\mathrm{cost}(H^{\prime})\leq\mathrm{cost}(H)
,
we can adapt the loans so that
\mathrm{cost}(H^{\prime})-\mathrm{loan}(H^{\prime})\leq\mathrm{cost}(H)-\mathrm{loan}(H)
.
A bride-covering path may cover some bridge
e
in another color.
If
\mathrm{col}(e)
has positive loans, we use the credits to pay (partially) for the loans since the credits are not used for buying extra edges on the path.
In this way, both
\mathrm{cr}(H^{\prime})
and
\mathrm{loan}(H^{\prime})
decrease by
(\frac{1}{4}-\delta)
.
So the value of
\mathrm{cost}(H^{\prime})-\mathrm{loan}(H^{\prime})
is unchanged,
and
7
(iii) is maintained.
Another case is that there exist
br^{\prime}<br
that
(\frac{1}{4}-\delta)br^{\prime}+bl-2\geq 0
, i.e., we do not need all the credits from the covered bridges to afford the extra bought edges.
In this case, we let
\mathrm{loan}(H^{\prime})\coloneq\max\{\mathrm{loan}(H)-(br-br^{\prime})(\frac{1}{4}-\delta),0\}
, and we have

\displaystyle(\mathrm{cost}(H)-\mathrm{cost}(H^{\prime}))-(\mathrm{loan}(H)-\mathrm{loan}(H^{\prime}))

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}{4}-\delta\right)br+bl-2-\left(\frac{1}{4}-\delta\right)(br-br^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}{4}-\delta\right)br^{\prime}+bl-2\geq 0\ ,

which implies
\mathrm{cost}(H^{\prime})-\mathrm{loan}(H^{\prime})\leq\mathrm{cost}(H)-\mathrm{loan}(H)
.
Further, if
bl=2
, then
br^{\prime}=0
and
7
(iii) is maintained by the following arguments since the credits and the loans decrease by the same amount.
If
bl=1
and
br\geq 5
(
br^{\prime}=5
), our algorithm will ensure to add such a bridge-covering path only if
(k-5)(\frac{1}{4}-\delta)\geq\mathrm{loan}_{f}(H)
, where
k
is the number of bridges in color
f
in
H
.
In the resulting solution
H^{\prime}
, we have
k-br
bridges in color
f
.
So
\mathrm{loan}_{f}(H^{\prime})=\mathrm{loan}_{f}(H)-(br-5)(\frac{1}{4}-\delta)\leq(k-5)(\frac{1}{4}-\delta)-(br-5)(\frac{1}{4}-\delta)\leq(k-br)(\frac{1}{4}-\delta)
, which satisfies
7
(iii).

To argue
7
(ii), we fix any deficient color
f
in a component
C
of
H
and let
F
(resp.
F^{\prime}
) be the set of edges in color
f
in
H
(resp.
H^{\prime}
).
Notice that
T_{C}^{\prime}(F^{\prime})
can be obtained from
T_{C}(F)
by contracting a connected subtree
T^{\prime}\subseteq T_{C}(F)
.
Hence,
T_{C}^{\prime}(F^{\prime})
has fewer or equal number of leaves than
T_{C}(F)
.
Since
\mathrm{loan}_{f}(H^{\prime})\leq\mathrm{loan}_{f}(H)
, the conditions in
7
(ii) are satisfied.

Main lemmas.

Let
H_{0}
be a canonical 2-edge cover of
G
(
Definition

3
).
We show that we can assign credits to elements of
H_{0}
as in
Definition

6
.

See
8

Proof.

Consider the following process.
Initially, we give
\nicefrac{{1}}{{4}}-\delta
credits to each edge that is not in a
\mathcal{C}_{4}
of
H_{0}
, and give
\nicefrac{{1}}{{4}}
credits to each edge in
\mathcal{C}_{4}
s.
Hence we initially give
\left(\frac{1}{4}-(1-\beta)\delta\right)|H_{0}|
credits in total.
We then redistribute the credits over other elements (components, blocks, bridges) of
H_{0}
to satisfy the credit scheme in
Definition

6
.
Each edge that is in a 2EC component gives its credits to the component it is in, which satisfies the credit scheme for non-complex components.
Consider a complex component
C
of
H_{0}
.
Each bridge keeps its credits.
We perform a case-distinction on the number of blocks in
C
and show how to redistribute the credits of non-bridge edges to components and blocks, and how to take loans.

C
has at least
5
blocks.

Each block has at least
5
edges and hence at least
5(\frac{1}{4}-\delta)
credits in total.
We use the credits of the edges in the blocks to pay for the block credits.
Then for each block, we still have
(\frac{1}{4}-5\delta)
unused credits.
In total, we have at least
\frac{5}{4}-25\delta\geq 1
unused credits (using
\delta\leq\frac{1}{100}
) over all the blocks.
We use the unused credits to pay for the component credit.

C
has exactly
4
blocks.

Let the total number of edges in these blocks be
k
.
Hence we have
k(\frac{1}{4}-\delta)
credits from these edges.
Note that
k\geq 20
since each block has at least
5
edges.
We need
4
credits for blocks and
1
credit for the component.
If
k\geq 21
, then
k(\frac{1}{4}-\delta)\geq\frac{21}{4}-21\delta\geq 5
(using
\delta\leq\frac{1}{84}
), we are done.
If
k=20
, we have
20(\frac{1}{4}-\delta)=5-20\delta
credits.
But we need
5
credits to pay for the
4
blocks and
1
credit for the component.
Hence we have to take a loan of
20\delta
credits.
Note that the credits from one bridge are enough to pay back the loan.
We will create a new color
f
, mark all the bridges in this component with color
f
, and set
\mathrm{loan}_{f}(H_{0})\coloneq 20\delta
.

C
has exactly
3
blocks.

Let the total number of edges in these blocks be
k
.
Hence we have
k(\frac{1}{4}-\delta)
credits from these edges.
Note that
k\geq 15
since each block has at least
5
edges.
We need
3
credits for blocks and
1
credit for the component.
If
k\geq 17
, then
k(\frac{1}{4}-\delta)\geq\frac{17}{4}-17\delta\geq 4
(using
\delta\leq\frac{1}{68}
), we are done.
If
k=16
, we have
16(\frac{1}{4}-\delta)=4-16\delta
credits.
But we need
4
credits to pay for the
3
blocks and
1
credit for the component.
Hence we have to take a loan of
16\delta
credits.
If
k=15
, we have
15(\frac{1}{4}-\delta)=\frac{15}{4}-15\delta
credits.
Hence we have to take a loan of
\frac{1}{4}+15\delta
credits.
Note that the credits from
2
bridges are enough to pay back the loan.
We will create a new color
f
, mark all the bridges in this component with color
f
, and set
\mathrm{loan}_{f}(H_{0})=16\delta
if
k=16
and
\mathrm{loan}_{f}(H_{0})=\frac{1}{4}+15\delta
if
k=15
.

C
has exactly
2
blocks.

Let the total number of edges in these blocks be
k
.
Hence we have
k(\frac{1}{4}-\delta)
credits from these edges.
Note that
k\geq 10
since each block has at least
5
edges.
We need
2
credits for blocks and
1
credit for the component.
If
k\geq 13
, then
k(\frac{1}{4}-\delta)\geq\frac{13}{4}-13\delta\geq 3
(using
\delta\leq\frac{1}{52}
), we are done.
If
10\leq k\leq 12
,
we have to take a loan of
3-k(\frac{1}{4}-\delta)=\frac{12-k}{4}+k\delta\leq\frac{1}{2}+10\delta\leq 3(\frac{1}{4}-\delta)
credits.
Note that the credits from at most
3
bridges are enough to pay back the loan.
We will create a new color
f
, mark all the bridges in this component with color
f
, and set
\mathrm{loan}_{f}(H_{0})\coloneq\frac{1}{2}+10\delta
.
Note that we need to satisfy the loan invariant that the total credits of bridges in color
f
must be at least
\mathrm{loan}_{f}(H_{0})
.
This is violated if and only if if
C
has exactly
2
blocks with both of them being
\mathcal{C}_{5}
, and has at most
2
bridges.
If this is the case, recall that we treat
C
differently.
Note that
C
has exactly
2
edges as otherwise we can remove the only bridge to obtain a triangle-free
2
-edge cover with fewer edges.
We remove the two bridges and treat their intersection vertex as a component.
We call such a vertex a
rich
vertex or component.
Further, we give the credits of the two edges to the rich vertex so that it has
2(\frac{5}{4}-\delta)
credits.

By the previous discussion, for each color
f
,
\mathrm{loan}_{f}(H_{0})\leq\frac{1}{2}+10\delta
and the credits of at most
3
bridges are enough to pay back the loans.
Further, if the credits on
2
bridges are not enough, it must be the case where the corresponding complex component in
H_{0}
has exactly two blocks and each of them are
\mathcal{C}_{5}
.
Otherwise, we have
\mathrm{loan}_{f}(H_{0})\leq\frac{1}{4}+11\delta
.
∎

We use the notations defined in
Section

2.5
.
We say that a vertex
v\in V(T_{C})\setminus\{u\}
is
reachable
from
u\in V(T_{C})
if there exists a bridge-covering path between
v
and
u
.
Let
R(W)
be the vertices in
V(T_{C})\setminus W
reachable from some vertex in
W\subseteq V(T_{C})
, and let us use
R(u)\coloneq R(\{u\})
for
u\in V(T_{C})
.
Notice that
v\in R(u)
if and only if
u\in R(v)
.
We first show the following useful lemma.

Lemma 17

(Lemma 29 in the full version of
[
Bos+25
]
)
.

Let
e=xy\in E(T_{C})
and let
X_{C}
and
Y_{C}
be the two sets of vertices of the two trees obtained from
T_{C}
after removing the edge
e
, where
x\in X_{C}
and
y\in Y_{C}
.
Then
R(X_{C})
contains a block node or
R(X_{C})\setminus\{y\}
contains at least 2 lonely nodes.

Let
T_{C}(u,v)
denote the path in
T_{C}
between vertices
u
and
v
.
The following lemmas show that we can make progress with
2
bridge-covering paths.

Lemma 18
.

Let
b
and
b^{\prime}
be two pendant (block) nodes of
T_{C}
.
Let
u\in R(b)
and
u^{\prime}\in R(b^{\prime})
be vertices of
V(T_{C})\setminus\{b,b^{\prime}\}
.
Suppose that
T_{C}(b,u)
and
T_{C}(b^{\prime},u^{\prime})
both contain some vertex
w
(possibly
w=u=u^{\prime}
) and
|E(T_{C}(b,u))\cup E(T_{C}(b^{\prime},u^{\prime}))|\geq 5
.
Assume that the bridges in
E(T_{C}(b,u))\cup E(T_{C}(b^{\prime},u^{\prime}))
are in the same color
f
.
Then in polynomial time one can find a 2-edge cover
H^{\prime}
satisfying the conditions of
Lemma

9
.

Proof.

Let
P_{bu}
(resp.
P_{b^{\prime}u^{\prime}}
) be the bridge-covering paths between
b
and
u
(resp.
b^{\prime}
and
u^{\prime}
).
If the two paths intersect internally, then there exists a bridge-covering path
P
between
b
and
b^{\prime}
, which is cheap.
Hence
H^{\prime}\coloneq H\cup P
satisfies the conditions of
Lemma

9
.
Otherwise, let
H^{\prime}\coloneq H\cup P_{bu}\cup P_{b^{\prime}u^{\prime}}
.
Note that these two bridge-covering paths together cover
k\geq 5
bridges and merge at least two blocks.
Hence, we have
|H^{\prime}|=|H|+|P_{bu}|+|P_{b^{\prime}u^{\prime}}|
and
\mathrm{cr}(H^{\prime})=\mathrm{cr}(H)+1-(|P_{bu}|-1)-(|P_{b^{\prime}u^{\prime}}|-1)-2-k(\frac{1}{4}-\delta)\leq\mathrm{cr}(H)-|P_{bu}|-|P_{b^{\prime}u^{\prime}}|-(k-5)(\frac{1}{4}-\delta)
.
So
\mathrm{cost}(H^{\prime})\leq\mathrm{cost}(H)-(k-5)(\frac{1}{4}-\delta)
, and we can let
\mathrm{loan}_{f}(H^{\prime})\coloneq\max\{\mathrm{loan}_{f}(H)-(k-5)(\frac{1}{4}-\delta),0\}
since these
(k-5)(\frac{1}{4}-\delta)
credits are not needed for buying the bridge-covering paths.
This proves the lemma.
∎

Lemma 19
.

Let
b
and
b^{\prime}
be two pendant (block) nodes of
T_{C}
.
Let
u\in R(b)
and
u^{\prime}\in R(b^{\prime})
be vertices of
V(T_{C})\setminus\{b,b^{\prime}\}
.
Suppose that
T_{C}(b,u)
and
T_{C}(b^{\prime},u^{\prime})
intersect on only one edge
e
, that is,
E(T_{C}(b,u))\cap E(T_{C}(b^{\prime},u^{\prime}))=\{e\}
.
Then in polynomial time one can find a 2-edge cover
H^{\prime}
satisfying the conditions of
Lemma

9
.

Proof.

Let
P_{bu}
(resp.
P_{b^{\prime}u^{\prime}}
) be the bridge-covering paths between
b
and
u
(resp.
b^{\prime}
and
u^{\prime}
).
If the two paths intersect internally, then there exists a bridge-covering path
P
between
b
and
b^{\prime}
, which is cheap.
Hence
H^{\prime}\coloneq H\cup P
satisfies the conditions of
Lemma

9
.
Otherwise, let
H^{\prime}\coloneq(H\setminus\{e\})\cup P_{bu}\cup P_{b^{\prime}u^{\prime}}
.
Note that these two bridge-covering paths merge at least two blocks, and
k\geq 1
bridges are covered or removed.
We have
|H^{\prime}|=|H|+|P_{bu}|+|P_{b^{\prime}u^{\prime}}|-1
and
\mathrm{cr}(H^{\prime})=\mathrm{cr}(H)+1-(|P_{bu}|-1)-(|P_{b^{\prime}u^{\prime}}|-1)-2-k(\frac{1}{4}-\delta)=\mathrm{cr}(H)-|P_{bu}|-|P_{b^{\prime}u^{\prime}}|+1-k(\frac{1}{4}-\delta)
.
So
\mathrm{cost}(H^{\prime})\leq\mathrm{cost}(H)-k(\frac{1}{4}-\delta)
, and we can let
\mathrm{loan}_{f}(H^{\prime})\coloneq\max\{\mathrm{loan}_{f}(H)-k(\frac{1}{4}-\delta),0\}
since these
k(\frac{1}{4}-\delta)
credits are not needed for buying the bridge-covering paths.
This proves the lemma.
∎

Lemma 20
.

Let
b
be a pendant (block) node of
T_{C}
and
b^{\prime}
be any other node (not necessarily a block node) of
T_{C}
.
Let
u\in R(b)
and
u^{\prime}\in R(b^{\prime})
be vertices of
V(T_{C})\setminus\{b,b^{\prime}\}
.
Suppose that
T_{C}(b,u)
and
T_{C}(b^{\prime},u^{\prime})
intersect on only one edge
e
and
|E(T_{C}(b,u))\cup E(T_{C}(b^{\prime},u^{\prime}))|\geq 5
.
Then in polynomial time one can find a 2-edge cover
H^{\prime}
satisfying the conditions of
Lemma

9
.

Proof.

Let
P_{bu}
(resp.
P_{b^{\prime}u^{\prime}}
) be the bridge-covering paths between
b
and
u
(resp.
b^{\prime}
and
u^{\prime}
).
If the two paths intersect internally, then there exists a bridge-covering path
P
between
b
and
b^{\prime}
, which is cheap.
Hence
H^{\prime}\coloneq H\cup P
satisfies the conditions of
Lemma

9
.
Otherwise, let
H^{\prime}\coloneq(H\setminus\{e\})\cup P_{bu}\cup P_{b^{\prime}u^{\prime}}
.
Note that these two bridge-covering paths merge at least two blocks, and
k\geq 5
bridges are covered or removed.
we have
|H^{\prime}|=|H|+|P_{bu}|+|P_{b^{\prime}u^{\prime}}|-1
and
\mathrm{cr}(H^{\prime})=\mathrm{cr}(H)+1-(|P_{bu}|-1)-(|P_{b^{\prime}u^{\prime}}|-1)-1-k(\frac{1}{4}-\delta)=\mathrm{cr}(H)-|P_{bu}|-|P_{b^{\prime}u^{\prime}}|+1-(k-5)(\frac{1}{4}-\delta)
.
So
\mathrm{cost}(H^{\prime})\leq\mathrm{cost}(H)-(k-5)(\frac{1}{4}-\delta)
, and we can let
\mathrm{loan}_{f}(H^{\prime})\coloneq\max\{\mathrm{loan}_{f}(H)-(k-5)(\frac{1}{4}-\delta),0\}
since these
(k-5)(\frac{1}{4}-\delta)
credits are not needed for buying the bridge-covering paths.
This proves the lemma.
∎

We will frequently use the above lemmas by passing the parameters in order of
(b,b^{\prime},u,u^{\prime})
.
The following lemma shows how to cover bridges of non-deficient colors. Note that we always maintain
7
.

Lemma 21
.

Given a canonical 2-edge cover
H
of a structured graph
G
and a complex component
C

without edges in deficient colors
, there is a polynomial-time algorithm that outputs a canonical 2-edge cover
H^{\prime}
of
G
satisfying the conditions of
Lemma

9
.

Proof.

If there exists a cheap bridge-covering path
P_{C}
(condition that we can check in
polynomial time), we simply augment
H
along
P_{C}
hence obtaining the desired
H^{\prime}
.

Thus, we next assume that no such path exists.
Let
P^{\prime}=b,u_{1},\ldots,u_{\ell}
in
T_{C}
be a longest path in
T_{C}
(interpreted as a sequence of vertices).
Notice that
b
must be a leaf of
T_{C}
, hence a block node.
Let us consider
R(b)
. Since by assumption there is no cheap bridge-covering path,
R(b)
does not contain any block node.
Hence
|R(b)\setminus\{u_{1}\}|\geq 2

by
Lemma

17
(applied to
xy=bu_{1}
).
This implies that
\ell\geq 3
.

We next distinguish a few subcases depending on
R(b)
.
Let
V_{i}
,
i\geq 1
, be the vertices in
V(T_{C})\setminus V(P^{\prime})
such that their path to
b
in
T_{C}
passes through
u_{i}
and not through
u_{i+1}
.
Notice that
\{V(P^{\prime}),V_{1},\ldots,V_{\ell}\}
is a partition of
V(T_{C})
.
We observe that any vertex in
V_{i}
is at distance at most
i
from
u_{i}
in
T_{C}
as otherwise
P^{\prime}
would not be a longest path in
T_{C}
.
We also observe that the leaves of
T_{C}
in
V_{i}
are block nodes.
This implies the following observations.

Claim 22
.

All the vertices in
V_{1}
are pendant block nodes;
all the vertices in
V_{2}
are either block nodes or are non-pendant vertices at distance
1
from
u_{2}
in
T_{C}
;
all the vertices in
V_{3}
are either block nodes or are non-pendant vertices at distance at most
2
from
u_{3}
in
T_{C}
.

Case 0:

\ell=4
.
Note that both
b
and
u_{4}
are pendant block nodes.
Further,
V_{3}
can only contain pendant block nodes as otherwise
P^{\prime}
was not a longest path.
We apply
Lemma

17
twice by setting
xy=bu_{1}
and
xy=u_{4}u_{3}
respectively.
Since
R(b)
and
R(u_{4})
contain no block node,
R(b)\setminus\{u_{1}\}
contains at least two nodes in
\{u_{2},u_{3}\}\cup V_{2}
and
R(u_{4})\setminus\{u_{3}\}
contains at least two nodes in
\{u_{1},u_{2}\}\cup V_{2}
.
If
R(b)\setminus\{u_{1}\}
contains some node
u
in
V_{2}
,
let
u^{\prime}\in R(u_{4})\setminus\{u_{3}\}
.
Then the tuple
(b,u_{4},u,u^{\prime})
satisfies the conditions of
Lemma

18
.
The case where
R(u_{4})\setminus\{u_{3}\}
contains some node in
V_{2}
is symmetric.
Hence we can assume
R(b)\setminus\{u_{1}\}=\{u_{2},u_{3}\}
and
R(u_{4})\setminus\{u_{3}\}\{u_{1},u_{2}\}
.
Then the tuple
(b,u_{4},u_{3},u_{2})
satisfies the conditions of
Lemma

19
.

For the following cases, we can assume that
\ell\geq 5
.

Case 1:
There exists
u\in R(b)
with
u\notin\{u_{1},u_{2},u_{3},u_{4}\}\cup V_{1}\cup V_{2}\cup V_{3}
. By definition there exists a bridge-covering path between
b
and
u
covering at least
5
bridges, hence cheap.
This is excluded by the assumption that there is no cheap bridge-covering path.

Case 2:
There exists
u\in R(b)
with
u\in V_{1}\cup V_{2}\cup V_{3}
.

First consider the case
u\in V_{3}
.
There must be some pendant block
b^{\prime}\in V_{3}
such that
u\in T_{C}(b^{\prime},u_{3})
.
Consider
R(b^{\prime})
and let
w
be the unique neighbor of
b^{\prime}
in
T_{C}
(possibly
w=u
).
By the assumption that there are no cheap bridge-covering paths and
Lemma

17
(applied to
xy=b^{\prime}w
),
R(b^{\prime})
contains at least one node
u^{\prime}\notin\{b^{\prime},b,w\}
. The tuple
(b,b^{\prime},u,u^{\prime})
satisfies the conditions of
Lemma

18
(specifically, both
T_{C}(b,u)
and
T_{C}(b^{\prime},u^{\prime})
contain
u
), hence we can obtain the desired
H^{\prime}
.

Now consider the case where
u\in V_{1}\cup V_{2}
.
Since
u
is not a block node,
u
must be a non-pendant vertex in
V_{2}
at distance
1
from
u_{2}
.
Furthermore,
V_{2}
must contain at least one pendant block node
b^{\prime}
adjacent to
u
.
Consider
R(b^{\prime})
.
By the assumption that there are no cheap bridge-covering paths and
Lemma

17
(applied to
xy=b^{\prime}u
),
|R(b^{\prime})\setminus\{u\}|\geq 2
.
Let
u^{\prime}\in R(b^{\prime})
.
The tuple
(b,b^{\prime},u,u^{\prime})
satisfies the conditions of
Lemma

18
(specifically, both
T_{C}(b,u)
and
T_{C}(b^{\prime},u^{\prime})
contain
u_{2}
) unless
u^{\prime}=u_{1}
or
u^{\prime}=u_{2}
(
|E(T_{C}(b,u))\cup E(T_{C}(b^{\prime},u^{\prime}))|=4<5
).
Since
|R(b^{\prime})\setminus\{u\}|\geq 2
, we can let
u^{\prime}=u_{2}
.
Then the tuple
(b,b^{\prime},u,u^{\prime})
satisfies the conditions of
Lemma

19
.

Case 3:

R(b)\setminus\{u_{1}\}\subseteq\{u_{2},u_{3},u_{4}\}
; so
|R(b)\setminus\{u_{1}\}\cap\{u_{2},u_{3},u_{4}\}|\geq 2
.
Let
j
(
3\leq j\leq 4
) be the maximum index such that
u_{j}\in R(b)
.

We first consider the case that
V_{1}\cup V_{2}\neq\emptyset
.
Take any pendant (block) node
b^{\prime}\in V_{1}\cup V_{2}
, say
b^{\prime}\in V_{i}
.
Let
\ell^{\prime}
be the vertex adjacent to
b^{\prime}
.
By the assumption that there are no cheap bridge-covering paths and
Lemma

17
(applied to
xy=b^{\prime}\ell^{\prime}
),
R(b^{\prime})\setminus\{\ell^{\prime}\}
has cardinality at least 2 and contains only lonely nodes.
Choose any
u^{\prime}\in R(b^{\prime})\setminus\{\ell^{\prime}\}
. Notice that
u^{\prime}\notin V_{1}
(but it could be a lonely node in
V_{2}
other than
\ell^{\prime}
).
Observe that the tuple
(b,b^{\prime},u_{j},u^{\prime})
satisfies the conditions of
Lemma

18

if
j=4
or
|T_{C}(b^{\prime},u^{\prime})\setminus P^{\prime}|\geq 2
.
It remains to consider the case that
j=3
and
b^{\prime}
is a pendant block node adjacent to
u_{1}
or
u_{2}
.
If
b^{\prime}\in V_{1}
, we can let
b^{\prime}\notin\{u_{1},u_{2}\}
since
|R(b)\setminus\{u_{1}\}|\geq 2
. Then the tuple
(b,b^{\prime},u_{2},u^{\prime})
satisfies the conditions of
Lemma

19
.
If
b^{\prime}\in V_{2}
, then the tuple
(b,b^{\prime},u_{3},u^{\prime})
satisfies the conditions of
Lemma

18
unless
u^{\prime}\in\{u_{1},u_{3}\}
.
If
u^{\prime}=u_{1}
or
u^{\prime}=u_{3}
, the tuple
(b,b^{\prime},u_{3},u^{\prime})
satisfies the conditions of
Lemma

19
.
In any case, we obtain the desired
H^{\prime}
.
In the following, we assume that
V_{1}\cup V_{2}=\emptyset
.

Case 3a:

R(b)\setminus\{u_{1}\}=\{u_{2},u_{3}\}
.
This implies
u_{2}
and
u_{3}
are lonely nodes as otherwise there is a cheap bridge-covering path.
By
Lemma

17
(applied to
xy=u_{2}u_{3}
) the set
R(\{b,u_{1},u_{2}\})
contains a block node or
R(\{b,u_{1},u_{2}\})\setminus\{u_{3}\}
contains at least
2
lonely nodes.
Since
R(b)\setminus\{u_{1}\}=\{u_{2},u_{3}\}
and
u_{3}
is a lonely node,
R(\{u_{1},u_{2}\})\setminus\{u_{3}\}
either contains a block node or at least
2
lonely nodes.
If
R(\{u_{1},u_{2}\})\setminus\{u_{3}\}
contains some node
w
at distance at least
2
from
u_{3}
, then the tuple
(b,u_{1},u_{2},w)
or
(b,u_{2},u_{3},w)
satisfies the conditions of
Lemma

20
depending on
w\in R(u_{1})
or
w\in R(u_{2})
.
Hence we assume that
R(\{u_{1},u_{2}\})\setminus\{u_{3}\}
only contains neighbors of
u_{3}
.

If
R(\{u_{1},u_{2}\})\setminus\{u_{3}\}
contains some block node
b^{\prime}
, then there is a bridge-covering path from
b^{\prime}
to
u_{i}
for some
i\in\{1,2\}
.
Hence we can apply
Lemma

19
using the tuple
(b,b^{\prime},u_{i+1},u_{i})
and make progress.
The remaining case is that
R(\{u_{1},u_{2}\})\setminus\{u_{3}\}
contains at least two lonely nodes among the neighbors of
u_{3}
, which implies that
V_{3}\neq\emptyset
and there is a bridge-covering path from some
u_{i}
with
i\in\{1,2\}
to some
u\in V_{3}
.
If
u
is a block node, then we can apply
Lemma

19
with
(b,u,u_{i+1},u_{i})
.

If
u
is not a block node, there must be some block node
b^{\prime}\in V_{3}
such that
u
lies on the path in
T_{C}
from
b^{\prime}
to
u_{3}
.
Then
R(b^{\prime})
must contain some lonely node
u^{\prime}\notin\{b,u\}
.
If the distance between
b^{\prime}
and
u_{3}
is
2
, then
u^{\prime}
(which is not a block node) is not in
V_{3}
.
Then the tuple
(b,b^{\prime},u,u^{\prime})
satisfies the conditions of
Lemma

19
.
If the distance between
b^{\prime}
and
u_{3}
is
3
, then either the tuple
(b,b^{\prime},u,u^{\prime})
satisfies the conditions of
Lemma

19
or
u\in T_{C}(u^{\prime},u_{3})
.
In the latter case, we can instead consider the longest path
T_{C}(b^{\prime},u_{l})
and we are in Case 2.

Case 3b:

\{u_{2},u_{4}\}\subseteq R(b)
or
\{u_{3},u_{4}\}\subseteq R(b)
.
We apply
Lemma

17
with
xy=u_{3}u_{4}
.
Let
X_{C}
be the subtree obtained from
T_{C}
by removing
u_{3}u_{4}
with
u_{3}\in X_{C}
and
u_{4}\in Y_{C}
.
Then either
R(X_{C})
contains a block node in
Y_{C}
or two lonely nodes in
Y_{C}\setminus\{u_{4}\}
.
If
R(X_{C})
contains a block node
b^{\prime}
in
Y_{C}
, it must be in
Y_{C}\setminus\{u_{4}\}
since
u_{4}\in R(b)
and
u_{4}
cannot be a block node.
Then we can apply
Lemma

18
using the tuple
(b,b^{\prime},u_{4},u)
, where
u\in X_{C}
and
b^{\prime}\in R(u)
.
In the following, we assume that
R(X_{C})
contains at least
2
lonely nodes in
Y_{C}\setminus\{u_{4}\}
.

If there is a bridge-covering path from
u_{3}\cup V_{3}
to
Y_{C}
, then we can apply
Lemma

20
to obtain the desired
H^{\prime}
.
Hence we can assume that the bridge-covering paths from
X_{C}
to
R(X_{C})\setminus\{u_{4}\}

start from either
u_{1}
or
u_{2}
.
Further, if there is no bridge-covering path from
u_{1}
(resp.
u_{2}
) to
R(X_{C})\setminus\{u_{4}\}
, then
\{u_{2},u_{4}\}
(resp.
\{u_{1},u_{4}\}
) would yield a non-isolating
2
-vertex-cut, which is impossible since our graph is structured.
