Hence, there must be some bridge-covering path from
u_{1}
to
v_{1}\in Y_{C}\setminus\{u_{4}\}
and some bridge-covering path from
u_{2}
to
v_{2}\in Y_{C}\setminus\{u_{4}\}
.
Note that we have either
u_{2}\in R(b)
or
u_{3}\in R(b)
.
If
u_{2}\in R(b)
, then the tuple
(b,u_{1},u_{2},v_{1})
satisfies the conditions of
Lemma

20
.
If
u_{3}\in R(b)
, then the tuple
(b,u_{2},u_{3},v_{2})
satisfies the conditions of
Lemma

20
.
∎

Now consider some deficient color
f
.
Let
F
be the set of bridges in color
f
.
By
7
,
F
is in the same complex component
C
of
H
and
F
induces a connected subtree of
T_{C}
, which we denote as
T_{C}(F)
.
Since we care about covering bridges in
T_{C}(F)
, if a bridge-covering path covers some bridge in
T_{C}(F)
, we can assume that both its endpoints are in
T_{C}(F)
.
If not, it covers some bridge
e^{\prime}
in color
f^{\prime}\neq f
in
T_{C}\setminus T_{C}(F)
, we use the credits on
e^{\prime}
to pay back
\mathrm{loan}_{f^{\prime}}(H)
.
This does not change the value of
\mathrm{cost}(H)-\mathrm{loan}(H)
and even reduces
\mathrm{loan}(H)
.
So in the worst case, we can assume this never happens.
By simulating the proof of
Lemma

21
, we have the following corollary.

Corollary 23
.

Let
\ell
be the smallest integer such that
\ell(\frac{1}{4}-\delta)\geq\mathrm{loan}_{f}(H)
.
If
T_{F}(C)
has at least
5+\ell
bridges, then in polynomial time one can find a 2-edge cover
H^{\prime}
satisfying the conditions of
Lemma

9
.

Proof.

In the proof of
Lemma

21
, the key is to find one or two bridge-covering paths that cover at least
5
bridges (to gain
5(\frac{1}{4}-\delta)\geq 1
credits).
We simulate the same process, and whenever we cover
k\geq 5
bridges,
we use the credits of
5
bridges to pay for the bought edges and the credits of the remaining
(k-5)
bridges to pay back the loans
\mathrm{loan}_{f}(H)
.
In this way, we cover bridges as well as maintain
7
.
∎

The following lemma implies that we can always make progress if
T_{C}(F)
has two block nodes
b
and
b^{\prime}
between which there are only lonely nodes of degree
2
.

Lemma 24
.

If there are two block nodes in
T_{C}(F)
,
b
and
b^{\prime}
, such that the path between them contains only lonely nodes of degree
2
, then there is a polynomial-time algorithm that outputs a canonical 2-edge cover
H^{\prime}
of
G
satisfying the conditions of
Lemma

9
.

Proof.

We label the nodes on
T_{C}(b,b^{\prime})
as
u_{1}=b,u_{2},\dots,u_{q}=b^{\prime}
.
Note that
u_{2},\cdots,u_{q-1}
are nodes of degree
2
.
We show that we can find bridge-covering paths to cover the bridges on the path
T_{C}(u_{1},u_{q})
.
Let
T_{u_{1}}
(resp.
T_{u_{q}}
) be the subtree of
T_{C}\setminus\{u_{1}u_{2}\}
(resp.
T_{u_{q-1}u_{q}}
) containing
u_{1}
(resp.
u_{q}
).
Since we aim to cover the bridges on the path
T_{C}(u_{1},u_{q})
, we can assume w.l.o.g. that any bridge-covering path starting from
T_{u_{1}}
(resp.
T_{u_{q}}
) starts from
u_{1}
(resp.
u_{q}
).
Further, we can consider
T_{C}^{\prime}
obtained from
T_{C}
by contracting
T_{u_{1}}
and
T_{u_{q}}

That is,
T_{C}^{\prime}
is a path whose two endpoints are block nodes and all internal nodes are lonely nodes.
We slightly abuse the notations by denoting the path
T_{C}^{\prime}
as
u_{1},u_{2},\dots,u_{q}
.

If
u_{q}\in R(u_{1})
, we can add any bridge-covering path between
u_{1}
and
u_{q}
since it covers
2
blocks and is cheap.
In the following, we assume that
u_{q}\notin R(u_{1})
and symmetrically
u_{1}\notin R(u_{q})
.
If
q\geq 9
, i.e., there are at least
8
bridges.
Then we can immediately apply
Corollary

23
with
l=3
.

Now we assume that
q\leq 8
.
We apply
Lemma

17
with
xy=u_{1}u_{2}
, and we can conclude that
|R(u_{1})\cap\{u_{3},u_{4},\dots,u_{q-1}\}|\geq 2
.
Symmetrically, we have
|R(u_{q})\cap\{u_{2},u_{3},\dots,u_{q-2}\}|\geq 2
.
If
q\leq 6
, i.e., there are at most
5
bridges, there must be some index
i
such that
u_{i}\in R(u_{q})
and
u_{i+1}\in R(u_{1})
.
Then the tuple
(u_{1},u_{q},u_{i+1},u_{i})
satisfies the conditions of
Lemma

19
and we can make progress.
Note that we use the credits on the covered bridges to pay for
\mathrm{loan}_{f}(H)
.
In the following, we assume
7\leq q\leq 8
, i.e., there are
6
or
7
bridges.

We first consider the case where
q=8
, i.e.,
T_{C}^{\prime}
is a path of
7
edges.
We will cover all these edges using up to
3
bridge-covering paths.
These
7
bridges have
7(\frac{1}{4}-\delta)
credits in total and we use them to fully pay for
\mathrm{loan}_{f}(H)\leq\frac{1}{2}+10\delta
.
After that, we have at least
7(\frac{1}{4}-\delta)-\mathrm{loan}_{f}(H)\geq\frac{5}{4}-17\delta\geq 1
unused credits,
\mathrm{loan}(H^{\prime})=0
and
\mathrm{cost}(H^{\prime})-\mathrm{loan}(H^{\prime})=\mathrm{cost}(H)-\mathrm{loan}_{f}(H)
since we decrease the credits and the loans by the same amount.
We will make use of this
1
unused credit to pay for the bridge-covering paths using the following operations.

(i)

(Only for
q=8
) Add two bridge-covering paths,
P
from
u_{1}
to some
u_{i}
and
Q
from
u_{j}
to
u_{q}
where
1<j\leq i<q
.
In this way, we obtain
H^{\prime}
with
|H^{\prime}|=|H|+|P|+|Q|
, and
\mathrm{cr}(H^{\prime})\leq\mathrm{cr}(H)+1-(|P|-1+|Q|-1+2+1)=\mathrm{cr}(H)-|P|-|Q|
.
The last
+1
is the unused credit.
Hence
\mathrm{cost}(H^{\prime})\leq\mathrm{cost}(H)
and we are done.

(ii)

(Only for
q=8
) Add three pairwise disjoint bridge-covering paths
P,Q,R
so that they cover all the bridges, and remove
1
edge so that no bridge is created.
In this way, we obtain
H^{\prime}
with
|H^{\prime}|=|H|+|P|+|Q|+|R|-1
, and
\mathrm{cr}(H^{\prime})\leq\mathrm{cr}(H)+1-(|P|-1+|Q|-1+|R|-1+2+1)=\mathrm{cr}(H)-|P|-|Q|-|R|+1
.
The last
+1
is the unused credit.
Hence
\mathrm{cost}(H^{\prime})\leq\mathrm{cost}(H)
and we are done.

(iii)

(Do not need the unused credits from the bridges)
Add three pairwise disjoint bridge-covering paths
P,Q,R
so that they cover all the bridges, and remove
2
edges so that no bridge is created.
In this way, we obtain
H^{\prime}
with
|H^{\prime}|=|H|+|P|+|Q|+|R|-2
, and
\mathrm{cr}(H^{\prime})\leq\mathrm{cr}(H)+1-(|P|-1+|Q|-1+|R|-1+2)=\mathrm{cr}(H)-|P|-|Q|-|R|+2
.
Hence
\mathrm{cost}(H^{\prime})\leq\mathrm{cost}(H)
and we are done.

If there are indices
i
and
j
with
i\leq j
such that
u_{j}\in R(u_{1})
and
u_{i}\in R(u_{7})
,
let the corresponding bridge-covering paths be
P
and
Q
.
If
P
and
Q
intersect internally, then there is another bridge-covering path between
u_{1}
and
u_{8}
, contracting
u_{8}\notin R(u_{1})
.
If
P
and
Q
are internally disjoint, we can perform Operation (i).

If there are not such
i
and
j
, the only remaining case is that
R(u_{1})\setminus\{u_{2}\}=\{u_{3},u_{4}\}
and
R(u_{8})\setminus\{u_{7}\}=\{u_{5},u_{6}\}
.
Then we apply
Lemma

17
with
xy=u_{5}u_{4}
and obtain that either
R(\{u_{5},u_{6},u_{7},u_{8}\})\setminus\{u_{4}\}
contains
2
lonely nodes or
R(\{u_{5},u_{6},u_{7},u_{8}\})
contains a block node (hence
u_{1}
).
However, the latter case cannot happen since
R(u_{1})\subseteq\{u_{2},u_{3},u_{4}\}
.
Consider the former case where
|R(\{u_{5},u_{6},u_{7},u_{8}\})\setminus\{u_{4}\}|\geq 2
, which implies
R(\{u_{5},u_{6},u_{7},u_{8}\})\setminus\{u_{4}\}=\{u_{2},u_{3}\}
.
Let
P
be any bridge-covering path from
u_{3}
to some
u_{j}
with
j\geq 5
.
We add to
H
a bridge-covering path
Q
from
u_{1}
to
u_{4}
,
P
, and a bridge-covering path
R
from
u_{5}
to
u_{8}
.
Further, we remove the edge
u_{3}u_{4}
.
The resulting graph
H^{\prime}
has fewer bridges than
H
, and we argue that
\mathrm{cost}(H^{\prime})\leq\mathrm{cost}(H)
.
Note that
P
and
Q
are internally disjoint as otherwise there would be another bridge-covering path from
u_{1}
to
u_{j}
.
Similarly,
Q
and
R
are internally disjoint.
Therefore, we can apply Operation (ii).

We finally consider the case where
q=7
, i.e.,
T_{C}^{\prime}
is a path of
6
edges.
Unless explicitly written, we will immediately pay back the loans using all the credits of the bridges, and will not use the credits on the bridges for buying edges.
By the above discussion, we have
R(u_{1})\cap\{u_{3},u_{4},\dots,u_{6}\}|\geq 2
and

|R(u_{7})\cap\{u_{2},u_{3},\dots,u_{5}\}|\geq 2
.
If there is some index
i
such that
u_{i+1}\in R(u_{1})
and
u_{i}\in R(u_{7})
, then the tuple
(u_{1},u_{7},u_{i+1},u_{i})
satisfies the conditions of
Lemma

19
, and we can make progress.
Hence we can assume this does not happen.
We make a case distinction on the maximum index
i
such that
u_{i}\in R(u_{1})
.

Case 1:

\argmax_{i}\{u_{i}\mid u_{i}\in R(u_{1})\}=6
.
We have
R(u_{7})\subseteq\{u_{2},u_{3},u_{4}\}
.
There are two subcases.

Case 1.1:

R(u_{1})\setminus\{u_{2}\}=\{u_{5},u_{6}\}
and
R(u_{7})\setminus\{u_{6}\}=\{u_{2},u_{3}\}
.
Consider the
3
-vertex cut
\{u_{2},u_{3},u_{6}\}
.
Let
A
(resp.
B
) be the set of vertices in
G\setminus\{u_{2},u_{3},u_{6}\}
reachable from the vertices in the block
u_{1}
(resp.
u_{7}
).
Let
R
be
V\setminus(A\cup B\cup C)
.

If
B
contains some other component, then
|B|\geq 9
since
u_{7}
contains at least
5
vertices and any other component contains at least
4
vertices.
Let
S:=A\cup R
,
T:=B
.
We claim that
\{u_{2},u_{3},u_{6}\}
is a large
3
-vertex cut.
Suppose it is not a large
3
-vertex cut.
Then
|A|\leq 7
, which implies that any bridge-covering path between
u_{1}
and
u_{5}
(or
u_{6}
) must be a single edge, and the block node
u_{1}
consists of a nice
\mathcal{C}_{5}
.
We number the
5
vertices
b_{1},\dots,b_{5}
of
u_{1}
as in
Definition

3
.
By
Lemma

4
, there is a 3-matching between
V(u_{7})
and
V\setminus V(u_{7})
.
Since
b_{2}
and
b_{5}
are vertices of degree
2
, the
3
-matching must be incident to
b_{1},b_{3}
and
b_{4}
, i.e., the
3
-matching matches
b_{1},b_{3},b_{4}
respectively to
u_{2},u_{5},u_{6}
(but the correspondence between the endpoints is not clear).
If there is an edge between
b_{3}
and
u_{2}
,
then there is another edge between
b_{4}
and some
w\in\{u_{5},u_{6}\}
.
We can add
b_{3}u_{2}
and
b_{4}w
, and remove
b_{3}b_{4}
and
b_{1}u_{1}
to obtain
the desired
H^{\prime}
with fewer bridges.
Symmetrically, we can assume that there is no edge between
b_{4}
and
u_{2}
.
Hence, we can assume
b_{3}u_{5}\in E
and
b_{4}u_{6}\in E
.
Then we add
b_{3}u_{5}
and
b_{4}u_{6}
, and remove
b_{3}b_{4}
and
u_{5}u_{6}
to obtain the desired
H^{\prime}
with fewer bridges.

If
B
does not contain any other component,
then any bridge-covering path starting from
u_{7}
is a single edge.
If
u_{7}
is not an original block in
H_{0}
, i.e., it is obtained after some steps of bridge-covering, then
|B|\geq 8
, which implies
\{u_{2},u_{3},u_{6}\}
is a large
3
-vertex cut.
Now assume that
u_{7}
is an original block of
H_{0}
.
If it contains at least
6
vertices, by the loan invariants, we have
\mathrm{loan}_{f}(H)\leq\frac{1}{4}+11\delta
.
We have
6
bridges and
6(\frac{1}{4}-\delta)
credits in total, and we use them to fully pay for
\mathrm{loan}_{f}(H)\leq\frac{1}{4}+11\delta
.
After that, we have at least
6(\frac{1}{4}-\delta)-\mathrm{loan}_{f}(H)\geq\frac{5}{4}-17\delta\geq 1
credits.
Then we can perform the same operations as we do for the case with
q=8
.
Finally, consider the case
u_{7}
is a (nice)
\mathcal{C}_{5}
.
Then we can perform the same operations as the previous case where
|A|=7
.

Case 1.2:

R(u_{1})\setminus\{u_{2}\}=\{u_{4},u_{6}\}
and
R(u_{7})\setminus\{u_{6}\}=\{u_{2},u_{4}\}
.
We claim that
\{u_{2},u_{4},u_{6}\}
is a large
3
-vertex cut, contradicting that
G
is structured.
Let
A
(resp.
B
) be the set of vertices in
G\setminus\{u_{2},u_{4},u_{6}\}
reachable from the vertices in the block
u_{1}
(resp.
u_{7}
).
Let
R
be
V\setminus(A\cup B\cup C)
.
We can w.l.o.g.
|A|\geq|B|
by symmetry.
We follow the notations in
Definition

21
.
Let
V_{1}:=A\cup R
and
V_{2}:=B\cup\{u_{3},u_{5}\}
.
If both
|A|\geq 8
and
|B|\geq 8
, then
\{u_{2},u_{4},u_{6}\}
is a large
3
-vertex cut, a contraction.
Now we assume
|B|<8
, which implies
u_{7}
must be a nice
\mathcal{C}_{5}
and
V(u_{7})
can only be adjacent to
u_{2}
and
u_{4}
by the definition of
B
.
We number the
5
vertices
b_{1},\dots,b_{5}
in
u_{7}
as in
Definition

3
.
By
Lemma

4
, there is a 3-matching between
V(u_{7})
and
V\setminus V(u_{7})
.
Since
b_{2}
and
b_{5}
are vertices of degree
2
, the
3
-matching must be incident to
b_{1},b_{3}
and
b_{4}
.
As in Case 1.1, we can assume that the
3
-matching contains
b_{3}u_{2}
and
b_{4}u_{4}
.
Then we can perform Operation (iii) by adding the bridge-covering path between
u_{1}
and
u_{6}
,
b_{3}u_{2}
, and
b_{4}u_{4}
,
and removing
b_{1}u_{6}
and
b_{3}b_{4}
.

Case 2:

\argmax_{i}\{u_{i}\mid u_{i}\in R(u_{1})\}=5
.
We can also assume
\argmin_{j}\{u_{j}\mid u_{j}\in R(u_{7})\}\geq 3
.
Otherwise, we can swap the roles of
u_{1},u_{7}
and end up with the previous case.
Recall that there is no such index
i
that
u_{i+1}\in R(u_{1})
and
u_{i}\in R(u_{7})
,
Hence, we have
R(u_{7})\setminus\{u_{6}\}=\{u_{3},u_{5}\}
, which implies
R(u_{1})\setminus\{u_{2}\}=\{u_{3},u_{5}\}
.
We apply
Lemma

17
with
xy=u_{2}u_{3}
and obtain that either
R(\{u_{1},u_{2}\})\setminus\{u_{3}\}
contains
2
lonely nodes or
R(\{u_{1},u_{2}\})
contains a block node (hence
u_{7}
).
The latter is impossible since
u_{1},u_{2}\notin R(u_{7})
.
In the former case,
there must be some bridge-covering path
P
from
u_{2}
to
u_{j}
for some
j\in\{4,6\}
.

Case 2.1:

j=4
.
Let
Q
be any bridge-covering path from
u_{1}
to
u_{3}
and
R
be any bridge-covering path from
u_{7}
to
u_{3}
.
By arguments similar to previous cases,
P,Q,R
are pairwise disjoint.
So we can perform Operation (iii): we add
P,Q,R
to
H
and remove the edges
u_{2}u_{3},u_{3}u_{4}
.

Case 2.2:

j=6
.
Let
Q
be any bridge-covering path from
u_{1}
to
u_{3}
and
R
be any bridge-covering path from
u_{7}
to
u_{5}
.
By arguments similar to previous cases,
P,Q,R
are pairwise disjoint.
So we can perform Operation (iii): we add
P,Q,R
to
H
and remove the edges
u_{2}u_{3},u_{5}u_{6}
.

Case 3:

\argmax_{i}\{u_{i}\mid u_{i}\in R(u_{1})\}=4
. We can also assume
\argmin_{j}\{u_{j}\mid u_{j}\in R(u_{7})\}=4
. Otherwise, we can swap the roles of
u_{1},u_{7}
and end up with the previous cases.
We apply
Lemma

17
with
xy=u_{5}u_{4}
and obtain that either
R(\{u_{5},u_{6},u_{7}\})\setminus\{u_{4}\}
contains
2
lonely nodes or
R(\{u_{5},u_{6},u_{7}\})
contains a block node (hence
u_{1}
).
However, the latter case cannot happen since
R(u_{1})\subseteq\{u_{2},u_{3},u_{4}\}
.
Hence
R(\{u_{5},u_{6},u_{7}\})\setminus\{u_{4}\}
contains
2
lonely nodes, which are
u_{2}
and
u_{3}
.
Let
P
be a bridge-covering path from
u_{2}
to some
u_{j}
with
5\leq j\leq 6
.
Let
Q
be any bridge-covering path from
u_{1}
to
u_{3}
and
R
be any bridge-covering path from
u_{7}
to
u_{j-1}
.
By arguments similar to previous cases,
P,Q,R
are pairwise disjoint.
So we can perform Operation (iii): we add
P,Q,R
to
H
and remove the edges
u_{2}u_{3},u_{j-1}u_{j}
.
∎

By
7
,
T_{C}(F)
has at most
4
leaves.
By exhaustively applying
Lemma

24
, we are done with the case where
T_{C}(F)
has
2
leaves.
Now we consider the case where
T_{C}(F)
has
3
leaves, i.e., it consists of
3
paths intersecting at one common node
r
.
By the arguments on the loans of each color, we have
\mathrm{loan}_{f}(H)\leq\frac{1}{4}+15\delta
, which implies the credits of two bridges are enough to pay for the loan.
Let the
3
leaves be
u_{1},u_{2},u_{3}
respectively.
We can assume that we cannot use
Lemma

24
to make progress.
Then
u_{1},u_{2},u_{3}
are the only block nodes in
T_{C}(F)
.
For
1\leq i\leq 3
, let
T_{u_{i}}
be the subtree of
T_{C}\setminus F
containing
u_{i}
.
Since we aim to cover the bridges in
F
, we can assume w.l.o.g. that any bridge-covering that has one endpoint in
T_{u_{i}}
starts at
u_{i}
.
Hence, we can consider
T_{C}^{\prime}
obtained from
T_{C}
by contracting
T_{u_{i}}
.
We slightly abuse the notations by denoting the leaves of
T_{C}^{\prime}
as
u_{1},u_{2},u_{3}
.

Lemma 25
.

If
T_{C}(F)
is a tree with
3
leaves and every non-leaf node is a lonely node, then there is a polynomial-time algorithm that outputs a canonical 2-edge cover
H^{\prime}
of
G
satisfying the conditions of
Lemma

9
.

Proof.

We can assume that w.l.o.g.
u_{1},u_{2},u_{3}
are sorted in non-increasing distances to
r
.
Hence
T_{C}^{\prime}(u_{1},u_{2})
is a longest path in
T_{C}^{\prime}
.
Suppose
T_{C}^{\prime}
has at least
7
bridges.
Then we can immediately apply
Corollary

23
with
l=2
.
Hence, we can assume that
T_{C}^{\prime}
has at most
6
edges in the following.
Let
d_{i}
be the distance between
u_{i}
and
r
on
T_{C}^{\prime}
.
Then the only possible configurations of
(d_{1},d_{2},d_{3})
are

(3,2,1),(3,1,1),(2,2,2),(2,2,1),(2,1,1)
and
(1,1,1)
.
We assume that there is no bridge-covering path between
u_{i}
and
u_{j}
for any
1\leq i,j\leq 3
, since otherwise we can add these cheap bridge-covering path and make progress.

Case 1:

(d_{1},d_{2},d_{3})=(3,2,1)
or
(d_{1},d_{2},d_{3})=(3,1,1)
.
We only show that case where
(d_{1},d_{2},d_{3})=(3,2,1)
.
The other case is similar.
We label the nodes on the path between
u_{1}
and
u_{2}
as
u_{1},v_{1},v_{2},v_{3},v_{4},u_{2}
.
By applying
Lemma

17
with
xy=u_{1}v_{1}
, we have
|R(u_{1})\setminus\{v_{1}\}\cap\{v_{2},v_{3},v_{4}\}|\geq 2
.
Similarly, we have
|R(u_{2})\setminus\{v_{4}\}\cap\{v_{1},v_{2},v_{3}\}|\geq 2
.
Then there must be some index
i
such that
v_{i+1}\in R(u_{1})
and
v_{i}\in R(u_{2})
.
Hence we can apply
Lemma

19
with
(u_{1},u_{2},v_{i+1},v_{i})
to make progress.

Case 2:

(d_{1},d_{2},d_{3})=(2,2,2)
or
(d_{1},d_{2},d_{3})=(2,2,1)
.
We only show that case where
(d_{1},d_{2},d_{3})=(2,2,2)
.
The other case is similar.
Let
v_{i}
be the node between
u_{i}
and
r
for
1\leq i\leq 3
.
By applying
Lemma

17
with
xy=u_{1}v_{1}
, we have
|R(u_{1})\setminus\{v_{1}\}\cap\{r,v_{2},v_{3}\}|\geq 2
.
We can assume w.l.o.g. that
v_{2}\in R(u_{1})
.
By applying
Lemma

17
with
xy=u_{2}v_{2}
, we have
|R(u_{1})\setminus\{v_{2}\}\cap\{r,v_{1},v_{3}\}|\geq 2
.
If
r\in R(u_{2})
, we can apply
Lemma

19
with
(u_{1},u_{2},v_{2},v_{1})
.
If
r\notin R(u_{2})
, then
v_{3}\in R(u_{2})
and we can apply
Lemma

18
with
(u_{1},u_{2},v_{2},v_{3})
.

Case 3:

(d_{1},d_{2},d_{3})=(2,1,1)
or
(d_{1},d_{2},d_{3})=(1,1,1)
.
By applying
Lemma

17
with
xy=u_{3}r
,
R(u_{3})\setminus\{r\}
contains
2
lonely nodes, which is impossible.
∎

Finally, we consider the case where
T_{C}(F)
has
4
leaves.

Lemma 26
.

If
T_{C}(F)
is a tree with
4
leaves and every non-leaf node is a lonely node, then there is a polynomial-time algorithm that outputs a canonical 2-edge cover
H^{\prime}
of
G
satisfying the conditions of
Lemma

9
.

Proof.

By
7
, we have
\mathrm{loan}_{f}(H)\leq 20\delta
, which implies the credits of one bridge are enough to pay for the loan.
Let the
4
leaf block nodes be
b_{1},b_{2},b_{3},b_{4}
.
If
T_{C}(F)
has at least
6
edges, then we can apply
Corollary

23
with
\ell=1
.
In the following, we can assume that
T_{C}(F)
has at most
5
edges and the longest path in
T_{C}(F)
has at most
3
edges.

Case 1:
the longest path has
2
edges.
In this case,
T_{C}(F)
must be isomorphic to a star, i.e., the
4
blocks are incident to a common node
r
.
We apply
Lemma

17
with
xy=b_{1}r
.
Since
r
is the only node that could be a lonely node, there must be a bridge-covering path between
b_{1}
and some other
b_{i}
for
2\leq i\leq 4
.
Hence, we can add the cheap bridge-covering path to make progress.

Case 2:
the longest path has
3
edges.
We assume that the longest path consists of
b_{1},v_{1},v_{2},b_{2}
.
Since it is a longest path,
b_{3}
and
b_{4}
must be incident to either
v_{1}
or
v_{2}
.
We apply
Lemma

17
with
xy=b_{1}v_{1}
.
Since
R(b_{1})\setminus\{v_{1}\}
cannot contain
2
lonely nodes (only
v_{2}
can be a lonely node), there must be a bridge-covering path between
b_{1}
and some other block node.
Hence, we can add the bridge-covering path to make progress.
∎

3.2
Gluing

We have a structured graph
G
as input and a bridgeless canonical 2-edge cover
H
of
G
with
\mathrm{cost}(H)\leq(\frac{1}{4}-\delta)\mathsf{opt}
such that
H
satisfies the credit invariant for each 2EC component:

•

Each large component receives a credit of
2
,

•

each rich vertex receives a credit of
2(\frac{5}{4}-\delta)
,

•

each
\mathcal{C}_{4}
receives a credit of
1
, and

•

each
\mathcal{C}_{i}
receives a credit of
(\frac{1}{4}-\delta)i
for
5\leq i\leq 8
.

The main goal of this section is to prove
Lemma

10
, which we restate for convenience.

See
10

We first introduce some definitions. In what follows, unless otherwise mentioned,
G
is a structured graph and
H
is a bridgeless weakly canonical 2-edge cover of
G
.

Observation 27
.

Let
A
be a node of
\bm{\hat{}}{G}_{H}
such that
C_{A}
is a rich vertex.
Then
A
cannot be a cut node of
\bm{\hat{}}{G}_{H}
.

Otherwise,
C_{A}
is a cut vertex of
G
, which contradicts the fact that
G
is structured.

Observation 28
.

Let
S
be a segment of
\bm{\hat{}}{G}_{H}
with
|S|=2
and let
A
be a node in
S
. Then
A
is either a cut node of
\bm{\hat{}}{G}_{H}
or a pendant node of
\bm{\hat{}}{G}_{H}
.

Proposition 29
.

Let
A
and
B
be adjacent nodes in
\bm{\hat{}}{G}_{H}
such that
A\cup B
is a segment. If neither
C_{A}
nor
C_{B}
is a rich vertex, then there exists a matching of size
3
going between
V(C_{A})
and
V(C_{B})
in
G
.

There is a node
L
of
\bm{\hat{}}{G}_{H}
such that
C_{L}
is a huge component.
Let
S
be a segment of
\bm{\hat{}}{G}_{H}
that contains
L
.

See
11

See
12

As explained before,
the above two lemmas immediately imply
Lemma

10
. All that remains is to prove the above two lemmas, which we do in the next two subsections.
The following lemma is a useful tool to prove the above two lemmas.

Lemma 30
.

Let
L
be a node of
\bm{\hat{}}{G}_{H}
such that
C_{L}
is a huge component.
Let
S
be a segment of
\bm{\hat{}}{G}_{H}
that contains
L
and let
A
be another node in
S
.
If there is a cycle
F
in
S
through
L
and
A
, such that
F
is incident to two distinct vertices
u
and
v
in
C_{A}
, and there is a Hamiltonian path
P
between
u
and
v
in
G[V(C_{A})]
, then we can compute in polynomial time a canonical bridgeless 2-edge cover
H^{\prime}
of
G
such that
\mathrm{cost}(H^{\prime})\leq\mathrm{cost}(H)
.

Proof.

We set
H^{\prime}\coloneq(H\setminus E(C_{A}))\cup F\cup P
, which is clearly a canonical bridgeless 2-edge cover of
G
with fewer components than
H
. Furthermore,
\mathrm{cost}(H^{\prime})-\mathrm{cost}(H)=(|H^{\prime}|-|H|)+(\mathrm{cr}(H^{\prime})-\mathrm{cr}(H))=(|F|-1)+(2-|F|-1)\leq 0
, since we added
|F|
edges, effectively removed
1
edge as
|C_{A}|-|P|=1
,
\mathrm{cr}(C_{L})=2
and
\mathrm{cr}(C_{A})\geq 1
in
H
, whereas in
H^{\prime}
those combine to form a single component with credit 2. Thus,
\mathrm{cost}(H^{\prime})\leq\mathrm{cost}(H)
, meeting our goal.
∎

3.2.1
Proof of
Lemma

11

Lemma 31

(Lemma 10 in
[
Bos+25
]
)
.

Let
S
be a segment of
\hat{G}_{H}
and let
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
be two
distinct components of
S
. Given edges
u_{1}X_{1},u_{2}X_{2}
, where
u_{1}\in V(C_{1}),u_{2}\in V(C_{2}),X_{1}\in V(S)\setminus\{C_{1}\},X_{2}\in V(S)\setminus\{C_{2}\}
, one can compute in polynomial time a cycle
F
in
S
containing
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
, such that
F
is incident on
u_{1}
and another vertex in
C_{1}
, and incident on
u_{2}
and another vertex in
C_{2}
.

Lemma 32

(Lemma 11 in
[
Bos+25
]
)
.

Let
S
be a segment of
\bm{\hat{}}{G}_{H}
and let
C_{1},C_{2}
be two different components of
S
such that

C_{1}
is a
4
-cycle. Given an edge
u_{2}X
, where
u_{2}\in V(C_{2})
and
X\in V(S)\setminus\{C_{2}\}
,
one can compute in polynomial time a cycle
F
in
S
incident to distinct vertices
u_{i},v_{i}\in V(C_{i})
for

i\in\{1,2\}
such that there is a Hamiltonian
u_{1},v_{1}
-path in
G[V(C_{1})]
.

Proof of
Lemma

11
.

Our goal is to construct a canonical bridgeless 2-edge cover
H^{\prime}
of
G
with fewer components compared to
H
such that
H^{\prime}
contains a huge component and
\mathrm{cost}(H^{\prime})\leq\mathrm{cost}(H)
.
Let
L
be the node corresponding to the huge component in
S
, and
A
be the other node in
S
.

If
C_{A}
is a rich vertex, then by
27
and
28
,
A
is a pendant node of
\bm{\hat{}}{G}_{H}
.
By the definition of a rich vertex,
C_{A}
is incident to at least
2
edges in
C_{L}
.
Then, we simply add any two edges between
C_{L}
and
C_{A}
to
H
to obtain
H^{\prime}
, and it can be easily checked that
H^{\prime}
meets our goal as
\mathrm{cost}(H^{\prime})-\mathrm{cost}(H)=(|H^{\prime}|-|H|)+(\mathrm{cr}(H^{\prime})-\mathrm{cr}(H))\leq 2+(2-2-2)\leq 0
(we buy 2 edges,
\mathrm{cr}(C_{L})=2,\mathrm{cr}(C_{A})\geq 2
, and, in
H^{\prime}
,
C_{L}
and
C_{A}
combine to form a single component with credit at least
2
).

From now on, we assume that
C_{A}
is not a rich vertex.
By
Proposition

29
, we know that there must be a 3-matching
M_{3}
between
C_{L}
and
C_{A}
.
First, note that if
C_{A}
is a large component, we simply add any two edges of
M_{3}
to
H
to obtain
H^{\prime}
, and it can be easily checked that
H^{\prime}
meets our goal as
\mathrm{cost}(H^{\prime})-\mathrm{cost}(H)=(|H^{\prime}|-|H|)+(\mathrm{cr}(H^{\prime})-\mathrm{cr}(H))\leq 2+(2-2-2)\leq 0
(we add 2 edges,
\mathrm{cr}(C_{L})=\mathrm{cr}(C_{A})=2
, and, in
H^{\prime}
,
C_{L}
and
C_{A}
combine to form a single component of credit
2
). Thus, we can assume that
C_{A}
is
\mathcal{C}_{i}
for some
4\leq i\leq 8
.

We now show that we can always apply
Lemma

30
. If
C_{A}
is a
\mathcal{C}_{4}
or a
\mathcal{C}_{5}
, then in either case, there must be two distinct edges in the matching
M_{3}
, say
e_{1}
and
e_{2}
, whose endpoints in
C_{A}
are adjacent via an edge
e
in
C_{A}
. Thus, the conditions of
Lemma

30
hold by setting
P=E(C_{A})\setminus\{e\}
. Otherwise,
C_{A}
is
\mathcal{C}_{i}
for some
6\leq i\leq 8
.

Case 1:
A
is not a pendant node in
\bm{\hat{}}{G}_{H}
.

Consider partitioning
V(G)
in two parts to have
V(C_{L})
in one part
V_{1}
and
V(C_{A})
in another part
V_{2}
such that the only edges crossing the parts are between
V(C_{L})
and
V(C_{A})
.
Since
L
is huge,
|V_{1}|\geq 11
.

Case 1.1:
|V_{2}|\geq 11
.

Then
Lemma

5
is applicable, implying a 4-matching
M_{4}
between
C_{A}
and
C_{L}
.
If
C_{A}
is a
\mathcal{C}_{6}
or
\mathcal{C}_{7}
,
as before, there must exist edges
e_{1}
and
e_{2}
whose endpoints in
C_{A}
are adjacent via an edge
e
in
C_{A}
. Again, we can apply
Lemma

30
by setting
P=E(C_{A})\setminus\{e\}
.
If
C_{A}
is a
\mathcal{C}_{8}
and the conditions of
Lemma

30
do not hold, then
L
must be adjacent to every other vertex of
C_{A}
.
Let the vertices of the cycle
C_{A}
be labeled as
v_{1}-v_{2}-v_{3}-v_{4}-v_{5}-v_{6}-v_{7}-v_{8}-v_{1}
and without loss of generality assume that
L
is adjacent to
v_{i}
for
i\in\{1,3,5,7\}
.
Let
S^{\prime}
be any segment of
\bm{\hat{}}{G}_{H}
containing
A
but not containing
L
.

We say an edge in
G[V(C_{A})]
is a nice diagonal if both its endpoints are in
\{v_{2},v_{4},v_{6},v_{8}\}
.
If there exists a nice diagonal
e
in
G[V(C_{A})]
,
then observe that there are two distinct edges in
M_{4}
that are incident to say
v_{i}
and
v_{j}
in
C_{A}
, and there is a Hamiltonian Path
P\subseteq\{e\}\cup E(C_{A})
from
v_{i}
to
v_{j}
in
G[V(C_{A})]
.
So we can apply
Lemma

30
.
If there is no nice diagonal, we claim that there is some edge
v_{j}X
for some
j\in\{2,4,6,8\}
and some
X\in V(S^{\prime})\setminus A
.
Otherwise, the
4
sets of edges of
G
, namely, edges incident to
v_{2}
, edges incident to
v_{4}
, edges incident to
v_{6}
, and edges incident to
v_{8}
are all inside
G[V(C_{A})]
and mutually disjoint.
As any 2ECSS of
G
must contain at least
2
edges incident on each vertex, it has to contain at least
8
edges from
G[V(C_{A})]
, and hence
C_{A}
is contractible.
We can assume without loss of generality that
v_{j}=v_{2}
.

Suppose
S^{\prime}
does not contain any component that is a
\mathcal{C}_{4}
.
Then each component (which can be a rich vertex) has at least
5(\frac{1}{4}-\delta)
credits.
We apply
Lemma

31
to
A
and any other node in
S^{\prime}
to find a cycle
F
in
S^{\prime}
containing
A
.
Further,
F
is incident to
u_{2}
and some other vertex
u_{k}
(
k\neq 2
).
Let
H^{\prime}:=(H\setminus\{v_{1}v_{2}\})\cup F\cup\{v_{1}L,v_{3}L\}
.
Notice that
|H^{\prime}|=|H|+|F|+1
.
In
H^{\prime}
there is a large component
C^{\prime}
spanning the nodes of all components of
H
incident on
F
and the component
C_{L}
.
Every such component yields at least
5(\frac{1}{4}-\delta)
credits, while
L
yields
2
credits and
A
yields
8(\frac{1}{4}-\delta)
credits.
Then we have
\mathrm{cr}(H^{\prime})\leq\mathrm{cr}(H)-(|F|-1)\cdot 5\cdot(\frac{1}{4}-\delta)-\mathrm{cr}(L)-\mathrm{cr}(A)+\mathrm{cr}(C^{\prime})\leq\mathrm{cr}(H)-(|F|-2)\cdot 5(\frac{1}{4}-\delta)-5(\frac{1}{4}-\delta)-2-8(\frac{1}{4}-\delta)+2=\mathrm{cr}(H)-(|F|-2)-13(\frac{1}{4}-\delta)\leq\mathrm{cr}(H)-|F|-1
.
Thus
\mathrm{cost}(H)-\mathrm{cost}(H^{\prime})=|H|-|H^{\prime}|+\mathrm{cr}(H)-\mathrm{cr}(H^{\prime})\geq-|F|-1+|F|+1\geq 0
.

If
H^{\prime}
contains another node
B
that is a
\mathcal{C}_{4}
,
we apply
Lemma

32
to find a cycle
F
in
S
incident to distinct nodes
b_{1},b_{2}\in V(B)
such that there is a Hamiltonian
b_{1},b_{2}
-path
P
in
G[V(B)]
, and incident to
v_{2}
and some
v_{k}
for some
k\neq 2
.
Set
H^{\prime}:=(H\setminus(E(C_{B})\cup\{v_{1}v_{2}\}))\cup\{v_{1}L,v_{3}L\}\cup F\cup P
.
Notice that
|H^{\prime}|=|H|+|F|
.
In
H^{\prime}
, there is a large component
C^{\prime}
spanning the nodes of all components of
H
incident on
F
and the component
C_{L}
. Every such component yields at least
1
credit, while
L
yields
2
credits.
Then we have
\mathrm{cr}(H^{\prime})\leq\mathrm{cr}(H)-(|F|-2)-\mathrm{cr}(L)-\mathrm{cr}(A)-\mathrm{cr}(B)+\mathrm{cr}(C^{\prime})=\mathrm{cr}(H)-(|F|-2)-2-1-1+2\leq\mathrm{cr}(H)-|F|
.
Hence
\mathrm{cost}(H)-\mathrm{cost}(H^{\prime})=|H|-|H^{\prime}|+\mathrm{cr}(H)-\mathrm{cr}(H^{\prime})\geq-|F|+|F|\geq 0
.

Case 1.2:
|V_{2}|\leq 10
.

Since
|A|\geq 6
, the only possible case is that
A
has a neighbor
B\neq L
in
\bm{\hat{}}{G}_{H}
where
B
is a
\mathcal{C}_{4}
.
If the conditions of
Lemma

30
do not hold, then
L
must be adjacent to every other vertex of
C_{A}
.
Let the vertices of the cycle
C_{A}
be labeled as
v_{1}-v_{2}-v_{3}-v_{4}-v_{5}-v_{6}-v_{1}
and without loss of generality assume that
L
is adjacent to
v_{i}
for
i\in\{1,3,5\}
.
Let
S
be the segment of
\bm{\hat{}}{G}_{H}
containing
A
and
B
.
There must be some edge
v_{j}X
for some
j\in\{2,4,6\}
and some
X\in V(S)\setminus A
, as otherwise
C_{A}
is contractible; the arguments are essentially the same as in Case 1.1.
We can assume without loss of generality that
v_{j}=v_{2}
.
We apply
Lemma

32
to find a cycle
F
in
S
incident to distinct nodes
b_{1},b_{2}\in V(B)
such that there is a Hamiltonian
b_{1},b_{2}
-path
P
in
G[V(B)]
, and incident to
v_{2}
and some
v_{k}
for some
k\neq 2
.
Set
H^{\prime}:=(H\setminus(E(C_{B})\cup\{v_{1}v_{2}\}))\cup\{v_{1}L,v_{3}L\}\cup F\cup P
.
Notice that
|H^{\prime}|=|H|+|F|
.
In
H^{\prime}
there is a large component
C^{\prime}
spanning the nodes of all components of
H
incident on
F
and the component
C_{L}
. Every such component yields at least
1
credit, while
L
yields
2
credits.
Then we have
\mathrm{cr}(H^{\prime})\leq\mathrm{cr}(H)-(|F|-2)-\mathrm{cr}(L)-\mathrm{cr}(A)-\mathrm{cr}(B)+\mathrm{cr}(C^{\prime})=\mathrm{cr}(H)-(|F|-2)-2-1-1+2\leq\mathrm{cr}(H)-|F|
.
Hence,
\mathrm{cost}(H)-\mathrm{cost}(H^{\prime})=|H|-|H^{\prime}|+\mathrm{cr}(H)-\mathrm{cr}(H^{\prime})\geq-|F|+|F|\geq 0
.

Case 2:
A
is a pendant node in
\bm{\hat{}}{G}_{H}
.

Case 2.1:
C_{A}
is a
\mathcal{C}_{6}
or
\mathcal{C}_{7}
.

Assuming that the condition of
Lemma

30
does not hold, we prove that
C_{A}
is contractible, which contradicts the fact that
G
is structured. Let the vertices of the cycle
C_{A}
be labeled as
v_{1}-v_{2}-v_{3}-v_{4}-v_{5}-v_{6}-v_{1}
if
C_{A}
is a
\mathcal{C}_{6}
or
v_{1}-v_{2}-v_{3}-v_{4}-v_{5}-v_{6}-v_{7}-v_{1}
if
C_{A}
is a
C_{7}
.
Since the condition of
Lemma

30
does not hold and there is the 3-matching
M_{3}
between
C_{L}
and
C_{A}
,
C_{L}
is adjacent to exactly three mutually non-adjacent vertices of
C_{A}
via
M_{3}
, say w.l.o.g.
\{v_{1},v_{3},v_{5}\}
(up to symmetry this is the only possibility in both the
\mathcal{C}_{6}
and
\mathcal{C}_{7}
cases).
We say an edge in
G[V(C_{A})]
is a nice diagonal if both its endpoints are in
\{v_{2},v_{4},v_{6}\}
.
Therefore, the set of nice diagonals are
\{v_{2}v_{4},v_{4}v_{6},v_{6}v_{1}\}
.
If there exists a nice diagonal
e
in
G[V(C_{A})]
,
then observe that there are two distinct edges in
M_{3}
that are incident to say
u
and
v
in
C_{A}
, and there is a Hamiltonian Path
P\subseteq\{e\}\cup E(C_{A})
from
u
to
v
in
G[V(C_{A})]
.
For example, if the diagonal
v_{2}v_{4}
exists in
G
and
C_{A}
is
\mathcal{C}_{6}
, then the desired Hamiltonian path is
v_{3}-v_{2}-v_{4}-v_{5}-v_{6}-v_{1}
(for
\mathcal{C}_{6}
, up to symmetry, this is the only case; the
\mathcal{C}_{7}
case is left to the reader to verify, a simple exercise).
Hence, the condition of
Lemma

30
holds, a contradiction.
Otherwise, if none of these nice diagonals are in
G
, we can conclude that
C_{A}
is contractible: The three sets of edges of
G
, namely, edges incident to
v_{2}
, edges incident to
v_{4}
, and edges incident to
v_{6}
are all inside
G[V(C_{A})]
and mutually disjoint.
As any 2ECSS of
G
must contain at least
2
edges incident on each vertex, it has to contain at least
6
edges from
G[V(C_{A})]
, and hence
C_{A}
is contractible.

Case 2.2:
C_{A}
is a
\mathcal{C}_{8}
.

Let
C_{A}
be
v_{1}-v_{2}-v_{3}-v_{4}-v_{5}-v_{6}-v_{7}-v_{8}-v_{1}
.
Since there is a
3
-matching between
C_{A}
and
C_{L}
, there are at least
3
vertices of
C_{A}
adjacent to
C_{L}
.
Since the condition of
Lemma

30
does not hold, up to symmetry,

C_{L}
is adjacent to exactly
\{v_{1},v_{3},v_{5},v_{7}\}
, or
\{v_{1},v_{3},v_{5}\}
, or
\{v_{1},v_{3},v_{6}\}
.

Case 2.2.1:
C_{L}
is adjacent to exactly
\{v_{1},v_{3},v_{5},v_{7}\}
.

Similar to Case 2.1, we can show that there is no edge between
v_{i}
and
v_{j}
for even values of
i
and
j
, which implies that
C_{A}
is contractible.

Case 2.2.2:
C_{L}
is adjacent to exactly
\{v_{1},v_{3},v_{5}\}
.

Similar to Case 2.1, we can show that none of the diagonals
\{v_{2}v_{4},v_{2}v_{6},v_{4}v_{6},v_{2}v_{8},v_{4}v_{8}\}
exists in
G
.
Hence, the
4
sets of edges of
G
, namely, edges incident to
v_{2}
, edges incident to
v_{4}
, edges incident to
v_{6}
, and edges incident to
v_{8}
are all inside
G[V(C_{A})]
and mutually disjoint except
v_{6}v_{8}
.
As any 2ECSS of
G
must contain at least
2
edges incident to each of
v_{2},v_{4},v_{6},v_{8}
, it has to contain at least
7
edges (not
8
due to the possible existence of
v_{6}v_{8}
) from
G[V(C_{A})]
, and hence
C_{A}
is contractible.

Case 2.2.3:
C_{L}
is adjacent to exactly
\{v_{1},v_{3},v_{6}\}
.

Similar to Case 2.1, we can show that none of the diagonals
\{v_{2}v_{4},v_{2}v_{5},v_{2}v_{6},v_{2}v_{8}\}
exists in
G
.
Hence any 2ECSS of
G
has to contain contain at least
2
edges whose other endpoints are not in
\{v_{4},v_{5},v_{7},v_{8}\}
.
Further, any 2ECSS of
G
has to contain contain at least
5
edges incident to vertices in
\{v_{4},v_{5},v_{7},v_{8}\}
, which implies at least
7
edges in
G[V(C_{A})]
.
We conclude that
C_{A}
is contractible.
∎

3.2.2
Proof of
Lemma

12

Lemma 33

(Local
3
-Matching Lemma)
.

Let
S
be a segment of
\hat{G}_{H}
and let
(\hat{V}_{1},\hat{V}_{2})
be a partition of the nodes of
S
such that
\hat{V}_{1}\neq\emptyset\neq\hat{V}_{2}
. Let
V_{i}=\cup_{\hat{C}_{i}\in\hat{V}_{i}}V(C_{i})
be the set of vertices of
G
that correspond to
\hat{V}_{i}
, for
i\in\{1,2\}
. Then, there is a matching of size
3
between
V_{1}
and
V_{2}
in
G
.

Lemma 34

(Local
4
-Matching Lemma)
.

Let
S
be a segment of
\hat{G}_{H}
and let
(\hat{V}_{1},\hat{V}_{2})
be a partition of the nodes of
S
such that
\hat{V}_{1}\neq\emptyset\neq\hat{V}_{2}
. Let
V_{i}=\cup_{\hat{C}_{i}\in\hat{V}_{i}}V(C_{i})
be the set of vertices of
G
that correspond to
\hat{V}_{i}
, for
i\in\{1,2\}
.
If
|V_{i}|\geq 11
for
i\in\{1,2\}
,
then there is a matching of size
4
between
V_{1}
and
V_{2}
in
G
.

Lemma 35

(2 edges between
L
and a
\mathcal{C}_{5}
)
.

Let
S
be a segment of
\hat{G}_{S}
with
|K|\geq 3
and each component in
S
has at least
5
vertices.
Let
L
be a node in
S
such that
C_{L}
is a huge component and let
A
be a node in
S
such that
C_{A}
is a
\mathcal{C}_{5}
.
If there is a 2-matching
M_{2}
between
C_{L}
and
C_{A}
, then in polynomial time we can compute a canonical bridgeless 2-edge cover
H^{\prime}
of
H
such that
H^{\prime}
has fewer components than
H
,
H^{\prime}
contains a huge component, and
\mathrm{cost}(H^{\prime})\leq\mathrm{cost}(H)
.

Proof.

Let the vertices in
C_{A}
be labeled as
v_{1}-v_{2}-v_{3}-v_{4}-v_{5}-v_{1}
.
If the two matching edges are incident to adjacent vertices in
C_{A}
, say
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
, then we can set
H^{\prime}\coloneq(H\setminus\{v_{1}v_{2}\})\cup M_{2}
.
We have
\mathrm{cost}(H^{\prime})-\mathrm{cost}(H)=(|H^{\prime}|-|H|)+(\mathrm{cr}(H^{\prime})-\mathrm{cr}(H))=(2-1)+(2-2-\mathrm{cr}(C_{A}))\leq 0
, since we added two edges, effectively removed 1 edge,
\mathrm{cr}(C_{L})=2
and
\mathrm{cr}(C_{A})\geq 1
in
H
, whereas in
H^{\prime}
those combine to form a single component with credit 2. Thus,
\mathrm{cost}(H^{\prime})\leq\mathrm{cost}(H)
, meeting our goal.

In the following, we assume that the two matching edges are incident to non-adjacent vertices in
C_{A}
, say
v_{1}
and
v_{3}
without loss of generality.
There must be a
3
-matching between
C_{A}
and
V(S)\setminus C_{A}

by
Lemma

33
.
So there exists some edge between
\{v_{2},v_{4},v_{5}\}
and
V(S)\setminus C_{A}
.
If there is some edge between
\{v_{2},v_{4},v_{5}\}
and
L
, then it follows as in the previous case where
M_{2}
is incident to adjacent vertices in
C_{A}
.
Hence there is some edge between
v_{i}
for
i\in\{2,4,5\}
and
C_{B}
for some
B\in S\setminus\{L,A\}
.
Let
v_{j}\in\{v_{1},v_{3}\}
be the neighbor of
v_{i}
with
v_{j}L\in M_{2}
.
Since
S
is 2-node-connected, there must be a path
P
in
S
between
L
and
B
without passing through
A
.
Let
F\coloneq P\cup\{v_{j}L,v_{i}B\}
.
Let
H^{\prime}:=H\cup F\setminus\{v_{i}v_{j}\}
.
In
H^{\prime}
there is a large component
C^{\prime}
spanning components incident to
F
. Every such component yields at least
1
credit, while
L
yields
2
credits.
Then we have
\mathrm{cr}(H^{\prime})\leq\mathrm{cr}(H)-\mathrm{cr}(L)-(|F|-1)+\mathrm{cr}(C^{\prime})=\mathrm{cr}(H)-2-(|F|-1)+2\leq\mathrm{cr}(H)-(|F|-1)
.
Hence
\mathrm{cost}(H)-\mathrm{cost}(H^{\prime})=|H|-|H^{\prime}|+\mathrm{cr}(H)-\mathrm{cr}(H^{\prime})\geq-|F|+1+(|F|-1)=0
.
∎

Lemma 36

(3 edges between
L
and a
\mathcal{C}_{6}
or a
\mathcal{C}_{7}
)
.

Let
S
be a segment of
\hat{G}_{S}
with
|S|\geq 3
.
Let
L
be a node in
S
such that
C_{L}
is a huge component and let
A
be a node in
S
such that
C_{A}
is a
\mathcal{C}_{6}
or a
\mathcal{C}_{7}
.
If there is a 3-matching
M_{3}
between
C_{L}
and
C_{A}
, then in polynomial time we can compute a canonical bridgeless 2-edge cover
H^{\prime}
of
H
such that
H^{\prime}
has fewer components than
H
,
H^{\prime}
contains a huge component, and
\mathrm{cost}(H^{\prime})\leq\mathrm{cost}(H)
.

Proof.

Let the vertices of
C_{A}
be labeled as
v_{1}-v_{2}-v_{3}-v_{4}-v_{5}-v_{6}-v_{1}
or
v_{1}-v_{2}-v_{3}-v_{4}-v_{5}-v_{6}-v_{7}-v_{1}
.
If the edges of
M_{3}
are incident to adjacent vertices in
C_{A}
, say
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
, then we can set
H^{\prime}\coloneq(H\setminus\{v_{1}v_{2}\})\cup M_{2}
.

Without loss of generality, we can assume that the edges of
M_{3}
are incident to
v_{1},v_{3}
and
v_{5}
.
Consider
\{v_{2},v_{4},v_{6}\}
.
If
E(G[\{v_{2},v_{4},v_{6}\}])\neq\emptyset
,
then there is a Hamiltonian path in
G[C_{A}]
and we can apply
Lemma

30
.
In the following, we assume that
E(G[\{v_{2},v_{4},v_{6}\}])=\emptyset
.
Then there must be some edge in
G
between
\{v_{2},v_{4},v_{6}\}
and
C_{B}
for some
B\notin\{L,A\}
.
Otherwise,
C_{A}
is contractible.
Without loss of generality, we can assume that
v_{2}
is adjacent to
C_{B}
for some
B\notin\{L,A\}
.

If
B\in S
, then we can find a cycle
F
in
S
through
L
and
A
, containing the two edges
v_{1}L
and
v_{2}B
as follows.
Since
S
is 2-node-connected, there must be a path
P
in
S
between
L
and
B
without passing through
A
.
Then
F\coloneq P\cup\{v_{1}L,v_{2}B\}
is a desired cycle.
Further, we can apply
Lemma

30
to find
H^{\prime}
using the
v_{1}
,
v_{2}
-Hamiltonian path in
G[V(C_{A})]
from
v_{1}
to
v_{2}
.

In the remaining case,
B
is in some other segment
S^{\prime}\neq S
(
A
is a cut node).
If the total number of vertices in
S^{\prime}
is at least
11
, then we can apply
Lemma

5
to find a 4-matching between
C_{B}
and
S\setminus C_{B}
.
Hence we are in the previous case where there is some edge in
G
between
\{v_{2},v_{4},v_{6}\}
and
C_{B}
for some
B\notin\{L,A\}
.
If the total number of vertices in
S^{\prime}
is at most
10
, then either
|S^{\prime}|=2
and it contains another node
B
that
C_{B}
is a
\mathcal{C}_{4}
, or
S^{\prime}
contains a rich vertex.

In the former case, by
Lemma

32
we can find a cycle
F
in
S^{\prime}
incident to two vertices
a,b
of
B
such that there is a Hamiltonian
a
,
b
-path
P
in
G[V(B)]
.
Further,
F
is incident to
v_{2}
and some
v_{k}
for some
k\neq 2
in
C_{A}
.
Set
H^{\prime}:=(H\setminus(E(C_{B})\cup\{v_{1}v_{2}\}))\cup\{v_{1}L,v_{3}L\}\cup F\cup P
.
Notice that
|H^{\prime}|=|H|+|F|
.
In
H^{\prime}
, there is a large component
C^{\prime}
spanning the nodes of all components of
H
incident on
F
and the component
C_{L}
. Every such component yields at least
1
credit, while
L
yields
2
credits.
Then we have
\mathrm{cr}(H^{\prime})\leq\mathrm{cr}(H)-(|F|-2)-\mathrm{cr}(L)-\mathrm{cr}(A)-\mathrm{cr}(B)+\mathrm{cr}(C^{\prime})=\mathrm{cr}(H)-(|F|-2)-2-1-1+2\leq\mathrm{cr}(H)-|F|
.
Hence
\mathrm{cost}(H)-\mathrm{cost}(H^{\prime})=|H|-|H^{\prime}|+\mathrm{cr}(H)-\mathrm{cr}(H^{\prime})\geq-|F|+|F|\geq 0
.

In the latter case with some rich vertex in
S^{\prime}
,
it follows similarly since a rich vertex contains at least
2
credits (we do not save one edge inside
B
, but we collect one more credit).
∎

We can generalize the above lemma by replacing one of the edge in the
3
-matching by a path.
This follows from that adding the path is equivalently to adding a single edge since each internal node on the path has at least
1
credit and can pay for one bought edge.

Corollary 37

(2 edges and a path between
L
and a
\mathcal{C}_{6}
)
.

Let
S
be a segment of
\hat{G}_{S}
with
|S|\geq 3
.
Let
L
be a node in
S
such that
C_{L}
is a huge component and let
A
be a node in
S
such that
C_{A}
is a
\mathcal{C}_{6}
, labelled as
v_{1}-v_{2}-v_{3}-v_{4}-v_{5}-v_{6}-v_{1}
.
Suppose that
v_{1}L,v_{3}L\in E(G)
and there is a path
P_{M}
in
S
between
L
and
A
that is incident to
v_{5}
.
Then in polynomial time we can compute a canonical bridgeless 2-edge cover
H^{\prime}
of
H
such that
H^{\prime}
has fewer components than
H
,
H^{\prime}
contains a huge component, and
\mathrm{cost}(H^{\prime})\leq\mathrm{cost}(H)
.

Proof.

By simulating the proof of
Lemma

36
, we can assume that
v_{j}\in\{v_{2},v_{4},v_{6}\}
is adjacent to
C_{B}
for some
B\notin\{L,A\}
.

If
B\in S
, then we can find a cycle
F
in
S
through
L
and
A
, containing the two edges
v_{j}B
and
v_{i}L
where
v_{i}
is adjacent to
v_{j}
in
C_{A}
as follows.
Since
S
is 2-node-connected, there must be a path
P
in
S
between
L
and
B
without passing through
A
.
By the conditions of the corollary, there must be some
v_{i}\neq v_{5}
such that
v_{i}L\in E(G)
.
Hence, we can apply
Lemma

30
to find
H^{\prime}
using the
v_{j}
,
v_{i}
-Hamiltonian path in
G[V(C_{A})]
from
v_{j}
to
v_{i}
.

If
B\notin S
, the arguments in the proof of
Lemma

36
follow similarly as adding the path between
L
and
v_{5}
is equivalently to adding a single edge
v_{5}L
.
∎

Proof of
Lemma

12
.

Let
C_{L}
be a huge component such that
L
is part of a segment
S
(
|S|\geq 3
) of
\bm{\hat{}}{G}_{H}
.
If
S
contains a rich vertex
r
,
then we find a cycle
F
in
S
through
L
and
r
.
Let
S^{\prime}:=S\cup F
.
Notice that
|S^{\prime}|=|S|+|F|
.
In
S^{\prime}
there is a large component
C^{\prime}
spanning the nodes of all components of
S
incident on
F
and the component
C_{L}
. Every such component yields at least
1
credit, while
L
and
r
yield at least
2
credits.
Then we have
\mathrm{cr}(S^{\prime})\leq\mathrm{cr}(S)-(|F|-2)-\mathrm{cr}(L)-\mathrm{cr}(A)+\mathrm{cr}(C^{\prime})=\mathrm{cr}(S)-(|F|-2)-2-2+2\leq\mathrm{cr}(S)-|F|
.
Hence,
\mathrm{cost}(S)-\mathrm{cost}(S^{\prime})=|S|-|S^{\prime}|+\mathrm{cr}(S)-\mathrm{cr}(S^{\prime})\geq-|F|+|F|\geq 0
.

If
S
contains another component
A
that is a
\mathcal{C}_{4}
, then we apply
Lemma

32
to find a cycle
F
in
S
incident to distinct nodes
b_{1},b_{2}\in V(A)
such that there is a Hamiltonian
b_{1},b_{2}
-path
P
in
G[V(A)]
.
Set
H^{\prime}:=(H\setminus E(C_{A}))\cup F\cup P
.
Notice that
|H^{\prime}|=|H|+|F|-1
.
In
H^{\prime}
there is a large component
C^{\prime}
spanning the nodes of all components of
H
incident on
F
and the component
C_{L}
. Every such component yields at least
1
credit, while
L
has
2
credits.
Then, we have
\mathrm{cr}(H^{\prime})\leq\mathrm{cr}(H)-(|F|-2)-\mathrm{cr}(L)-\mathrm{cr}(A)+\mathrm{cr}(C^{\prime})=\mathrm{cr}(H)-(|F|-2)-2-1+2\leq\mathrm{cr}(S)-|F|+1
.
Hence
\mathrm{cost}(H)-\mathrm{cost}(H^{\prime})=|H|-|H^{\prime}|+\mathrm{cr}(H)-\mathrm{cr}(H^{\prime})\geq 1-|F|+|F|-1\geq 0
.

Further, if there is a cycle
F
in
\bm{\hat{}}{G}_{H}
containing
L
with
|F|\geq 6
, then we can add the cycle, i.e.,
H^{\prime}=H\cup F
.
In
H^{\prime}
there is a large component
C^{\prime}
spanning the nodes of all components of
S
incident on
F
and the component
C_{L}
.
Every such component yields at least
5(\frac{1}{4}-\delta)
credit, while
L
has
2
credits.
Then we have
\mathrm{cr}(H^{\prime})\leq\mathrm{cr}(H)-(|F|-1)\cdot 5(\frac{1}{4}-\delta)-\mathrm{cr}(L)+\mathrm{cr}(C^{\prime})=\mathrm{cr}(H)-(|F|-1)\cdot 5(\frac{1}{4}-\delta)-2+2\leq\mathrm{cr}(H)-(|F|-1)\cdot(\frac{5}{4}-5\delta)
.
Hence
\mathrm{cost}(H)-\mathrm{cost}(H^{\prime})=|H|-|H^{\prime}|+\mathrm{cr}(S)-\mathrm{cr}(H^{\prime})\geq-|F|+(|F|-1)\cdot(\frac{5}{4}-5\delta)=(|F|-1)\cdot(\frac{1}{4}-5\delta)-1\geq\frac{5}{4}-25\delta-1\geq 0
.

In the following, we assume that

•

H
contains only components of size at least
5
and

•

any cycle containing
L
in
S
has length at most
5
.

Let
F^{*}
be a
longest
cycle in
S
containing
L
. For the remainder of this proof, we distinguish different cases based on
|F^{*}|
.

Case 1:
|F^{*}|=3
.

In this case,
S
contains only
3
components, where one is
L
.
Let
A
and
B
be the other two components in
S
. Let
F^{*}=\{LA,LB,AB\}
.
If
C_{A}
and
C_{B}
together have at least
3
credits, then
S^{\prime}:=S\cup F^{*}

satisfies the lemma since we buy
3
edges, which can be paid by the credits
of
A
and
B
. In the following, we assume that
C_{A}
and
C_{B}
together have
less than
3
credits. So neither of them can be a large component and the
total number of edges in them is at most
12
. Without loss of generality, we
assume that
|C_{A}|\leq|C_{B}|
.

Case 1.1:
A
and
B
are both a
\mathcal{C}_{5}
.

There must be a
3
-matching between
C_{L}
and
C_{A}\cup C_{B}

by
Lemma

33
. Since
A
and
B
are both
\mathcal{C}_{5}
, either
A

or
B
must be incident to at least
2
matching edges. Thus, we can
apply
Lemma

35
to find
H^{\prime}
, and we are done.

Case 1.2:
A
is a
\mathcal{C}_{5}
and
B
is a
\mathcal{C}_{6}
or a
\mathcal{C}_{7}
.

We apply
Lemma

34
to
L
and
A\cup B
so that there is a
4
-matching between
L
and
A\cup B
, because there are at least
11
vertices in
A\cup B
.
If there are at least
2
matching edges incident to
A
, then we can apply
Lemma

35
to find
H^{\prime}
.
Hence, we can assume that there is only one matching edge incident to
A
and
3
matching edges incident to
B
.
Then we can apply
Lemma

36
to find
H^{\prime}
.

Case 1.3:
A
and
B
are both a
\mathcal{C}_{6}
.

We apply
Lemma

34
to
L
and
A\cup B
so that there is a

4
-matching between
L
and
A\cup B
. If there are at least
3
matching
edges incident to one of
A
or
B
, then we can apply
Lemma

36
to
find
H^{\prime}
. In the remaining case, the
4
-matching has
2
edges incident to

A
and
2
edges incident to
B
. Let the vertices of
C_{A}
be labeled as

v_{1}-v_{2}-v_{3}-v_{4}-v_{5}-v_{6}-v_{1}
. Without loss of generality, we can assume that
the
4
-matching is incident to
v_{1}
and to one of
\{v_{2},v_{3},v_{4}\}
; the cases with
v_{5}
or
v_{6}
follow symmetrically. If

v_{2}L\in E(G)
, then there is a Hamiltonian
v_{1},v_{2}
path in
G[C_{A}]
, and
we can apply
Lemma

30
. If
v_{4}L\in E(G)
, we know there
is a
3
-matching between
C_{A}
and
C_{L}\cup C_{B}
by
Lemma

33
.
Hence there exists some edge in
G
between
\{v_{2},v_{3},v_{5},v_{6}\}
and
C_{B}
.
Without loss of generality, we can assume that
v_{2}B\in E(G)
.
Then we can apply
Lemma

30
with
F=\{v_{1}L,v_{2}B,LB\}
, because there is a Hamiltonian
v_{1},v_{2}
path in
G[C_{A}]
.

In the last case, the
4
-matching is incident to
v_{1}
and
v_{3}
.
By
Lemma

33
, there must be a 3-matching between
C_{A}
and
C_{L}\cup C_{B}
.
Hence for some
i\in\{2,4,5,6\}
,
v_{i}B\in E(G)
.
We can again apply
Lemma

30
if
i\in\{2,4,6\}
.
If
i=5
, we can apply
Corollary

37
.

Case 2:
|F^{*}|=4
.

In this case,
S
contains at least
4
components, where one is
L
. Let
A_{1},A_{2}
, and
A_{3}
be some other components. Let
\bar{F}=\{LA_{1},A_{1}A_{2},A_{2}A_{3},A_{3}L\}
.
If
A_{1},A_{2}
, and
A_{3}
together have at least
4
credits, then
S^{\prime}:=S\cup F^{*}
satisfies the lemma since we buy
4
edges, which can be paid by the
4
credits.
In the following, we assume that
A_{1},A_{2}
, and
A_{3}
together have less than
4
credits.
So neither of them can be a large component and the total number of edges in them is at most
16
.
We conclude that they can be either three
\mathcal{C}_{5}
’s or two
\mathcal{C}_{5}
’s and a
\mathcal{C}_{6}
.
Since
S
is 2VC and the longest cycle in
S
through
L
is of length
4
, we can observe that there are only
3
cases on the structure of
S
.

Case 2.1:
S=\{L,A_{1},A_{2}=D_{1},A_{3},D_{2},\ldots,D_{k}\}
for
k\geq 2
;
E[\hat{G}_{S}]=\{D_{i}A_{1},D_{i}A_{3}\mid i=1,\ldots,k\}\cup\{LA_{1},LA_{3}\}
.

By
Lemma

34
, there must be a
4
-matching between
C_{L}

and the remaining vertices in
V(S)
. Therefore, there is a 2-matching between
C_{L}

and
C_{A_{1}}
or
C_{A_{3}}
. If both
C_{A_{1}}
and
C_{A_{3}}
are
\mathcal{C}_{5}
’s,
then we can apply
Lemma

35
to find
H^{\prime}
. Therefore,
C_{A_{1}}
and

C_{A_{3}}
must be a
\mathcal{C}_{6}
and a
\mathcal{C}_{5}
, and
C_{A_{2}}
must be a
\mathcal{C}_{5}
.
Without loss of generality, we can assume that
C_{A_{1}}
is a
\mathcal{C}_{5}
and

C_{3}
is a
\mathcal{C}_{6}
. Further, we can assume that the
4
-matching has one edge
incident to
C_{A_{1}}
and
3
edges incident to
C_{3}
.
Then we can apply
Lemma

36
to find
H^{\prime}
.

Case 2.2:
S=\{L,A_{1},A_{2},A_{3}\}
.

By
Lemma

34
, there must be a
4
-matching between
C_{L}
and

C_{A_{1}}\cup C_{A_{2}}\cup C_{A_{3}}
. If all of
C_{A_{i}}
are
\mathcal{C}_{5}
, then one
of them is incident to
2
edges in the
4
-matching, and we can
apply
Lemma

35
to find
H^{\prime}
. Hence, one of
C_{A_{i}}
must be a
\mathcal{C}_{6}

and the others are
\mathcal{C}_{5}
. Further, the
4
-matching has
2
edges incident
to the
\mathcal{C}_{6}
and one edge incident to each of the
\mathcal{C}_{5}
. Without loss of
generality, we assume that
C_{A_{1}}
is a
\mathcal{C}_{6}
, and we label the vertices of

C_{A_{1}}
as
u_{1}-u_{2}-u_{3}-u_{4}-u_{5}-u_{6}-u_{1}
. Also, we can assume that the

4
-matching has
2
edges incident to non-adjacent vertices, i.e.,
u_{1}
and

u_{4}
, or to
u_{1}
and
u_{3}
.

Consider the case the
u_{1}L,u_{4}L\in E(G)
.
By
Lemma

33
, there must be some edge
e^{\prime}
between
u_{i}\in\{u_{2},u_{3},u_{5},u_{6}\}
and
L\cup C_{A_{2}}\cup C_{A_{3}}
.
Let
u_{i}
be the adjacent vertex of
u_{j}
in
C_{A_{1}}
such that
u_{i}L\in E(G)
.
By the previous arguments, there exists a cycle
F
in
\bm{\hat{}}{G}_{H}
through
L
and
A_{1}
containing the edges
u_{i}L
and
e^{\prime}
.
We set
H^{\prime}:=(H\setminus\{u_{i}u_{j}\})\cup F
.
Then we have
|H^{\prime}|=|H|+|F|-1
and
\mathrm{cr}(H^{\prime})\leq\mathrm{cr}(H)-(|F|-1)-2+2=\mathrm{cr}(H)-|F|+1
.
Hence,
\mathrm{cost}(H^{\prime})\leq\mathrm{cost}(H)
and
H^{\prime}
satisfies the lemma.

In the remaining case,
u_{1}L,u_{3}L\in E(G)
.
By
Lemma

33
, there must be some edge
e^{\prime}
between
u_{i}\in\{u_{2},u_{4},u_{5},u_{6}\}
and
L\cup C_{A_{2}}\cup C_{A_{3}}
.
If
u_{i}
is adjacent to
u_{1}
or
u_{3}
, then we can find
H^{\prime}
as in the previous case.
Hence, we can assume that
u_{i}=u_{5}
.

If
e^{\prime}
is incident to
L
, we can apply
Lemma

36
to find
H^{\prime}
.
If
e^{\prime}
is incident to
A_{j}
for
j\in\{2,3\}
, then
there is a path
u_{5}-A_{j}-L
from
u_{5}
to
L
.
So we can apply
Corollary

37
.

Case 2.3:

S=\{L,A_{1}=D_{1},A_{2},A_{3}=D_{2},D_{3},\ldots,D_{k}\}
for
k\geq 3
;
E[\hat{G}_{S}]=\{D_{i}L,D_{i}A_{2}\mid i=1,\ldots,k\}
,
or
E[\hat{G}_{S}]=\{D_{i}L,D_{i}A_{2}\mid i=1,\ldots,k\}\cup\{LA_{2}\}
.
If there are
D_{i}
and
D_{j}
such that
D_{i}
and
D_{j}
are both
\mathcal{C}_{6}
’s.
Then we can obtain
H^{\prime}
by adding to
H
the edges in the cycle

L-D_{i}-A_{2}-D_{j}
. The total number of edges in
D_{i},A_{2},D_{j}
is at least
17
, so they have at least
4
credits, which can pay for the
4
edges
that we add. In the following, we assume that at most one of
D_{i}
is a
\mathcal{C}_{6}
and
the others are
\mathcal{C}_{5}
’s. For each
i
such that
D_{i}
is a
\mathcal{C}_{5}
,
by
Lemma

33
we can find a
3
-matching between
C_{D_{i}}
and

C_{L}\cup C_{A_{2}}
. Further, the
3
-matching has at least
2
edges incident
to two adjacent vertices
u_{i},v_{i}
in
C_{D_{i}}
. We denote the
2
edges
incident to two adjacent vertices
u_{i},v_{i}
as
u_{i}U_{i},v_{i}V_{i}
for
U_{i},V_{i}\in\{L,A_{2}\}
.

If there is a
D_{i}
that is a
\mathcal{C}_{6}
, say,
D_{1}
, then we set
H:=H\cup\{D_{1}L,D_{1}A_{2},D_{2}L,D_{2}A_{2},u_{3}U_{3},v_{3}V_{3}\}\setminus\{u_{3}v_{3}\}
.
We have
|H^{\prime}|=|H|+6-1=|H|+5
and
\mathrm{cr}(H^{\prime})\leq\mathrm{cr}(H)-2-(6+5+5+5)(\frac{1}{4}-\delta)+2\leq\mathrm{cr}(H)-5
.
Thus,
\mathrm{cost}(H^{\prime})\leq\mathrm{cost}(H)
.

Hence, assume that there is no
D_{i}
that is a
\mathcal{C}_{6}
; that is, all
D_{i}
are
\mathcal{C}_{5}
.
If
k\geq 4
, then we set
H:=H\cup\{D_{1}L,D_{1}A_{2},D_{2}L,D_{2}A_{2},u_{3}U_{3},v_{3}V_{3},u_{4}U_{4},v_{4}V_{4}\}\setminus(\{u_{3}v_{3}\}\cup\{u_{4}v_{4}\})
.
We have
|H^{\prime}|=|H|+8-2=|H|+6
and
\mathrm{cr}(H^{\prime})\leq\mathrm{cr}(H)-2-(5+5+5+5+5)(\frac{1}{4}-\delta)+2\leq\mathrm{cr}(H)-6
.
Thus,
\mathrm{cost}(H^{\prime})\leq\mathrm{cost}(H)
.

If
k=3
, we first assume that there is no edge in
G
between
L
and
A_{2}
. Then by
Lemma

34
there is a
4
-matching between
C_{L}
and
C_{D_{1}}\cup C_{D_{2}}\cup C_{D_{3}}
.
Hence, there is a
2
-matching between
C_{L}
and
C_{D_{i}}
for some
i\in\{1,2,3\}
and we can apply
Lemma

35
to find
H^{\prime}
.
Now consider the case where
k=3
and
LA_{2}\in E(G)
.
For each
D_{i}
, if
U_{i}=V_{i}=L
or
\{U_{i},V_{i}\}=\{L,A_{2}\}
, then
we can apply
Lemma

30
.
So we can assume that
U_{i}=V_{i}=A_{2}
, i.e.,
u_{i}A_{2},v_{i}A_{2}\in E(G)
.
If
|E[C_{A_{2}}]|\geq 6
, then
D_{1},D_{2},D_{3},A_{2}
together have at least
21
edges and
4
credits.
In this case, we can set
H:=H\cup\{LD_{3},D_{3}A_{2},LA_{2},u_{1}A_{2},v_{1}A_{2},u_{2}A_{2},v_{2}A_{2}\}\setminus(\{u_{1}v_{1}\}\cup\{u_{2}v_{2}\})
.
We have
|H^{\prime}|=|H|+7-2=|H|+5
and
\mathrm{cr}(H^{\prime})\leq\mathrm{cr}(H)-2-(5+5+5+6)(\frac{1}{4}-\delta)+2\leq\mathrm{cr}(H)-5
.
Thus,
\mathrm{cost}(H^{\prime})\leq\mathrm{cost}(H)
.
Hence, in the remaining case, we have that
A_{2}
is a
\mathcal{C}_{5}
, labeled as
q_{1}-q_{2}-q_{3}-q_{4}-q_{5}-q_{1}
.
Suppose that for some
D_{i}
, the two edges
u_{i}A_{2},v_{i}A_{2}
are incident to adjacent vertices of
C_{A_{2}}
, say,
q_{1}
and
q_{2}
.
Then we can let
H^{\prime}\coloneq H\setminus\{u_{i}v_{i},q_{1}q_{2}\}\cup\{u_{i}A_{2},v_{i}A_{2}\}
.
We have
|H^{\prime}|=|H|
and
\mathrm{cr}(H^{\prime})\leq\mathrm{cr}(H)-(5+5)(\frac{1}{4}-\delta)+2\leq\mathrm{cr}(H)
since we merge two
\mathcal{C}_{5}
s into a bigger 2EC component.
Hence we can make progress as
\mathrm{cost}(H^{\prime})\leq\mathrm{cost}(H)
.
In the remaining case we know that
u_{i}A_{2},v_{i}A_{2}
are not incident to adjacent vertices of
A_{2}
. Hence, w.l.o.g. we assume that
u_{1}q_{1},v_{1}q_{3}\in E(G)
.
Suppose that there are two adjacent vertices in
C_{A_{2}}
, say,
q_{1}
and
q_{2}
, such that
q_{1}D_{i},q_{2}D_{j}\in E(G)
for some
i\neq j
.
Then we can apply
Lemma

30
using the cycle
L-D_{i}-A_{2}-D_{j}
and a
q_{1},q_{2}
-Hamiltonian path in
C_{A_{2}}
.
Therefore, in the following we can assume that for each
D_{i}
, the edges between
D_{i}
and
A_{2}
are incident to either
q_{1}
or
q_{3}
.
By
Lemma

33
there must be a 3-matching between
C_{A_{2}}
and
V(S)\setminus C_{A_{2}}
.
Hence the edge
LA_{2}
must be incident to
q_{2},q_{4}
, or
q_{5}
.
Without loss of generality, we assume the edge is incident to
q_{2}
.
Then we can apply
Lemma

30
with the cycle
L-D_{3}-A_{2}-L
and a
q_{1},q_{2}
-Hamiltonian path in
C_{A_{2}}
.

Case 3:
|F^{*}|=5
.

In this case,
S
contains at least
5
components, where one is
L
. Let
A_{1},A_{2}
,
A_{3}
, and
A_{4}
be some other components. Let
F^{*}=\{LA_{1},A_{1}A_{2},A_{2}A_{3},A_{3}A_{4},A_{4}L\}
. If
A_{1},A_{2},A_{3},A_{4}
together have at least
5

credits, or equivalently, have at least
21
edges, then
H^{\prime}:=H\cup F^{*}

satisfies the lemma since we buy
4
edges, which can be paid by the credits
of
A_{i}
.

Hence, we can assume in the following that all
A_{i}
are
\mathcal{C}_{5}
’s.

Case 3.1:
L
has a neighbor
B
in
S
where
B\notin\{A_{1},\dots,A_{4}\}
.

In this case, there must be a path in
S
from
B
to some
A_{i}
without passing through
L
since
S
is 2-node-connected.
Hence, there is a cycle
F
in
S
containing
L
,
B
, and
k
(
k\geq 3
) nodes in
\{A_{1},A_{2},A_{3},A_{4}\}
.
If
B
is not a
\mathcal{C}_{5}
(
|C_{B}|\geq 6
), then we can set
H^{\prime}:=H\cup F
.
We have
\mathrm{cr}(H^{\prime})=\mathrm{cr}(H)-2-(6+5k)(\frac{1}{4}-\delta)+2\leq\mathrm{cr}(H)-k-2
and
|H^{\prime}|=|H|+k+2
, which satisfies the lemma.
If
B
is a
\mathcal{C}_{5}
, let the vertices of
C_{B}
be labeled as
v_{1}-v_{2}-v_{3}-v_{4}-v_{5}
and
v_{1}L\in E(G)
.
Since
S
is 2-node-connected, there must be a path
P
in
\hat{G}[S]
from
B
to some
A_{i}
without passing through
L
.
If
i=1
or
i=4
, then there is a longer cycle in
\hat{G}[S]
, which contradicts the definition of
F^{*}
.
Without loss of generality, we can assume that
i=3
.
Further,
P
consists of a single edge as otherwise there is a longer cycle, i.e., there is an edge in
G
between
A_{3}
and
B
.
By
Lemma

33
, there must be a
3
-matching between
C_{B}
and the rest of vertices in
V(S)
, which implies that there is an edge
e^{\prime}
in
\bm{\hat{}}{G}[S]
incident to some
v_{j}\in\{v_{2},v_{4},v_{5}\}
.
Let
P^{\prime}
be any path in
\bm{\hat{}}{G}[S]
from
v_{j}
to
\{L,A_{1},A_{2},A_{3},A_{4}\}
and let
v_{k}
be the neighbor of
v_{j}
in
C_{B}
.
We set
H^{\prime}:=H\setminus\{v_{j}v_{k}\}\cup F^{*}\cup P^{\prime}
.
We have
|H^{\prime}|=|H|+|F^{*}|+|P^{\prime}|+1-1=|H|+|P^{\prime}|+5
and
\mathrm{cr}(H^{\prime})\leq\mathrm{cr}(H)-25(\frac{1}{4}-\delta)-(|P^{\prime}|-1)-2+2=\mathrm{cr}(H)-6-|P^{\prime}|+1=\mathrm{cr}(H)-|P^{\prime}|-5
.
Hence
\mathrm{cost}(H^{\prime})\leq\mathrm{cost}(H)
and
H^{\prime}
satisfies the lemma.

Case 3.2:
L
has no other neighbors in
S
than
A_{1},\dots,A_{4}
.

By
Lemma

34
, there must be a
4
-matching between
C_{L}
and
C_{A_{1}}\cup C_{A_{2}}\cup C_{A_{3}}\cup C_{A_{4}}
.
If there is a
2
-matching between
C_{L}
and
C_{A_{i}}
for some
i\in\{1,2,3,4\}
, then we can apply
Lemma

35
to find
H^{\prime}
.
Hence, we can assume that the
4
-matching has one edge incident to
each of
C_{A_{1}},C_{A_{2}}
,
C_{A_{3}}
, and
C_{A_{4}}
.

We first show that
S
only contains
L,A_{1},A_{2},A_{3}
, and
A_{4}
. Suppose
A_{1}
has some neighbor
B
that is not in
\{L,A_{2},A_{3},A_{4}\}
. Then there must be a path
P
in
S
from
B
to
A_{2}
,
A_{3}
, or
A_{4}
without passing through
A_{1}
since
S
is 2VC.
Further, the path cannot pass through
L
since
L
has no other neighbors in
S
other than
A_{1},\dots,A_{4}
.
Since there are edges in
G
between
L
and each of
A_{1},A_{2},A_{3},A_{4}
, no matter where
P
ends at
A_{2}
,
A_{3}
, or
A_{4}
, there must be a cycle in
S
through
L
of length at least
6
. This contradicts the definition of
F^{*}
.
By symmetry,
A_{4}
has no neighbor that is not in
\{L,A_{1},A_{2},A_{3}\}
.
If
A_{2}
has some neighbor
B
that is not in
\{L,A_{1},A_{3},A_{4}\}
, then there must be a path in
S
from
B
to
A_{3}
without passing through
A_{2}
, which implies a cycle of length at least
6
in
S
through
L
.
We conclude that
S
only contains
L,A_{1},A_{2},A_{3}
, and
A_{4}
.

For each
i\in\{1,2,3,4\}
, let the vertices of
C_{A_{i}}
be labeled as
v_{1}^{i}-v_{2}^{i}-v_{3}^{i}-v_{4}^{i}-v_{5}^{i}
.
By previous arguments, we can assume that
v_{1}^{i}L\in E(G)
.
For each
i
, if
v^{i}_{2}
or
v^{i}_{5}
has an incident edge to other nodes
A_{j}
for
j\neq i
, then we can let
H^{\prime}:=H\setminus\{v^{i}_{1}v^{i}_{2}\}\cup\{LA_{i},LA_{j},v^{i}_{2}A_{j}\}
.
We have
|H^{\prime}|=|H|+3-1=|H|+2
,
\mathrm{cr}(H^{\prime})\leq\mathrm{cr}(H)-2-(5+5)(\frac{1}{4}-\delta)+2\leq\mathrm{cr}(H)-2
, and
\mathrm{cost}(H^{\prime})\leq\mathrm{cost}(H)
.
In the following, we assume that
v^{i}_{2}
and
v^{i}_{5}
have no incident edges to other nodes
A_{j}
for
j\neq i
.
By
Lemma

33
, there must be a
3
-matching between
C_{A_{i}}
and
V(S)\setminus V(C_{A_{i}})
.
Thus, there must be a
2
-matching between
\{v^{i}_{3},v^{i}_{4}\}
and
V(S)\setminus\{v^{i}_{3},v^{i}_{4}\}
.
If the
2
-matching is incident to different nodes
A_{j},A_{k}
for
i\neq j\neq k
, i.e.,
v^{i}_{3}A_{j},v^{i}_{4}A_{k}\in E(G)
, then we can set
H^{\prime}:=H\setminus\{v^{i}_{3}v^{i}_{4}\}\cup\{v^{i}_{3}A_{j},v^{i}_{4}A_{k},LA_{j},LA_{k}\}
.
We have
|H^{\prime}|=|H|+4-1=|H|+3
,
\mathrm{cr}(H^{\prime})\leq\mathrm{cr}(H)-2-(5+5+5)(\frac{1}{4}-\delta)+2\leq\mathrm{cr}(H)-3
, and
\mathrm{cost}(H^{\prime})\leq\mathrm{cost}(H)
.
In the remaining case, the
2
-matching is incident to the same node
A_{j}
.
We call an incident edge of
v^{i}_{3}
or
v^{i}_{4}
an outgoing edge if the other vertex is in some other component
A_{j}
for
j\neq i
.
By previous arguments, we can assume without loss of generality that the outgoing edges of
v_{3}^{1},v_{4}^{1}
(resp.
v_{3}^{2},v_{4}^{2}
) can only reach vertices in
\{v_{1}^{2},v_{3}^{2},v_{4}^{2}\}
(resp.
\{v_{1}^{1},v_{3}^{1},v_{4}^{1}\}
).
By
Lemma

33
, there must be a
3
-matching between
C_{A_{1}}\cup C_{A_{2}}
and the remaining vertices in
V(S)
.
Since there are only
2
edges between
C_{L}
and
C_{A_{1}}\cup C_{A_{2}}
, incident to
v_{1}^{1}
and
v_{1}^{2}
, there must be an edge in
G
between
(C_{A_{1}}\cup C_{A_{2}})\setminus\{v_{1}^{1},v_{1}^{2}\}
and
C_{A_{3}}\cup C_{A_{4}}
.
This is a contradiction to the previous cases.
∎

4
Algorithm for
MANY

In this section, we present an algorithm for the
MANY
case. Intuitively, that is the case where

almost all
components of the initial canonical
2
-edge cover
H_{0}
are a
\mathcal{C}_{4}
.
That is, this section is dedicated to proving
Lemma

13
, which we restate for convenience.

See
13

We first recall and introduce some definitions.
Recall the definition of the component graph
\bm{\hat{}}{G}_{H}
for a 2-edge cover
H
.
We say that a component
C
of
H
is a
cut component
if
G\setminus V(C)
is disconnected.
That is, if
c
is the node in
\bm{\hat{}}{G}_{H}
corresponding to
C
, then
\bm{\hat{}}{G}_{H}\setminus\{c\}
is disconnected. We then also say that
c
is a
cut node
. If
C
is a
\mathcal{C}_{4}
, we call
C
a
\mathcal{C}_{4}
-cut component and
c
a
\mathcal{C}_{4}
-cut node.

Recall that we say that a component is
huge
if it contains at least
32
vertices and
large
if it contains at least
9
vertices and is 2EC (cf.
Definition

5
).
Note that a canonical
2
-edge cover always contains a huge component.
Further, note that a complex component of a canonical
2
-edge cover contains at least
11
vertices (cf.
Definition

3
)

In this section, we use the following credit scheme.

See
8

Recall that the
cost
of a
2
-edge cover
H
of
G
is defined as
\mathrm{cost}(H)=|H|+\mathrm{cr}(H)
where
\mathrm{cr}(H)
denotes the sum total of all credits in
H
.
We can bound the cost of a canonical
2
-edge cover in terms of the cost as follows.

See
14

Proof.

We first assign
\frac{1}{4}-\delta
credit to each edge in a
\mathcal{C}_{4}
and
4
credits to each edge not contained in a
\mathcal{C}_{4}
.
Then, we have
\mathrm{cost}(H)=|H|+\mathrm{cr}(H)=m_{4}+m_{r}+m_{4}\cdot(\frac{1}{4}-\delta)+4\cdot m_{r}=(\frac{5}{4}-\delta)\cdot m_{4}+5\cdot m_{r}
, which is the bound in the lemma.

Now we redistribute the credits to obtain a credit assignment as in
Definition

8
.
For each edge in some
\mathcal{C}_{i}
for
4\leq i\leq 8
, we give the credit of the edges to the components.
For each 2EC component that contains at least
9
edges, we collect
2
credits from the edges.
For each complex component, we keep the credits in the bridge, and each block collects
1
credit from its edges.
Since a block only needs
1
credit and an edge in the block has
4
credits, we can assign
1
credit to the complex component.
In this way, we can redistribute the credits without increasing the cost.
∎

Our first goal in this section is to turn
H_{0}
into a
core-square configuration
, which we define next. See
Fig.

2(a)
for an example of a core-square configuration.

See
9

We have the following observation on the structure of a core-square configuration.

Lemma 38
.

Let
H
be a core-square configuration and
C\neq L
be a cut component of
H
.
Let
S^{\prime}
be the unique segment of
\bm{\hat{}}{G}_{H}
such that
C\in S^{\prime}
and
L\notin S^{\prime}
.
Then
S^{\prime}
consists of either
3
or
4

\mathcal{C}_{4}
s and in
S^{\prime}
there is a cycle containing all components of
S^{\prime}
.

Proof.

If
S^{\prime}
consists of only
2

\mathcal{C}_{4}
’s, then by
Lemma

33
, there must be a
3
-matching between
C
and the other
\mathcal{C}_{4}
in
S^{\prime}
, and we can always merge the two
\mathcal{C}_{4}
s into a
\mathcal{C}_{8}
, which contradicts that
H
is canonical.
Hence,
S^{\prime}
consists of either
3
or
4

\mathcal{C}_{4}
’s.
Since
S^{\prime}
is 2-node-connected, there is a cycle in
S^{\prime}
containing all the components of
S^{\prime}
.
∎

The main goal of this section is to compute a bridgeless core-square configuration, summarized in the following lemma.

See
15

Given a bridgeless core-square configuration, we then show how to prove
Lemma

13
.
In the next subsection we first prove
Lemma

13
assuming we are given a bridgeless core-square configuration.
In
Section

4.2
, we prove
Lemma

15
, which is the main contribution of this section.

4.1
Proof of
Lemma

13

We next prove
Lemma

13
assuming
Lemma

15
. That is, we assume we are given a bridgeless core-square configuration
H
and we want to turn it into a
2
-edge connected component that has at most
\left(\frac{5}{4}-\frac{1}{28}\right)\cdot\beta|H_{0}|+5(1-\beta)|H_{0}|+\frac{1}{35}\mathsf{opt}
many edges, where
\beta\in[0,1]
such that the number of edges of
\mathcal{C}_{4}
’s in
H_{0}
is
\beta|H_{0}|
.
For convenience, we let
m_{4}
and
m_{r}
be the number of edges of
H_{0}
contained in a
\mathcal{C}_{4}
or not contained in a
\mathcal{C}_{4}
, respectively. That is,
m_{4}=\beta|H_{0}|
and
m_{r}=(1-\beta)|H_{0}|
. We then show that we can compute a 2ECSS
H
of
G
using at most
(\frac{5}{4}-\frac{1}{28})\cdot m_{4}+5\cdot m_{r}+\frac{1}{35}\mathsf{opt}
many edges.

Proof of
Lemma

13
.

If
H
consists of only a huge component
L
, we are done since there is only one component
L
, which does not contain a bridge. This component has a credit of
2
, and hence
|H|\leq\mathrm{cost}(H)-2=\mathrm{cost}(H_{0})+2-2\leq(\frac{5}{4}-\frac{1}{28})\cdot m_{4}+5\cdot m_{r}
by
Lemma

15
and
Lemma

14
.

Otherwise, let
C_{1},...,C_{k}
be the
\mathcal{C}_{4}
(which are also segments, by the definition of a core-square configuration) that are adjacent to
L
in
\bm{\hat{}}{G}_{H}
.
Furthermore, let
S_{i}
be the inclusion-wise maximal segment of
\bm{\hat{}}{G}_{H}\setminus L
which contains
C_{i}
. Note that these segments
S_{1},...,S_{k}
are all pairwise disjoint by the definition of a core-square configuration.
Furthermore, recall that each
S_{i}
contains either 1, 3, or 4 components that are all
\mathcal{C}_{4}
and that each
S_{i}
is connected to
L
in
\bm{\hat{}}{G}_{H}
.
Consider some segment
S_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq k
.
We glue
V(S_{i})
optimally to
L
, i.e., we contract
V(G)\setminus V(S_{i})
to a single vertex and compute an optimum solution
\mathsf{OPT}_{i}
.
That is
\mathsf{OPT}_{i}
is an optimum solution to
G|(V(G)\setminus V(S_{i}))
.
Note that
\mathsf{OPT}_{i}
can be computed in polynomial time since
|V(S_{i})|\leq 16
.
Let
|S_{i}|
be the number of components of
H
in
S_{i}
and let
F_{i}
be the set of edges of
H
in
S_{i}
.
If
|\mathsf{OPT}_{i}|\leq 5\cdot|S_{i}|-1
, then set
H^{\prime}=(H\setminus F_{i})\cup\mathsf{OPT}_{i}
and observe that
H^{\prime}
is still a bridgeless core-square configuration.
Furthermore,
|H^{\prime}|=|H|+|\mathsf{OPT}_{i}|-4|S_{i}|
and
\mathrm{cr}(H^{\prime})\leq\mathrm{cr}(H)-|S_{i}|\cdot(1-4\delta)
.
Since
\mathsf{OPT}_{i}\leq 5\cdot|S_{i}|-1
, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{cost}(H^{\prime})

\displaystyle=|H^{\prime}|+\mathrm{cr}(H^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq|H|+|\mathsf{OPT}_{i}|-4|S_{i}|+\mathrm{cr}(H)-|S_{i}|\cdot(1-4\delta)

\displaystyle\leq|H|+\mathrm{cr}(H)+5\cdot|S_{i}|-1-4|S_{i}|-|S_{i}|\cdot(1-4\delta)

\displaystyle\leq|H|+\mathrm{cr}(H)+|S_{i}|\cdot 4\delta-1

\displaystyle\leq|H|+\mathrm{cr}(H)+16\cdot\delta-1

\displaystyle\leq|H|+\mathrm{cr}(H)\ .

Hence, assume that we are now given a bridgeless core-square configuration
H^{\prime}
and let again
S_{1},S_{2},...,S_{p}
be the remaining segments of
\bm{\hat{}}{G}_{H^{\prime}}\setminus L
, such that
\mathsf{OPT}_{i}\geq 5\cdot|S_{i}|
.
For each
i\in\{1,\dots,p\}
, let
\ell_{i}
be the number of components of
H^{\prime}
in
S_{i}
.

We first show a different bound on
\mathsf{OPT}
.
Let
G_{i}
be the graph of
G
induced by
V(S_{i})
. Let
E(G_{i})
be the edges of this graph and let
E(L,G_{i})
be the edges in
G
between the vertices of node
L
and the vertices of
G_{i}
. Note that
(E(G_{i})\cup E(L,G_{i}))\cap(E(G_{j})\cup E(L,G_{j}))=\emptyset
for
i\neq j
, by the definition of a core-square configuration. We have

\mathsf{opt}\geq\sum_{i=1}^{p}|\mathsf{OPT}(G)\cap(E(G_{i})\cup E(L,G_{i}))|\geq\sum_{i=1}^{k}5\ell_{i}\ .

We now show that we can turn
H^{\prime}
into a single 2EC component
H^{\prime\prime}
such that
\mathrm{cost}(H^{\prime\prime})\leq\mathrm{cost}(H)+4\delta\sum_{i=1}^{p}\ell_{i}
.
For each
i\in\{1,\dots,p\}
, let
C_{i}
be the cut component separating
L
from the remaining components of
S_{i}
.
We now find edge sets
X_{i}\subseteq E\setminus E(H^{\prime})
and
Y_{i}\subseteq E(H^{\prime})
such that
H^{\prime\prime}=\Big(H^{\prime}\cup\bigcup_{1\leq i\leq p}X_{i}\Big)\setminus\Big(\bigcup_{1\leq i\leq p}\{Y_{i}\}\Big)
is 2EC and
|X_{i}|-|Y_{i}|\leq|S_{i}|
, which implies that
\mathrm{cost}(H^{\prime\prime})\leq\mathrm{cost}(H^{\prime})+4\delta\sum_{i=1}^{p}\ell_{i}\leq\mathrm{cost}(H)+4\delta\sum_{i=1}^{p}\ell_{i}
.
To find these edge sets, we iteratively appply
Lemma

32
.
First, using
Lemma

32
we find 2 edges incident to
L
and
C_{i}
such that those edges are incident to distinct vertices
u_{i},v_{i}\in V(C_{i})
such that there is a Hamiltonian
u_{i},v_{i}
-path in
G[V(C_{i})]
. The two edges between
L
and
C_{i}
are added to
X_{i}
, while the edge
u_{i}v_{i}
is added to
Y_{i}
.
Then, for some other
C_{i}^{\prime}
in
S_{i}
distinct from
C_{i}
we apply
Lemma

32
to find a cycle
K
through
C_{i}^{\prime}
and
C_{i}
to distinct vertices
u_{i}^{\prime},v_{i}^{\prime}\in V(C_{i}^{\prime})
such that there is a Hamiltonian
u_{i}^{\prime},v_{i}^{\prime}
-path in
G[V(C_{i}^{\prime})]
. The edges of
K
are added to
X_{i}
, while the edge
u_{i}^{\prime}v_{i}^{\prime}
is added to
Y_{i}
.
If
K
involves all nodes of
S_{i}
, we are done.
Otherwise, we repeat this process where we temporarily shrink all nodes involved with
K
to a single node and find a cycle
K^{\prime}
through this shrunk node and a not yet visited component
C_{i}^{\prime\prime}
from
S_{i}
, where
K^{\prime}
is incident to distinct vertices
u_{i}^{\prime\prime},v_{i}^{\prime\prime}\in V(C_{i}^{\prime\prime})
such that there is a Hamiltonian
u_{i}^{\prime\prime},v_{i}^{\prime\prime}
-path in
G[V(C_{i}^{\prime\prime})]
, by
Lemma

32
. Again, the edges of
K^{\prime\prime}
are added to
X_{i}
and the edge
u_{i}^{\prime\prime}v_{i}^{\prime\prime}
is added to
Y_{i}
.
This process stops after at most
|S_{i}|
many steps and we have that
|X_{i}|-|Y_{i}|\leq|S_{i}|
.
Furthermore, observe that
H^{\prime\prime}=\Big(H^{\prime}\cup\bigcup_{1\leq i\leq p}X_{i}\Big)\setminus\Big(\bigcup_{1\leq i\leq p}\{Y_{i}\}\Big)
is 2EC. So
\mathrm{cost}(H^{\prime\prime})-\mathrm{cost}(H^{\prime})=|H^{\prime\prime}|-|H^{\prime}|+\mathrm{cr}(H^{\prime\prime})-\mathrm{cr}(H^{\prime})\leq\sum_{i=1}^{p}\ell_{i}-\sum_{i=1}^{p}(1-4\delta)\cdot\ell_{i}\leq 4\delta\sum_{i=1}^{p}\ell_{i}
.

Let
\sum_{i=1}^{p}\ell_{i}=\ell^{*}
.
Combining
Lemma

15
, we have

\mathrm{cost}(H^{\prime\prime})\leq\mathrm{cost}(H_{0})+2+4\delta\cdot\ell^{*}
, where
H_{0}
is the initial canonical
2
-edge cover.
Since
H^{\prime\prime}
consists of a single 2EC component, which has a credit of 2, we have

|H^{\prime\prime}|\leq\mathrm{cost}(H_{0})+4\delta\cdot\ell^{*}\leq(\frac{5}{4}-\delta)\cdot m_{4}+5\cdot m_{r}+4\delta\cdot\ell^{*}
by
Lemma

14
.

Using
\mathsf{opt}\geq\sum_{i=1}^{k}5\ell_{i}=5\ell^{*}
and by setting
\delta=\frac{1}{28}
, we can bound

\displaystyle|H^{\prime\prime}|

\displaystyle\leq\Big(\frac{5}{4}-\delta\Big)\cdot m_{4}+5\cdot m_{r}+4\delta\cdot\ell^{*}

\displaystyle\leq\Big(\frac{5}{4}-\delta\Big)\cdot m_{4}+5\cdot m_{r}+\frac{4}{5}\delta\mathsf{opt}

\displaystyle=\Big(\frac{5}{4}-\frac{1}{28}\Big)\cdot m_{4}+5\cdot m_{r}+\frac{4}{5}\cdot\frac{1}{28}\mathsf{opt}

\displaystyle=\Big(\frac{5}{4}-\frac{1}{28}\Big)\cdot m_{4}+5\cdot m_{r}+\frac{1}{35}\mathsf{opt}\ ,

which proves the statement.
∎

4.2
Computing a Bridgeless Core-Square Configuration (Proof of
Lemma

15
)

To prove
Lemma

15
, we start with a canonical
2
-edge cover
H
and step-by-step decrease the number of components while always maintaining that the new canonical
2
-edge cover
H^{\prime}
satisfies
\mathrm{cost}(H^{\prime})\leq\mathrm{cost}(H)
, except for at most one step in which we might increase the cost by at most
2
.
From now on we always assume that we are given some canonical
2
-edge cover
H
.

Throughout this section we focus on components of
H
belonging to the same segment
S
of
\bm{\hat{}}{G}_{H}
, i.e., the same
2
-node connected part
S
of the component graph
\bm{\hat{}}{G}_{H}
.
For such components
C
of
S
, we will usually apply the
3
-Matching
Lemma

4
.
If
C
is not a cut node, then it is clear that all edges implied by the
3
-Matching
Lemma

4
are going to
S
.
If
C
is a cut note, this is not immediate. However, we can divide
V
into two sets
V_{1}
and
V_{2}
such that
V(S)\setminus V(C)\subseteq V_{1}
,
V(C)\subseteq V_{2}
and the only edges between
V_{1}
and
V_{2}
are incident to
C
. Then, applying the
3
-Matching
Lemma

4
to
(V_{1},V_{2})
implies that these edges are incident to
V(C)
and
S
.
We will always make use of this fact implicitly without explicitly giving this partition.

Useful definitions and lemmas.

Before we proceed, we first define certain situations in which we can turn
H
into a canonical
2
-edge cover
H^{\prime}
such that
H^{\prime}
contains fewer components than
H
and
\mathrm{cost}(H^{\prime})\leq\mathrm{cost}(H)
.

Definition 12

(covering a bridge)
.

Let
H
be a canonical
2
-edge cover of a structured graph
G
that contains a complex
component
C
. Let
X\subseteq E(G)\setminus E(H)
be such that
X
is a cycle in the
component graph
\bm{\hat{}}{G}_{H}
incident to
C
at vertices
u_{1},u_{2}\in V(C)
such that the
path from
u_{1}
to
u_{2}
in
C
contains at least one bridge
e\in E(C)
.
We say that
X

covers
the bridge
e
.

Note that
X
can also be a cycle containing only one edge if
e
connects different vertices of
V(C)
.

Definition 13

(shortcut)
.

Let
X\subseteq E(G)\setminus E(H)
such that
X=X_{1}\cup X_{2}
with
X_{1}\cap X_{2}=\emptyset
,
where
X_{1}
is a simple cycle in the component graph
\bm{\hat{}}{G}_{H}
.
X_{2}
is either a possibly empty simple path in the
component graph
\bm{\hat{}}{G}_{H}
such that the endpoints of
X_{2}
intersect with
X_{1}
and all interior
nodes of
X_{2}
are distinct from
X_{1}
, or
X_{2}
is a simple cycle that intersects
X_{1}
at precisely one node.
Let
C
be a component incident to
X
that is a
\mathcal{C}_{4}
.
We say that
C
is
shortcut
w.r.t.
X
if there are two edges
e_{1},e_{2}\in X
with
e_{1}=u_{1}v_{1}
and
e_{2}=u_{2}v_{2}
,
u_{1},u_{2}\in V(C)
and
v_{1},v_{2}\in V(G)\setminus V(C)
such that
u_{1}u_{2}\in E(C)\cap H
, i.e.,
u_{1}
and
u_{2}
are adjacent in
H
. We also say that
e=u_{1}u_{2}\in E(H)
is
shortcut
.

For each
\mathcal{C}_{4}
component
C
incident to
X
that is shortcut, we let
e(X,C)
be the edge that is shortcut.
There might be more than one edge of
C
that is shortcut w.r.t.
X
. In this case we pick an arbitrary such edge as
e(X,C)
.
We define
F(X)\coloneqq\{e(X,C)\mid C\text{ is shortcut w.r.t.\ $X$}\}
.

Figure 4
:
An example of a double
(5,1)
-merge. Edges in
X_{1}
are orange and edges in
X_{2}
are blue. The red edge is shortcut.

Note that in the above definition
X_{2}
can also be empty, in which case
X
is just a simple cycle in the component graph.

Using this definition of shortcut, we now define a single
(a,b)
-merge and a double
(a,b)
-merge.

Definition 14

(single
(a,b)
-merge)
.

Let
H
be a canonical
2
-edge cover of a structured graph
G
.
For
a,b\geq 0
, a single
(a,b)
-merge w.r.t.
H
is a set of edges
X\subseteq E(G)\setminus E(H)
such that

•

X
is a cycle in the component graph
\bm{\hat{}}{G}_{H}
,

•

if
X
is incident to a complex component
C^{\prime}
, then
X
is either incident to a vertex contained in a block of
C^{\prime}
or incident to at least 2 distinct vertices of
C^{\prime}
,

•

a
is the number of distinct components
C
in
X
, and

•

b
is the number of distinct
\mathcal{C}_{4}
components
C
in
X
such that
X
does not shortcut
C
.

Definition 15

(double
(a,b)
-merge)
.

Let
H
be a canonical
2
-edge cover of a structured graph
G
.
For
a,b\geq 0
, a double
(a,b)
-merge w.r.t.
H
is a set of edges
X\subseteq E(G)\setminus E(H)
such that

•

X\subseteq E(G)\setminus E(H)
such that
X=X_{1}\cup X_{2}
with
X_{1}\cap X_{2}=\emptyset
, where
X_{1}
is a simple cycle in the component graph
\bm{\hat{}}{G}_{H}
and
X_{2}
is a simple path in the component graph
\bm{\hat{}}{G}_{H}
such that the endpoints of
X_{2}
intersect with
X_{1}
and all interior nodes of
X_{2}
are distinct from
X_{1}
,

•

if
X
is incident to a complex component
C^{\prime}
, then
X
is either incident to a vertex contained in a block of
C^{\prime}
or incident to at least 2 distinct vertices of
C^{\prime}
,

•

a
is the number of distinct components
C
in
X
, and

•

b
is the number of distinct
\mathcal{C}_{4}
components
C
in
X
such that
X
does not shortcut
C
.

An example of a double
(5,1)
-merge is given in
Figure

4
.
We further define
short heavy cycles
.

Definition 16

(short heavy cycle)
.

Let
H
be a canonical
2
-edge cover of a structured graph
G
.
A set of edges
X\subseteq E(G)\setminus E(H)
is a short heavy cycle if

•

X
is a simple cycle in the component graph of length at most
6
in
\bm{\hat{}}{G}_{H}
, and

•

X
is incident to at least one component
C
that is a
\mathcal{C}_{i}
,
5\leq i\leq 8
or complex. If
C
is complex then
X
covers at least one bridge of
C
.

The following lemma will be useful throughout this section.

Lemma 39
.

Let
H
be a canonical
2
-edge cover of a structured graph
G
and let
X\subseteq E(G)\setminus E(H)
such that one of the following conditions is satisfied:

•

X
is a single
(k,j)
-merge for some
k\geq 3
and
j\leq 2
such that
k-j\geq 3
,

•

X
is a double
(k,j)
-merge for some
k\geq 5
and
