j\leq 3
such that
k-j\geq 4
, or

•

X
is a short heavy cycle.

Then
(H\cup X)\setminus F(X)
is a canonical
2
-edge cover with fewer components than
H
and
\mathrm{cost}((H\cup X)\setminus F(X))\leq\mathrm{cost}(H)
.

Proof.

Let
H^{\prime}=(H\cup X)\setminus F(X)
.
We first prove that
H^{\prime}
is a canonical
2
-edge cover.
This is clearly true for short heavy cycles, since we only add a cycle in the component graph and do not remove any edges, i.e.,
F(X)=\emptyset
in this case.
If
X
is a single or double merge, we have the same if we do not remove an edge, i.e., is
F(X)=\emptyset
.
If we remove an edge, we only remove it from a
\mathcal{C}_{4}
and we remove at most one edge from each component.
Let
C
be a component where we remove the edge
e=uv
. Note that there is still a Hamiltonian Path from
u
to
v
within
C
in
H^{\prime}
. Further, there is a connection from
u
to
v
in
H^{\prime}
using no edges within
C
.
Since we have this for every component for which we removed an edge, this shows that the newly created block or 2EC component in
H^{\prime}
is indeed 2EC.
Further, observe that we have not created any new bridges.

This shows that
H^{\prime}
is a canonical
2
-edge cover.
It remains to prove that
\mathrm{cost}(H^{\prime})\leq\mathrm{cost}(H)
.
Note that
|H^{\prime}|\leq|H|+|X|-|F(X)|
.
Let
C_{1},...,C_{p_{c}}
be the set of complex components incident to
X
and assume that
X
contains
p_{\ell}
2EC components that are large or a
\mathcal{C}_{i}
for some
5\leq i\leq 8
and
p_{4}
components that are a
\mathcal{C}_{4}
. Further, for
1\leq i\leq p_{c}
let
bl_{i}
be the number of blocks incident to
X
and
br_{i}
be the number of bridges in
C_{i}
covered by
X
.
We have that

\mathrm{cr}(H^{\prime})\leq\mathrm{cr}(H)+2-\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{p_{c}}(bl_{i}+4\cdot br_{i}+1)+2p_{\ell}+(1-4\delta)\cdot p_{4}\Big)\ .

The
+2
comes from the fact that in
H^{\prime}
, we either create a new large component (which receives a credit of
2
) or a new block within a new complex component (where the block and the component each receive a credit of
1
).
The number of credits that are removed in
H^{\prime}
compared to
H
are all credits inside components, blocks and bridges in
H
that are now in the newly formed block or 2EC component in
H^{\prime}
. These include all blocks incident to
X
or bridges covered by
X
, and all 2EC components incident to
X
. Using the credit assignment scheme, we observe that the above inequality on
\mathrm{cr}(H^{\prime})
holds.

If
X
is a short heavy cycle, then
|X|\leq 6
, and either
X
is incident to a component that is a
\mathcal{C}_{i}
for some
5\leq i\leq 8
which has at least
20
credits, or there is some complex component incident to
X
, say
C_{1}
, and
br_{1}\geq 1
and
bl_{1}+4\cdot br_{1}+1\geq 5
.
In either case, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{cr}(H^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{cr}(H)+2-\Big(5+(|X|-1)\cdot(1-4\delta)\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{cr}(H)-|X|\ ,

since
|X|\leq 6
and the worst-case is if there is exactly one complex component and
5
components that are
\mathcal{C}_{4}
and
X
covers only one bridge of the complex component.
Hence, we have
\mathrm{cost}(H^{\prime})\leq\mathrm{cost}(H)
.

Next, assume
X
is a single
(k,j)
-merge for some
k\geq 3
and
j\leq 2
such that
k-j\geq 3
or a double
(k,j)
-merge for some
k\geq 5
and
j\leq 2
such that
k-j\geq 4
.
Then
|F(X)|\geq p_{4}-j\geq 0
.
Note that for each
i\in\{1,...,p_{c}\}
we have
bl_{i}+4\cdot br_{i}+1\geq 2
in this case since by the definition of a single or double merge,
X
must be incident to either a block or it covers at least one bridge of each complex component.
Further recall that
k=p_{c}+p_{\ell}+p_{4}
and that
|X|\in\{k,k+1\}
.
We have

\displaystyle\mathrm{cost}(H^{\prime})

\displaystyle=|H^{\prime}|+\mathrm{cr}(H^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq|H|+|X|-|F(X)|+\mathrm{cr}(H)+2-\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{p_{c}}(bl_{i}+4\cdot br_{i}+1)+2p_{\ell}+(1-4\delta)\cdot p_{4}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq|H|+\mathrm{cr}(H)+|X|+2-\Big(2\cdot(p_{c}+p_{\ell})+(1-4\delta)\cdot p_{4}+|F(X)|\Big)

\displaystyle\leq|H|+\mathrm{cr}(H)+|X|+2-\Big(2\cdot(p_{c}+p_{\ell})+(1-4\delta)\cdot p_{4}+p_{4}-j\Big)

\displaystyle\leq|H|+\mathrm{cr}(H)+|X|+2-\Big(2\cdot(p_{c}+p_{\ell})+(2-4\delta)\cdot p_{4}-j\Big)

\displaystyle\leq|H|+\mathrm{cr}(H)+|X|+2-\Big((2-4\delta)\cdot(p_{c}+p_{\ell}+p_{4})-j\Big)

\displaystyle\leq|H|+\mathrm{cr}(H)+|X|+2+j-(2-4\delta)\cdot k\ ,

where we used all inequalities from above.

Consider first the case that
X
is a single merge.
Then we have
|X|=k\geq 3
,
j\leq 2
and
k-j\geq 3
.
Therefore, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathrm{cost}(H^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq|H|+\mathrm{cr}(H)+|X|+2+j-(2-4\delta)\cdot k

\displaystyle=|H|+\mathrm{cr}(H)+k+2+j-(2-4\delta)\cdot k

\displaystyle=|H|+\mathrm{cr}(H)+2+j-(1-4\delta)\cdot k

\displaystyle\leq|H|+\mathrm{cr}(H)+2+2-(1-4\delta)\cdot 5

\displaystyle\leq|H|+\mathrm{cr}(H)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{cost}(H)\ .

If
X
is a double merge, then we have
|X|=k+1\geq 6
,
j\leq 2
and
k-j\geq 4
.
Therefore, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathrm{cost}(H^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq|H|+\mathrm{cr}(H)+|X|+2+j-(2-4\delta)\cdot k

\displaystyle=|H|+\mathrm{cr}(H)+k+1+2+j-(2-4\delta)\cdot k

\displaystyle=|H|+\mathrm{cr}(H)+3+j-(1-4\delta)\cdot k

\displaystyle\leq|H|+\mathrm{cr}(H)+3+3-(1-4\delta)\cdot 7

\displaystyle\leq|H|+\mathrm{cr}(H)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{cost}(H)\ .

Note that all inequalities hold for
\delta\leq\frac{1}{28}
.
This finishes the proof of the lemma.
∎

Furthermore, we will sometimes use the following two lemmas.

Lemma 40
.

Let
H
be a canonical
2
-edge cover of a structured graph
G
and let
C
be a component of
H
contained in a segment
S
that consists of exactly three or four nodes that are all large or a
\mathcal{C}_{4}
.
Then there is a single
(k,j)
-merge containing
C
in
S
not shortcutting
C
such that
k-j\geq 2
and
k\in\{3,4\}
.

Proof.

We first consider the case that
S
contains exactly
3
nodes, i.e.,
S
contains the three components
C
,
C_{1}
, and
C_{2}
.
We assume that
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
are
\mathcal{C}_{4}
.
It is easy to see how to adapt to the case that one (or both) of
C_{1}
or
C_{2}
are large.
Let
v_{1}-v_{2}-v_{3}-v_{4}-v_{1}
be the cycle of
C_{1}
and
u_{1}-u_{2}-u_{3}-u_{4}-u_{1}
be the cycle of
C_{2}
.
We show that there must exist a single
(3,1)
-merge in
S
that does not shortcut
C
.

By the
3
-Matching
Lemma

4
and since
S
is
2
-node connected, there must be a pair of edges
e_{1}
and
e_{2}
incident to adjacent vertices of
C_{1}
. W.l.o.g. assume that
e_{1}
is incident to
v_{1}
and
C
and
e_{2}
is incident to
v_{2}
and
C_{2}
. Further, assume that
e_{2}
is incident to
u_{1}
at
C_{2}
.

C_{2}
must also be incident to an edge
f
incident to
C
, as otherwise
S
is not
2
-node connected.
If
f
is incident to either
u_{2}
or
u_{4}
, then
e_{1},e_{2}
and
f
together form the desired single
(3,1)
-merge that does not shortcut
C
.
Hence, assume that w.l.o.g.
f
is incident to
u_{3}
.

Again, by the
3
-Matching
Lemma

4
, either
u_{2}
or
u_{4}
is incident to an edge
g
to either
C_{1}
or
C
.
If
g
is incident to
C
, we are clearly done.
Hence, assume
g
is incident to
C_{1}
.
If
g
is incident to either
v_{1}
or
v_{3}
, then we can merge
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
into a
\mathcal{C}_{8}
, a contradiction.
If
g
is incident to either
v_{2}
or
v_{4}
, then
e_{1},g
, and
f
together form the desired
(3,1)
-merge that does not shortcut
C
. This finishes the case that
S
contains exactly
3
nodes.

Let us now assume that
S
contains exactly
4
nodes, i.e., the components
C_{1},C_{2},C_{3}
, and
C
.
We show that there must exist a single
(k,1)
-merge or a single
(4,2)
merge in
S
that does not shortcut
C
, for some
k\in\{3,4\}
.
This then implies the lemma statement.

We assume that
C_{1},C_{2}
, and
C_{3}
are
\mathcal{C}_{4}
.
It is easy to see how to adapt to the case that a subset of them is large.
We take the same labeling for
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
and let
C_{3}
be the cycle
w_{1}-w_{2}-w_{3}-w_{4}-w_{1}
.
W.l.o.g.
C_{1}
is adjacent to
C
.
By the
3
-Matching
Lemma

4
and since
S
is
2
-node connected, there must be a pair of edges
e_{1}
and
e_{2}
incident to adjacent vertices of
C_{1}
.
W.l.o.g. assume that
e_{1}
is incident to
v_{1}
and
C
and
e_{2}
is incident to
v_{2}
and
C_{2}
. Further, assume that
e_{2}
is incident to
u_{1}
at
C_{2}
.

Assume first that
C_{2}
is adjacent to
C
by an edge
f
.
If
f
is incident to either
u_{2}
or
u_{4}
, we are done.
Hence, assume w.l.o.g. that
f
is incident to
u_{3}
.
By the
3
-Matching
Lemma

4
, there must be another edge
g
incident to either
u_{2}
or
u_{4}
.
If
g
is incident to
C_{1}
, then either we can merge
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
into a
\mathcal{C}_{8}
(if
g
is incident to either
v_{1}
or
v_{3}
) or we have a single
(3,1)
-merge using
f,g
and
e_{1}
(if
g
is incident to either
v_{2}
or
v_{4}
).
If
g
is incident to
C
, then
e_{1},e_{2}
and
g
also form a single
(3,1)
-merge.
Hence,
g
is incident to
C_{3}
at vertex
w_{1}
.
By the
3
-Matching
Lemma

4
, there must be an edge
h
incident to either
w_{2}
or
w_{4}
.
If
h
is incident to
C
, then
h,g
and
f
form a single
(3,1)
-merge. If
h
is incident to
C_{1}
, then
f,g,h
and
e_{1}
form a single
(4,2)
-merge.
Hence,
h
is incident to
C_{2}
.
If
h
is incident to
u_{1}
or
u_{3}
, then we can merge
C_{2}
and
C_{3}
to a single
\mathcal{C}_{8}
, a contradiction.
Hence,
h
is incident to
u_{2}
or
u_{4}
(w.l.o.g.
u_{2}
).
Now there has to be an edge
i
incident to
C_{3}
and incident to some other node than
C_{2}
.
If
i
is incident to
C
, then
f,i
and either
g
or
h
is a single
(3,1)
-merge.
If
i
is incident to
C_{1}
, then
f,i,e_{1}
and either
g
or
h
is a single
(4,2)
-merge.

Next, assume that
C_{2}
is not adjacent to
C
.

C_{3}
then must be adjacent to
C
by an edge
f
, otherwise
S
is not
2
-node connected.
Furthermore,
C_{1}
must be adjacent to
C_{3}
by an edge
g
.
If
g
is incident to either
u_{2}
or
u_{3}
, then
e_{1},e_{2},f
and
g
form a single
(4,2)
-merge.
Hence, assume that
g
is incident to, w.l.o.g.
u_{1}
. We assume that neither
u_{2}
nor
u_{4}
is adjacent to
C_{3}
.
However, by the
3
-Matching
Lemma

4
, either
u_{2}
or
u_{4}
must have an edge, say
h
, to another component, which must be
C_{1}
.
If
h
is incident to
v_{1}
or
v_{3}
, then we can merge
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
into one
\mathcal{C}_{8}
, a contradiction.
Hence,
h
is incident to, say
v_{2}
.
Now there is a single
(4,2)
-merge using
e_{1},h,g
and
f
.
This finishes the case and the proof.
∎

Lemma 41
.

Let
H
be a canonical
2
-edge cover of a structured graph
G
and let
C
be a component of
H
contained in two distinct segments
S,S^{\prime}
such that
S
consists of exactly three or four nodes that are all large or a
\mathcal{C}_{4}
.
Furthermore, there is a single
(k,j)
-merge
X^{\prime}
containing
C
in
S^{\prime}
such that
k-j\geq 2
and
k\in\{3,4\}
.
Then there is also a double
(k,j)
-merge containing
C
for some
k\geq 5
and
k-j\geq 4
.

Proof.

By
Lemma

40
, there is a single
(k,j)
-merge
X
containing
C
in
S
not shortcutting
C
such that
k-j\geq 2
and
k\in\{3,4\}
.
If the single merge
X^{\prime}
in
S^{\prime}
does not shortcut
C
(or if
C
is large), then
C
is not shortcut in both single merges
X
and
X^{\prime}
and hence
X\cup X^{\prime}
is a double
(k,j)
-merge containing
C
for some
k\geq 5
and
k-j\geq 4
, as desired.
If
X^{\prime}
does shortcut
C
, then in
X\cup X^{\prime}
there are also at least
k-j
components that are shortcut or large.
Hence
X\cup X^{\prime}
is a double
(k,j)
-merge containing
C
for some
k\geq 5
and
k-j\geq 4
, as desired.
∎

Main Lemmas.

The main technical contribution is the following lemma. We postpone its proof, which is given in
Section

4.2.1
.

See
16

For the remainder we assume that we have applied the above lemma exhaustively.
Hence, from now on we assume that every segment
S
with at least
5
nodes does not contain a huge component.

Next, we show that if there is a segment that contains a
\mathcal{C}_{i}
for
5\leq i\leq 8
and at most
i
components, then we can turn
H
into a canonical
2
-edge cover
H^{\prime}
with fewer components than
H
and
\mathrm{cost}(H^{\prime})\leq\mathrm{cost}(H)
.

Lemma 42
.

Given a canonical
2
-edge cover
H
of a structured graph
G
such that the component graph
\bm{\hat{}}{G}_{H}
has a segment that contains a
\mathcal{C}_{i}
for
5\leq i\leq 8
and at most
i
components.
Then we can compute a canonical
2
-edge cover
H^{\prime}
with fewer components than
H
and
\mathrm{cost}(H^{\prime})\leq\mathrm{cost}(H)
in polynomial time.

Proof.

Since a
\mathcal{C}_{i}
has
4i
credits for
5\leq i\leq 8
, we can redistribute the credits of the
\mathcal{C}_{i}
such that each component of the segment has
2
credits.
Then we iteratively perform the following steps until the segment has only one component.
We find any cycle
F
of length at least
3
in the segment and let
H^{\prime}\coloneq H\cup F
.
We have
|H^{\prime}|=|H|+|F|
and
\mathrm{cr}(H^{\prime})\leq\mathrm{cr}(H)-2|F|+2
.
Since
|F|\geq 2
, we have
\mathrm{cost}(H^{\prime})\leq\mathrm{cost}(H)+2-|F|\leq\mathrm{cost}(H)
. This can be done in polynomial time.
∎

Hence, from now on we assume that we have exhaustively applied the above lemma.

Next, we show that if there is a segment
S
with at most
4
nodes containing two components that are large or complex, then we can turn
H
into a canonical
2
-edge cover
H^{\prime}
with fewer components than
H
and
\mathrm{cost}(H^{\prime})\leq\mathrm{cost}(H)
.

Lemma 43
.

Given a canonical
2
-edge cover
H
of a structured graph
G
such that the component graph
\bm{\hat{}}{G}_{H}
contains a segment
S
with at most
4
components and
S
contains two components that are large or a complex. Then we can compute a canonical
2
-edge cover
H^{\prime}
with fewer components than
H
and
\mathrm{cost}(H^{\prime})\leq\mathrm{cost}(H)
in polynomial time.

Proof.

We first apply
Lemma

42
exhaustively, so that we can assume that
S
does not contain a
\mathcal{C}_{i}
for
5\leq i\leq 8
.
Now we can assume that
S
contains only large, complex, and
\mathcal{C}_{4}
components.

We will iteratively find a cycle
F
in
S
and merge the components together until only one component remains.
We first argue that complex components can be treated equivalently as large components as follows.
If the cycle
F
covers some bridge of a complex component, then we gain
4
credits from the bridge, which is much more than we gain from a large component (only
2
credits).
If the cycle
F
does not cover any bridge of a complex component, then the two edges in
F
incident to the complex component are incident to the same block of the complex component.
Hence, if
F
is incident to a block node
B
of some complex component, the block has a credit of one and after adding
F
to
H
, the components incident to
F
are part of
B
and again need a credit of one.

Therefore, we can first check if there is a cycle
F
that covers some bridge of a complex component and add if there is such a cycle.
If there is no such cycle, all edges incident to a complex component are only incident to one block of it and hence we can effectively treat the complex component as a large component.
In the following, we assume that
S
consists only of large components and
\mathcal{C}_{4}
components.

If there is no
\mathcal{C}_{4}
, then each component has at least
2
credits and we can iteratively find a cycle in
S
and merge the components together until only one component remains, as we do in the proof of
Lemma

42
.

Let the components of
S
be
C_{1},C_{2},C_{3},C_{4}
.
Let
C_{1}
be a
\mathcal{C}_{4}
, with cycle
v_{1}-v_{2}-v_{3}-v_{4}-v_{1}
.
By the
3
-Matching
Lemma

4
and since
S
is
2
-node connected, there must be two edges
e_{1}
and
e_{2}
such that, w.l.o.g.,
e_{1}
is incident to
v_{1}
and
C_{2}
and
e_{2}
is incident to
v_{2}
and
C_{3}
.

Assume there exists a path
P
in
S

(of length 1 or 2) starting in
C_{2}
, ending in
C_{3}
, not containing
C_{2}
but containing
C_{4}
(if
S
has 4 components).
Then setting
H^{\prime}=(H\cup P\cup\{e_{1},e_{1}\})\setminus\{v_{1}v_{2}\}
is a canonical
2
-edge cover where the components all components of
S
are now in a single 2EC component of
H^{\prime}
.
Hence,
H^{\prime}
has fewer components than
H
.
Furthermore,
\mathrm{cost}(H^{\prime})\leq\mathrm{cost}(H^{\prime})
since
|H^{\prime}|\leq|H|+|S|-1
and
\mathrm{cr}(H^{\prime})\leq\mathrm{cr}(H)-4-(|S|-2)(1-4\delta)+2\leq\mathrm{cr}(H)-|S|+1
, since
|S|\in\{3,4\}
and
S
contains at least 2 large components.

Note that such a path
P
as above exists if either
C_{1}
is the only
\mathcal{C}_{4}
in
S
or there exist a
\mathcal{C}_{4}
in
S
that is only adjacent to two components in
S
. Hence, in those cases we are done.
Therefore, assume there is no such path for any
\mathcal{C}_{4}
in
S
.
This implies that
S
contains exactly two
\mathcal{C}_{4}
, say
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
and two large components, say
C_{3}
and
C_{4}
. Let the cycle on
C_{2}
be
u_{1}-u_{2}-u_{3}-u_{4}-u_{1}
.
Furthermore, in
S
the two
\mathcal{C}_{4}
’s
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
are adjacent to all components of
S
and
C_{3}
and
C_{4}
are only adjacent to the components
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
in
S
. Otherwise, we have a path
P
as above.
Again, by the
3
-Matching
Lemma

4
and since
S
is
2
-node connected, there must be two edges
e_{1}
and
e_{2}
such that, w.l.o.g.,
e_{1}
is incident to
v_{1}
and
C_{2}
and
e_{2}
is incident to
v_{2}
and
C_{3}
. Note that
e_{1}
can not be incident to
C_{4}
, as then again we can find the desired path
P
.
Assume that
e_{1}
is incident to
u_{1}
on
C_{2}
.
There must be an edge
f
incident to
u_{2}
or
u_{4}
and some other component of
S
, by the 3-Matching
Lemma

4
. Assume w.l.o.g.
f
is incident to
u_{2}
. We make a case distinction on which component
f
is incident to.
If
f
is incident to
C_{3}
, then also we can find a good cycle:
Then
H^{\prime}=(H\cup\{e_{1},e_{2},f\})\setminus\{v_{1}v_{2},u_{1}u_{2}\}
is a canonical 2-edge cover that has fewer components than
H
and it can be checked similarly to the first case that
\mathrm{cost}(H^{\prime})\leq\mathrm{cost}(H)
.
Assume
f
is incident to
C_{4}
.
There must be an edge
g
from
C_{3}
to
C_{2}
and
h
from
C_{4}
to
C_{1}
, since
S
is
2
-node connected and
C_{3}
and
C_{4}
are not connected to each other in
S
, by assumption.
Now set
H^{\prime}=(H\cup\{e_{1},e_{2},f,g,h\})\setminus\{v_{1}v_{2},u_{1}u_{2}\}
.
Observe that
H^{\prime}
is a canonical
2
-edge cover with fewer components than
H
.
Further,
\mathrm{cost}(H^{\prime})\leq\mathrm{cost}(H^{\prime})
since
|H^{\prime}|\leq|H|+3
and
\mathrm{cr}(H^{\prime})\leq\mathrm{cr}(H)-4-2-2(1-4\delta)+2\leq\mathrm{cr}(H)-3
.
Hence, we assume that
f
is incident to
C_{1}
. Note that
f
cannot be incident to
v_{2}
or

v_{4}
as then
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
can be merged into a single
\mathcal{C}_{8}
, a contradiction to
H
being
canonical.
Hence, assume that
f
is incident to
v_{1}
(the case that
f
is incident to
v_{3}
is the same).
Again, there must be an edge from
C_{3}
to
C_{2}
, say
g
.
Now observe that either
H^{\prime}=(H\cup\{e_{1},e_{2},g\})\setminus\{v_{1}v_{2},u_{1}u_{2}\}
or
H^{\prime\prime}=(H\cup\{e_{1},f,g\})\setminus\{v_{1}v_{2},u_{1}u_{2}\}
is a canonical
2
-edge cover with fewer components than
H
.
Further,
\mathrm{cost}(H^{\prime})\leq\mathrm{cost}(H^{\prime})
since
|H^{\prime}|\leq|H|+1
and
\mathrm{cr}(H^{\prime})\leq\mathrm{cr}(H)-2-2-2(1-4\delta)+2\leq\mathrm{cr}(H)-1
.
This finishes the case distinction and the proof of the lemma.
∎

We assume that we have applied all previous lemmas exhaustively.
In particular, the last lemma implies that every segment with at most four components contains at most one component that is either large or complex.

Next, we show that if there is a segment with at least
5
nodes that does not contain a huge component and does contain a
\mathcal{C}_{i}
for some
4\leq i\leq 8
, then we can turn
H
into a canonical
2
-edge cover
H^{\prime}
with fewer components than
H
and
\mathrm{cost}(H^{\prime})\leq\mathrm{cost}(H)
.

Lemma 44
.

Given a canonical
2
-edge cover
H
of a structured graph
G
such that the component graph
\bm{\hat{}}{G}_{H}
contains a segment
S
with at least
5
components but
S
does not contain a huge component.
Then we can compute a canonical
2
-edge cover
H^{\prime}
with fewer components than
H
and
\mathrm{cost}(H^{\prime})\leq\mathrm{cost}(H)
in polynomial time.

Proof.

Let
S^{\prime}
be another segment of
\bm{\hat{}}{G}_{H}
that contains a huge component
L
.
In
S
there must be a cut node
z
that separates
S\setminus\{z\}
from
S^{\prime}
and therefore also from
L
.
If
z
is a
\mathcal{C}_{i}
for
5\leq i\leq 8
, then we can assume that
S\setminus\{z\}
has at least
i
components since we can apply
Lemma

42
exhaustively.

Suppose
z
is a
\mathcal{C}_{i}
for
4\leq i\leq 8
, then
S\setminus\{z\}
contains at least
i
components and
4i
vertices.
This implies that
z
is a large
\mathcal{C}_{i}
cut since
L
contains at least
32
vertices, contradicting that
G
is structured.
Hence,
z
corresponds to either a large or complex component.

If
z
and
L
are in the same segment, then we can either apply
Lemma

16
if the segment has at least
5
components or apply
Lemma

43
if the segment has at most
4
components.
Otherwise, consider the segment
S^{\prime\prime}\neq S
containing
z
and another cut node
z^{\prime}
separating
L
from
z
.
If
S^{\prime\prime}
has at least
5
components, we continue with the same arguments as above, i.e., replace
S
with
S^{\prime\prime}
.
If
S^{\prime\prime}
has at most
4
components, then we can argue as above that
z^{\prime}
is also a large or complex component.
Then we can apply
Lemma

43
in
S^{\prime\prime}
.
∎

We assume that we have applied the above lemmas exhaustively.
This implies the following:
H
does not contain any
\mathcal{C}_{i}
for
5\leq i\leq 8
, and each segment has at most
4
nodes and contains at most one large or complex component.

Next, we show that if there are two components that are large or complex, then we can turn
H
into a canonical
2
-edge cover
H^{\prime}
with fewer components than
H
and
\mathrm{cost}(H^{\prime})\leq\mathrm{cost}(H)
.

Lemma 45
.

Given a canonical
2
-edge cover
H
of a structured graph
G
such that the component graph
\bm{\hat{}}{G}_{H}
contains a huge component
L
and another component
C\neq L
that is either large or complex.
Then we can compute a canonical
2
-edge cover
H^{\prime}
with fewer components than
H
and
\mathrm{cost}(H^{\prime})\leq\mathrm{cost}(H)
in polynomial time.

Proof.

We assume that we have applied all of the above lemmas exhaustively and hence no condition of the above lemmas is met.
Let
S
be the segment containing
L
and
S^{\prime}\neq S
the segment containing
C
, such that there is a
\mathcal{C}_{4}
-cut node
z
in
S
separating
C
and
L
.
Since
H
is canonical and we cannot merge two
\mathcal{C}_{4}
s into one
\mathcal{C}_{8}
,
z
cannot be in a segment with only
2

\mathcal{C}_{4}
’s.
Since
z
cannot correspond to a large
\mathcal{C}_{4}
cut (we cannot partition
G\setminus V(z)
into two parts with at least
16
vertices) as
G
is structured and
|V(C)|\geq 9
,

z
must be in exactly
2
segments:
S
and
S^{\prime}
.
Furthermore,
S^{\prime}
either consists of
z
and
C
, or
z
,
C
and another component
z^{\prime}
that is a
\mathcal{C}_{4}
.
Let the vertices of
z
be labeled as
v_{1}-v_{2}-v_{3}-v_{4}
.
We apply
Lemma

32
to find a cycle
F
in
S
containing
L
and
z
such that
F

is incident to
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
.

If
S^{\prime}
consists of
z
and
C
,
by
Lemma

33
, there must be a 3-matching between
V(z)
and
V(C)
, which implies that there is a pair
v_{i}
and
v_{i+1}
for some
i\in\{2,3\}
, such that
v_{i}C,v_{i+1}C\in E(G)
.
We let
H^{\prime}\coloneq H\setminus\{v_{1}v_{2},v_{i}v_{i+1}\}\cup F\cup\{v_{i}C,v_{i+1}C\}
.
In this way we save the credits of the components incident to
F
and
C
.
Therefore,
\mathrm{cr}(H^{\prime})\leq\mathrm{cr}(H)-(|F|-1)\cdot 4\cdot(\frac{1}{4}-\delta)-2-2+2=\mathrm{cr}(H)-|F|+4\delta(|F|-1)-1\leq\mathrm{cr}(H)-|F|
.
The last inequality holds since
S
contains at most
4
components and
|F|\geq 4
.
We have
|H^{\prime}|=|H|-2+|F|+2=|H|+|F|
, which implies that
\mathrm{cost}(H^{\prime})\leq\mathrm{cost}(H)
.

Assume next that
S^{\prime}
consists of
z
,
C
, and another component
z^{\prime}
that is a
\mathcal{C}_{4}
.
Let the vertices of
z^{\prime}
be labeled as
v_{1}^{\prime}-v_{2}^{\prime}-v_{3}^{\prime}-v_{4}^{\prime}
.
Since
S^{\prime}
is 2-node-connected, there is a cycle
F^{\prime}
in
S^{\prime}
through
z,z^{\prime}
and
C
.
We show that there is a cycle
F^{\prime\prime}
in
S^{\prime}
through
z,z^{\prime}
and
C
, and incident to neighboring vertices of
z^{\prime}
, say,
v_{1}^{\prime}
and
v_{2}^{\prime}
.
Suppose
F^{\prime}
does not satisfy this condition, say,
F^{\prime}
is incident to
v_{1}^{\prime}
and
v_{3}^{\prime}
.
We apply
Lemma

33
to find a 3-matching between
V(z^{\prime})
and
V(C)\cup V(z)
.
We can assume that there is an edge in the 3-matching that is incident to
v_{2}^{\prime}
.
If the edge is incident to
C
, we let
F^{\prime\prime}\coloneq F\setminus\{z^{\prime}C\}\cup{v_{2}C}
, otherwise we let
F^{\prime\prime}\coloneq F\setminus\{z^{\prime}z\}\cup{v_{2}^{\prime}z}
.
It can be easily checked that
F^{\prime\prime}
is a cycle in
S^{\prime}
through
z,z^{\prime}
and
C
, and incident to neighboring vertices of
z^{\prime}
, say,
v_{1}^{\prime}
and
v_{2}^{\prime}
(or
v_{2}^{\prime}
and
v_{3}^{\prime}
).
We let
H^{\prime}\coloneq H\setminus\{v_{1}v_{2},v_{1}^{\prime}v_{2}^{\prime}\}\cup F^{\prime\prime}\cup F
.
Then we have
|H^{\prime}|=|H|-2+|F^{\prime\prime}|+|F|=|H|+|F|+1
and
\mathrm{cr}(H^{\prime})\leq\mathrm{cr}(H)-(|F|-1+1)\cdot 4\cdot(\frac{1}{4}-\delta)-2-2+2=\mathrm{cr}(H)-|F|-2+4\delta|F|\leq\mathrm{cr}(H)-|F|-1
.
The last inequality holds since
S
contains at most
4
components and
|F|\geq 4
.
Therefore,
\mathrm{cost}(H^{\prime})\leq\mathrm{cost}(H)
.
∎

We assume that we have applied the above lemmas exhaustively.
This implies the following:

H
contains one huge component
L
, which may be complex, and all other components are
\mathcal{C}_{4}
’s.
Now we prove
Lemma

15
.

Proof of
Lemma

15
.

Recall that
L
can be 2EC or complex, i.e., containing some bridges.
Assume
L
is complex.
Before we prove the properties of a core-square configuration, we turn the component
L
into a 2EC component without increasing
\mathrm{cost}(H)
.

Consider a pendant block
B
of
L
that is adjacent to a bridge
e
.
Since
G
is 2EC, there must be a bridge-covering path
P
in
G
starting from
B
that covers
e
.
Since we have exhaustively applied the above lemmas, we know that
L
is only contained in segments of
\bm{\hat{}}{G}_{H}
of size at most
4
.
Therefore, observe that there is a bridge-covering path
P
of length at most
4
starting in
B
and covering
e
.
Hence,
P
is a short heavy cycle and according to
Lemma

39
,
H^{\prime}=H\cup P
is a canonical
2
-edge cover with
\mathrm{cost}(H)\leq\mathrm{cost}(H^{\prime})
.

If after the application of this bridge-covering step one of the above lemmas applies, then we exhaustively apply that lemma.
Hence, we can cover all bridges of
L
and turn it into a 2EC component.
Therefore, we now assume that
L
is bridgeless and we have exhaustively applied the above lemmas.
These directly imply the Properties (i) and (ii) of the core-square configuration.

We next show Property (iv).
That is, we show that each cut component which is a
\mathcal{C}_{4}
is contained in at most
2
segments.
Suppose that there is a cut node
z
which corresponds to a
\mathcal{C}_{4}
and is contained in at least
3
segments, say,
S_{1},S_{2}
and
S_{3}
.
We can assume that
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
do not contain the huge component
L
.
Then each of
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
must contain at least
3
components, as otherwise we can merge two
\mathcal{C}_{4}
s into a
\mathcal{C}_{8}
.
This implies that
z
is a large
\mathcal{C}_{4}
cut since
(S_{1}\cup S_{2})\setminus z
already contains at least
16
vertices, which contradicts that
G
is structured.

For each cut node
z
corresponding to a
\mathcal{C}_{4}
,
let
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
be the segments containing
z
.
We show that either of
S_{1}
or
S_{2}
contains
L
.
Otherwise, there must be another cut component
z^{\prime}
which is a
\mathcal{C}_{4}
and separates
\{S_{1}\cup S_{2}\}\setminus\{z^{\prime}\}
from
L
.
This implies that
z^{\prime}
is a large
\mathcal{C}_{4}
cut since
\{S_{1}\cup S_{2}\}\setminus\{z^{\prime}\}
contains at least
4\cdot 4=16
vertices and
L
contains at least
32
vertices, which contradicts that
G
is structured.
Without loss of generality, we can assume that
S_{1}
contains
L
.
Then
S_{2}
must contain at most
4
components, otherwise
z
is a large
\mathcal{C}_{4}
cut, which contradicts that
G
is structured.
Hence, we ensure the Property (iv) of the core-square configuration.

We next prove Property (iii), i.e., we show that we can turn
H
into a canonical
2
-edge cover such that
L
is only contained in segments of size
2
.

Assume first that
L
is contained in at least two segments
S,S^{\prime}
of size at least 3 (and at most 4, since we can not apply the above lemmas).
Then, by
Lemma

40
, in
S
there exists a single
(k,j)
-merge containing
L
such that
k-j\geq 2
and
k\in\{3,4\}
.
Then, by
Lemma

41
, there is a double
(k,j)
-merge containing
L
for some
k\geq 5
and
k-j\geq 4
.
Hence, by
Lemma

39
we can turn
H
into a canonical
2
-edge cover
H^{\prime}
with
\mathrm{cost}(H^{\prime})\leq\mathrm{cost}(H)
.
If after the application of this step one of the above lemmas applies, then we exhaustively apply that lemma.
Observe that again the Properties (i), (ii), and (iv) are satisfied.

Now assume there is only one segment
S
containing
L
and having at least 3 nodes (and at most 4).
If one of the other components
C
in
S
(which is a
\mathcal{C}_{4}
), is a cut node, then
C
is contained in another segment
S^{\prime}
containing either 3 or 4 components.
Then we do the same as in the previous step:
By
Lemma

40
, in
S
there exists a single
(k,j)
-merge containing
C
such that
k-j\geq 2
and
k\in\{3,4\}
.
Furthermore, by
Lemma

41
, there is a double
(k,j)
-merge containing
C
for some
k\geq 5
and
k-j\geq 4
.
Hence, by
Lemma

39
we can turn
H
into a canonical
2
-edge cover
H^{\prime}
with
\mathrm{cost}(H^{\prime})\leq\mathrm{cost}(H)
.

If after the application of this step one of the above lemmas applies, then we exhaustively apply that lemma.
Observe that again the Properties (i), (ii), and (iv) are satisfied.
Hence, in the remaining case we have that there is only one segment
S
containing
L
and having at least 3 nodes (and at most 4).
Furthermore, none of the nodes in the segment is a cut node.
Observe that there exists a simple cycle
X
in
S
containing all nodes of
S
. Set
H^{\prime}=H\cup X
.
Then,
|H^{\prime}|=|H|+|X|
and

\mathrm{cr}(H^{\prime})\leq\mathrm{cr}(H)+2-2-(|X|-1)\cdot(1-4\delta)\leq\mathrm{cr}(H)-|X|+1+(|X|-1)\cdot 4\delta\leq\mathrm{cr}(H)-|X|+2\ ,

since the newly created large 2EC component needs 2 credits, the huge 2EC component
L
has a credit of 2 and every other component is a
\mathcal{C}_{4}
.
Then we have
\mathrm{cost}(H^{\prime})\leq\mathrm{cost}(H)+2
, as desired.
Furthermore, observe that after this step Property (iii) is satisfied, since now
L
is only contained in segments of size 2.
This finishes the proof.
∎

4.2.1
Proof of
Lemma

16

To prove
Lemma

16
, we use the definition of a
gluing path
. A similar concept in a weaker form has been used in
[
GGJ23
]
.

See
10

An example of a gluing path is given in
Figure

5
.
The benefit of a gluing path is the following: Assume we are given a gluing path
P
of length
k\geq 4
.
If there is some edge
e
from a node
v
of
P
to some node
u
of
P
such that their distance on
P
is at least
4
, then consider the cycle
C
formed by the subpath of
P
from
u
to
v
and the edge
e
.
Clearly, adding the
k+1
edges of
C
to
H
turns the
k+1
components of
C
into one single
2
-edge connected component
C^{\prime}
. Let
H^{\prime}
be the new
2
-edge cover.
Furthermore, due to the definition of a gluing path, each component of
C
that is a
\mathcal{C}_{4}
(except the components corresponding to
u
and
v
) can be shortcut. That is, we can remove one edge for each such component such that
C^{\prime}
remains
2
-edge connected.
Therefore, it can be easily checked that
H^{\prime}
is also a canonical
2
-edge cover such that
\mathrm{cost}(H^{\prime})\leq\mathrm{cost}(H)
.

Of course, such edges may not always exist.
Hence, our goal is the following:
Given some canonical
2
-edge cover and some gluing path
P
of length
k
, either we want to make progress by turning
H
into some canonical
2
-edge cover
H^{\prime}
such that
H^{\prime}
contains fewer components than
H
and
\mathrm{cost}(H^{\prime})\leq\mathrm{cost}(H)
or we can extend the gluing path
P
to a gluing path
P^{\prime}
such that the length of
P
is greater than the length of
P
.
Since the length of a gluing path can easily be bounded by the number of vertices of
G
, after polynomially many extension we must find the desired
H^{\prime}
.
This motivates the definition of
progress
.

Definition 17

(progress)
.

Given a
2
-edge cover
H
with a gluing path
P
, we say that we make
progress
if in polynomial time either

(i)

we can turn
H
into a canonical
2
-edge cover
H^{\prime}
such that
\mathrm{cost}(H^{\prime})\leq\mathrm{cost}(H)
and either
H^{\prime}
contains fewer components than
H
or
H^{\prime}
has the same number of components as
H
but
H^{\prime}
has fewer bridges than
H
, or

(ii)

we can extend the gluing path
P
to a gluing path
P^{\prime}
such that the length of
P^{\prime}
is greater than the length of
P
.

We will then show the following lemma.

Lemma 46
.

Given a canonical
2
-edge cover
H
of some structured graph
G
that is not in a core-square configuration and a gluing path
P
, then we can make progress.

Thus,
Lemma

46
implies that eventually we end up with a core-square configuration.
Hence, proving
Lemma

46
implies
Lemma

16
.
Therefore, for now we want to prove
Lemma

46
.

Throughout this subsection, we assume that we are given some
2
-node connected segment
S
of
\bm{\hat{}}{G}_{H}
of
H
that contains at least
5
nodes (see prerequisites of
Lemma

16
).
Consider some gluing path
P=z_{k}-z_{k-1}-...-z_{1}
of length
k-1
on the nodes
z_{1},...,z_{k}
(each corresponding to some component of
H
).
We say that
z_{k}
is the start of the gluing path and
z_{1}
is the end.
If
z_{i}
is a
\mathcal{C}_{4}
, we label the vertices of
z_{i}
by
v_{1}^{i},v_{2}^{i},v_{3}^{i},v_{4}^{i}
, which also defines the cycle of the
\mathcal{C}_{4}
.
If
z_{i}
is not a
\mathcal{C}_{4}
,
V(z_{i})
contains at least
4
vertices and we say that
v_{1}^{i},v_{2}^{i},v_{3}^{i},v_{4}^{i}
are some arbitrary distinct vertices of
V(z_{i})
.
For
i\leq j
, the sub-path of
P
from node
i
to node
j
, i.e.,
z_{i}-z_{i+1}-...-z_{j-1}-z_{j}
is denoted by
P_{ij}
.
For a node
z_{i}
, we say that the in-vertex is the vertex incident to the edge
z_{i+1}z_{i}
and the out-vertex is the vertex incident to the edge
z_{i}z_{i-1}
.
Without loss of generality we let the in-vertex of node
z_{i}
be
v_{1}^{i}
and the out-vertex be
v_{2}^{i}
(recall that according to the definition of a gluing path, the in-vertex and the out-vertex must be adjacent in
H
for a
\mathcal{C}_{4}
; for complex components, the in-vertex and the out-vertex must be distinct if they do not belong to the same block).

Figure 5
:
Example of a gluing path of length
5
:
z_{5}
,
z_{2}
, and
z_{1}
are
\mathcal{C}_{4}
’s,
z_{4}
is complex, and
z_{3}
is a
\mathcal{C}_{5}
.

We first prove that we can compute a gluing path of length at least
3
in polynomial time.

Lemma 47
.

Given a
2
-edge cover
H
of some structured graph
G
such that
H
contains a
2
-node connected segment
S
of
\bm{\hat{}}{G}_{H}
of
H
that contains at least
3
nodes, we can compute a gluing path of length at least
2
in polynomial time.

Proof.

If
S
contains a node
z_{2}
that is not a
\mathcal{C}_{4}
, then there is clearly a gluing path of length
2
:
Since
S
is
2
-node connected, there must be two edges incident to
z_{2}
to two distinct nodes
z_{1},z_{3}
, that are not
z_{2}
. Say
e_{1}
is incident to
z_{1}
and
e_{2}
is incident to
z_{3}
.
Furthermore, there must be two such edges such that they are not incident to the same vertex of
z_{2}
, as otherwise
G
is not
2
-vertex connected, a contradiction to
G
being structured. Hence, we can use such two edges, which form a path
z_{1}-z_{2}-z_{3}
that forms a gluing path.

Hence, let us assume all nodes in
S
are a
\mathcal{C}_{4}
.
Let
z_{2}
be one such component.
Since
S
is
2
-node connected, there must be two edges
e_{1},e_{2}
incident to
z_{2}
to two distinct nodes
z_{1},z_{3}
, that are not
z_{2}
.
If
e_{1},e_{2}
are incident to vertices that are adjacent within
z_{2}
w.r.t. the edges of
H
, then one can easily check that
z_{1}-z_{2}-z_{3}
is a gluing path using the edges
e_{1}
and
e_{2}
.
Hence, let us assume this is not the case.
Let
v_{1}^{2}-v_{2}^{2}-v_{3}^{2}-v_{4}^{2}-v_{1}^{2}
be the
4
-cycle when expanding
z_{2}
.
Let us assume
e_{1},e_{2}
are incident to the same vertex, say
v_{1}^{2}
. The case that one edge is incident to
v_{1}^{2}
and the other to
v_{3}^{2}
is analogous.
By the
3
-Matching
Lemma

4
, there must be an edge
e^{\prime}
incident to either
v_{2}^{2}
or
v_{4}^{2}
that is going to some component that is not
z_{2}
. Both cases are symmetric, so assume
e^{\prime}
is incident to
v_{2}^{2}
.
If
e^{\prime}
is incident to some component
z^{\prime}\neq z_{1}
, one can easily check that there is a gluing path
z_{1}-z_{2}-z^{\prime}
using the edges
e_{1}
and
e^{\prime}
.
Otherwise, if
e^{\prime}
is incident to
z_{1}
, one can easily check that there is a gluing path
z_{1}-z_{2}-z_{3}
using the edges
e^{\prime}
and
e_{2}
.
∎

Hence, from now on we assume that we are always given some gluing path
P=z_{3}-z_{2}-z_{1}
of length at least
2
.

Next, we show the following useful lemma. An illustration of the lemma is given in
Figure

6
.

Lemma 48
.

Let
P=z_{k}-z_{k-1}-...-z_{1}
be a gluing path of length
k-1\geq 2
. Then either
v_{2}^{1}
is adjacent to one of
\{v_{2}^{3},v_{4}^{3}\}
or we can make progress.
Similarly, either
v_{1}^{k}
is adjacent to one of
\{v_{1}^{k-2},v_{3}^{k-2}\}
or we can make progress.

Figure 6
:
Lemma

48
: Either at least one of the dashed orange edges exists, or we can make progress.

Proof.

We prove the statement for
v_{2}^{1}
. The statement for
v_{1}^{k}
is analogous.
We can apply the
3
-Matching
Lemma

4
to
V(z_{1})
and hence there must be an edge
e
outgoing to some other node in
S
from either
v_{2}^{1}
or
v_{4}^{1}
.
Without loss of generality, we assume that
e
is incident to
v_{2}^{1}
, i.e.,
e=v_{2}^{1}u
.
We assume that
u\notin\{v_{2}^{3},v_{4}^{3}\}
and show that we can make progress.

If
u
is part of some component not on
P
, then note that we can add
e

to the gluing path
P
and obtain a longer gluing path
P^{\prime}
and hence make progress.
So now assume that
u\in V(P)
.
This is independent of what type of component
z_{1}
is.

If
u\in V(z_{i})
,
4\leq i\leq k
, then we can make progress as follows:
Note that
K=P_{1i}\cup e
is a cycle in the component graph.
Let
H^{\prime}=H\cup K
and note that it is a canonical
2
-edge cover.
Further, note that for each node
z_{j}
,
1<j<i
, that is a
\mathcal{C}_{4}
, the edge
v_{1}^{j}v_{2}^{j}
can be removed from
H^{\prime}
such that
H^{\prime}
is still a canonical
2
-edge cover.
Therefore,
C
defines a single
(i,1)
-merge, and hence, by
Lemma

39
,
\mathrm{cost}(H^{\prime})\leq\mathrm{cost}(H)
.
Thus, we can assume that
u\in V(z_{2})\cup V(z_{3})
.

If
u\in\{v_{3}^{3},v_{1}^{3}\}
, then similar to the previous case we can make progress as follows:
P_{13}\cup e
defines a single
(3,0)
-merge, and hence by
Lemma

39
, we can make progress.

Therefore, let us assume that
u\in V(z_{2})
.
If either
z_{1}
or
z_{2}
is a
\mathcal{C}_{5}
,
\mathcal{C}_{6}
,
\mathcal{C}_{7}
, or
\mathcal{C}_{8}
, then there is a short heavy cycle between
z_{1}
and
z_{2}
and we can make progress according to
Lemma

39
.
Similarly, if neither
z_{1}
nor
z_{2}
is a
\mathcal{C}_{4}
, i.e., if
z_{1},z_{2}
are either large or complex, then we can make progress according to
Lemma

39
.

Hence, in the remaining case we have that at least one of
z_{1},z_{2}
is a
\mathcal{C}_{4}
and at most one of
\{z_{1},z_{2}\}
is large or complex.
We now show that either we can make progress, or we can switch the edge
z_{2}z_{1}
from
P
with the edge
e
such that we still have a gluing path
P^{\prime}
of length
k-1
.
In particular, the in-vertex of
z_{1}
on
P
is
v_{1}^{1}
and the in-vertex of
z_{1}
on
P^{\prime}
is
v_{2}^{1}
.
For the sake of contradiction, assume the statement is not true.
Then
z_{2}
can not be large, as otherwise the above statement is always true.
If
z_{2}
is complex, then
u
can not be equal to
v_{1}^{2}
, the out-vertex of
z_{2}
, since then we can also find the desired gluing path
P^{\prime}
.
Furthermore, the out-vertex of
z_{2}
can also not be in the same block as
u
on
z_{2}
.
Hence, the unique path from
v_{2}^{2}
to
u
contains at least one bridge.
But then
e
and the edge from
z_{2}
to
z_{1}
on
P
defines a short heavy cycle and hence
Lemma

39
implies that we can make progress.
Thus,
z_{2}
is a
\mathcal{C}_{4}
.
We now make a case distinction on whether
z_{1}
is complex, large or a
\mathcal{C}_{4}
.
If
z_{1}
is a
\mathcal{C}_{4}
, then
u
can neither be
v_{3}^{2}
nor
v_{1}^{2}
:
Otherwise this is a contradiction to
H
being a canonical
2
-edge cover, as then
e
and the edge on
P
connecting
z_{2}
and
z_{1}
can be used to merge
z_{1}
and
z_{2}
into one
\mathcal{C}_{8}
.
Therefore,
u\in\{v_{2}^{2},v_{4}^{2}\}
.
Now clearly there is such a desired gluing path
P^{\prime}
of length
k-1
.
Hence,
z_{1}
is either large or complex.
There must be an edge
e^{\prime}
from
z_{1}
to some other node distinct from
z_{2}
(and
z_{1}
), as otherwise
z_{1}
is not in a
2
-node connected component of
\bm{\hat{}}{G}_{H}
containing at least
3
nodes.
If
z_{1}
is large, then following the same proof as before but switching
e
with
e^{\prime}
, we can make progress.
In particular, if
e^{\prime}
is incident to
z_{i}
with
i\geq 4
, or
e^{\prime}
is incident to
v_{3}^{3}
or
v_{1}^{3}
, then we can make progress since this defines a single
(i,1)
-merge or a single
(3,0)
-merge and the result follows by
Lemma

39
.
Hence,
z_{1}
is complex.
If the in-vertex of
z_{1}
, i.e.,
v_{1}^{1}
, is not part of some block of
z_{1}
, then the edge from
z_{2}
to
z_{1}
from
P
together with
e
defines a short heavy cycle, since there is at least one bridge on the unique path from
v_{1}^{1}
to
v_{2}^{1}
. Therefore, we can make progress according to
Lemma

39
.
Hence, assume that the in-vertex of
z_{1}
, i.e.,
v_{1}^{1}
, is part of some block of
z_{1}
.
But then the statement clearly follows because we can replace the edge
z_{2}z_{1}
on
P
with
e
to obtain a new gluing path
P^{\prime}
of length
k-1
ending on
v_{2}^{1}
, as desired.

Therefore, since we assume that we can not make progress, in the remaining case we assume that we can replace the edge
z_{2}z_{1}
of the gluing path by the edge
e
and still have a gluing path of length
k-1
.
Since there must be a
3
-matching outgoing from
z_{1}
by
Lemma

4
, there must be an additional edge
f
incident to
z_{1}
.
Since we can replace the gluing path
P
with the gluing path
P^{\prime}
, by symmetry, we can w.l.o.g. assume that
f
is incident to
v_{4}^{1}
.
Now for
f
we must have the same conditions as for
e
and hence, either
f
is incident to one of
\{v_{2}^{3},v_{4}^{3}\}
, to
z_{2}
, or we can make progress.
The only case in which we do not show the statement of the lemma is that
f
is incident to
z_{2}
.
Now observe that this is true for any edge
f
incident to
z_{1}
and some other component that is not
z_{1}
.
But then
z_{1}
is not part of the
2
-node connected segment
S
, a contradiction.
This finishes the proof.
∎

Hence, from now on we can always assume that
v_{2}^{1}
is adjacent to one of
\{v_{2}^{3},v_{4}^{3}\}
and
v_{2}^{k}
is adjacent to one of
\{v_{2}^{k-2},v_{4}^{k-2}\}
.

The next lemma states some useful facts about what concrete components
z_{1}
,
z_{2}
and
z_{3}
can be.

Lemma 49
.

Let
P=z_{k}-z_{k-1}-...-z_{1}
be a gluing path of length
k-1\geq 2
. We can make progress if
one of
z_{1},z_{2},z_{3}
(or one of
z_{k},z_{k-1},z_{k-2}
) is a
\mathcal{C}_{5},\mathcal{C}_{6},\mathcal{C}_{7}
or a
\mathcal{C}_{8}
.

Proof.

We show the lemma only for
z_{2}
as the other statement is analogous.
By
Lemma

48
, we know that either we can make progress or there is an edge
e
from
v_{1}^{2}
to one of
\{v_{2}^{3},v_{4}^{3}\}
. In the former case we are done. Hence, assume we are in the latter case.
Say without loss of generality
e
is incident to
v_{2}^{3}
.
Observe that there is a cycle
K
on the nodes
z_{1}-z_{2}-z_{3}
using
P_{13}
and
e
.
If one of
z_{1},z_{2},z_{3}
is a
\mathcal{C}_{5},\mathcal{C}_{6},\mathcal{C}_{7}
or a
\mathcal{C}_{8}
, then this cycle is a short heavy cycle, and hence we can make progress.
This proves the first statement.
∎

The next two lemmas show that we can always assume that we are given a gluing path of length at least
4
, i.e., the gluing path contains at least 5 nodes.

Lemma 50
.

Given a
2
-edge cover
H
of some structured graph
G
such that
H
contains a
2
-node connected segment
S
of
\bm{\hat{}}{G}_{H}
of
H
that contains at least
4
nodes, in polynomial time we can compute a gluing path of length at least
3
or we can make progress.

Proof.

According to
Lemma

47
we can find a gluing path
P=z_{3}-z_{2}-z_{1}
of length
2
.
Furthermore, by
Lemma

49
, we know that components on
P
are either large, complex or a
\mathcal{C}_{4}
.
Additionally, by
Lemma

48
, we know that
v_{2}^{1}
is adjacent to one of
\{v_{2}^{3},v_{4}^{3}\}
or we can make progress and
v_{1}^{3}
is adjacent to one of
\{v_{1}^{1},v_{3}^{1}\}
or we can make progress.
If we can make progress, we are done. Hence, we assume that those two edges, say
e_{1},e_{3}
, exist. Let
e_{1}
be the edge incident to
v_{2}^{1}
and
e_{3}
be the edge incident to
v_{1}^{3}
.

First, assume that both
z_{1}
and
z_{3}
are
\mathcal{C}_{4}
.
But now it is easy to check that
e_{1}
and
e_{3}
can be used to turn
z_{1}
and
z_{3}
into a
\mathcal{C}_{8}
, a contradiction to our starting canonical
2
-edge cover.

Hence, assume w.l.o.g. that
z_{1}
is not a
\mathcal{C}_{4}
. Now it is easy to see that
P_{13}\cup\{e_{3}\}
is a single
(3,0)
-merge, and hence, by
Lemma

39
, we can make progress.
∎

Lemma 51
.

Given a
2
-edge cover
H
of some structured graph
G
such that
H
contains a
2
-node connected segment
S
of
\bm{\hat{}}{G}_{H}
of
H
that contains at least
5
nodes, in polynomial time we can compute a gluing path of length at least
4
or we can make progress.

Proof.

According to
Lemma

50
we can find a gluing path
P=z_{4}-z_{3}-z_{2}-z_{1}
of length
3
.
Furthermore, by
Lemma

49
, we know that components on
P
are either large, complex or a
\mathcal{C}_{4}
.
Additionally, by
Lemma

48
, we know that
v_{2}^{1}
is adjacent to one of
\{v_{2}^{3},v_{4}^{3}\}
or we can make progress and
v_{1}^{4}
is adjacent to one of
\{v_{1}^{2},v_{3}^{2}\}
or we can make progress.
If we can make progress, we are done. Hence, we assume that those two edges, say
e_{1},e_{4}
, exist. Let
e_{1}
be the edge incident to
v_{2}^{1}
and
e_{4}
be the edge incident to
v_{1}^{4}
.
Now observe that

Z=\Big(P_{14}\cup E(z_{1})\cup E(z_{2})\cup E(z_{3})\cup E(z_{4})\cup\{e_{1},e_{2}\}\Big)\setminus\{v_{1}^{1}v_{2}^{1},v_{1}^{2}v_{2}^{2},v_{1}^{3}v_{2}^{3},v_{1}^{4}v_{2}^{4}\}

makes
V(z_{1})\cup V(z_{2})\cup V(z_{3})\cup V(z_{4})

2
EC. Further,
H^{\prime}=(H\setminus E(z_{1})\cup E(z_{2})\cup E(z_{3}\cup E(z_{4}))\cup Z)
is a canonical
2
-edge cover with fewer components than
H
and
\mathrm{cost}(H^{\prime})\leq\mathrm{cost}(H)
. Hence, we can make progress.
∎

We next consider different types of
branches
, i.e., we consider the case that we are given two gluing paths that overlap significantly in some way.
We show that if we have such branches, then we can make progress. This is summarized as follows.

Figure 7
:
An example for a double
(5,1)
-merge in
Lemma

52
.

Lemma 52
.

Let
P=z_{k}-z_{k-1}-...-z_{3}-z_{2a}-z_{1a}
be a gluing path of length
k-1\geq 2
and let
P^{\prime}=z_{k}-z_{k-1}-...-z_{3}-z_{2b}-z_{1b}
be another gluing path of length
k-1\geq 2
such that the first
k-2
nodes are the same in
P
and
P^{\prime}
but the remaining
4
nodes are all distinct, i.e.,
z_{1a}\neq z_{2b}\neq z_{2a}
and
z_{1a}\neq z_{1b}\neq z_{2a}
.
Then, we can make progress.

Proof.

Assume we cannot make progress. Then, applying
Lemma

48
to
P
and
P^{\prime}
on
z_{1a}
and
z_{1b}
, we know there is an edge
e_{a}
from
v_{2}^{1a}
to one of
\{v_{2}^{3},v_{4}^{3}\}
and one edge
e_{b}
from
v_{2}^{1b}
to one of
\{v_{2}^{3},v_{4}^{3}\}
.
Now observe that
P_{1a3}
,
P^{\prime}_{1b3}
together with
e_{a}
and
e_{b}
form a double
(5,1)
-merge, and hence by
Lemma

39
we can make progress. This situation is illustrated in
Figure

7
.
∎

Before we proceed with the next type of branch, we first show that
z_{3}
is a
\mathcal{C}_{4}
or we can make progress.

Lemma 53
.

Let
P=z_{k}-z_{k-1}-...-z_{1}
be a gluing path of length
k-1\geq 2
. We can make progress if

z_{3}
(or
z_{k-2}
) is not a
\mathcal{C}_{4}
.

Proof.

We show the lemma only for
z_{3}
as the other statement is analogous.
By
Lemma

48
, we know that either we can make progress or there is an edge
e
from
v_{1}^{2}
to one of
\{v_{2}^{3},v_{4}^{3}\}
. In the former case we are done. Hence, assume we are in the latter case.
Observe that there is a cycle
K
on the nodes
z_{1}-z_{2}-z_{3}
using
P_{13}
and
e
.
By
Lemma

49

z_{3}
is either large, complex or a
\mathcal{C}_{4}
.
If
z_{3}
is large, then
K
is a single
(3,0)
-merge, and hence we can make progress according to
Lemma

39
.
Hence, in the remainder assume that
z_{3}
is a complex component.
If
e
is not incident to
v_{2}^{3}
, then either
K
is a short heavy cycle (if
K
covers a bridge of
z_{3}
) or it is a single
(3,0)
-merge (if
K
is incident to a block of
z_{3}
). Hence, in either case we can make progress.
Hence, for the remainder assume that
e
is incident to
v_{2}^{3}
and that this vertex is not contained in a block. Otherwise, as above,
K
is a single
(3,0)
-merge, and we are done.
Furthermore, w.l.o.g. at most one vertex of
z_{3}
, say
v_{2}^{3}
, is adjacent to
z_{1}
.
Otherwise, if there are two vertices of
z_{3}
adjacent to
z_{1}
, by the previous arguments we know that adding both edges covers at least one bridge of
z_{3}
and hence we make progress.

Assume that one of
z_{1}
or
z_{2}
(w.l.o.g.
z_{1}
; the other case is analogous) is incident to some edge
e^{\prime}
which is incident to
z_{i}
with
i\geq 4
.
Then
P_{1i}
together with
e^{\prime}
is a cycle, which is either a short heavy cycle (if
i\leq 6
) or a single
(i,j)
-merge for
j\in\{0,1\}
(if
i\geq 7
), and hence in either case we can make progress.
If
z_{1}
or
z_{2}
is incident to a vertex of
z_{3}
distinct from
v_{2}^{3}
, then there is also a short heavy cycle.
Hence, let us assume this is not the case. This implies that
z_{1},z_{2}
are only connected to each other, to
v_{2}^{3}
and potentially to some other nodes distinct from
P
.

We next prove that both
z_{1},z_{2}
are
\mathcal{C}_{4}
.
Note that if
z_{1}
or
z_{2}
is complex, then
K
does not cover a bridge of any of these components, since otherwise we have a heavy short cycle.
Hence, if neither
z_{1}
nor
z_{2}
is
\mathcal{C}_{4}
, it can be easily checked that adding
K
to
H
satisfies the credit invariant and we made progress.
Hence, assume exactly one of
z_{1},z_{2}
is a
\mathcal{C}_{4}
. W.l.o.g. we can assume
z_{2}
is a
\mathcal{C}_{4}
, since the other case is symmetric.
If
z_{1}
is complex, then there is only one block of
z_{1}
which is connected to
S
, the segment of
P
.
Otherwise, we can make progress since either we can extend the gluing path (if the edge is incident to some node of
S
not on
P
), we have a short heavy cycle covering a bridge of
z_{1}
(if the edge is incident to some node
z_{i}
with
i\leq 6
), or there is a single
(i,j)
-merge for
j\in\{0,1\}
(if the edge is incident to
z_{i}
on
P
with
i\geq 7
).
Hence, since all edges incident to
z_{1}
and
S
are incident to a specific block
B_{1}
of
z_{1}
, in fact
z_{1}
acts like a large component locally for
S
.
Since
z_{1}
(and therefore
B_{1}
) is only incident to
v_{2}^{3}

and
z_{2}
,
|V(z_{3})|\geq 11
, and
|V(z_{1})\cup V(z_{2})|\geq 11
, by applying the local
4
-Matching Lemma to
V(z_{1})\cup V(z_{2})
there must be a
3
-Matching from
V(z_{2})
to some nodes of
S
not on
P
. This is true since
z_{2}
is only incident to
z_{1}
,
v_{2}^{3}
and some other nodes of
S
not on
P
.
Let these outgoing edges from
z_{2}
to other nodes of
S
not on
P
be
e^{\prime},e^{\prime\prime},e^{\prime\prime\prime}
.
But then
P_{3k}
together with
e
, the edge
z_{1}z_{2}
from
P
and one of the edges of
\{e^{\prime},e^{\prime\prime},e^{\prime\prime\prime}\}
is a longer gluing path since
\{e^{\prime},e^{\prime\prime},e^{\prime\prime\prime}\}
is a
3
-matching incident to the
4
-cycle
z_{2}
. Hence, we can make progress.
Thus, from now on, we assume that both
z_{1},z_{2}
are
\mathcal{C}_{4}
.

We next prove that neither
z_{2}
nor
z_{1}
can be cut nodes, i.e., they are both only adjacent to nodes of
S
, the segment containing
P
.
Assume the contrary and that
z_{2}
is a cut node. The other case with
z_{1}
is analogous.
Hence,
z_{2}
is part of some other segment
S^{\prime}
.
First note that
S
contains at least
16
vertices, as otherwise either
v_{2}^{3}
is a cut vertex, which is a contradiction, or
S
contains less than
6
components and hence must contain a short heavy cycle.
Hence,
S^{\prime}
contains at most
15
vertices, as otherwise
z_{2}
would be a large
\mathcal{C}_{4}
cut, a contradiction.

S^{\prime}
only consists of components that are
\mathcal{C}_{4}
or large, as otherwise there must be a short heavy cycle in
S^{\prime}
and we can make progress.
Hence,
S^{\prime}
contains at most
4
components, of which one is
z_{2}
.
We can view
K
as a single
(3,1)
-merge since
z_{3}
acts equivalently as a
C_{4}
that is not shortcut.
Hence, we can apply
Lemma

41
to
S
,
S^{\prime}
and
K
and obtain that there is a good merge possible and we can make progress.
Hence, neither
z_{2}
nor
z_{1}
is a cut node.

If neither
z_{2}
nor
z_{1}
has an outgoing edge to a node of
S
not on
P
, then
v_{2}^{3}
is a cut node, a contradiction.
Hence, assume without loss of generality that
z_{1}
has an outgoing edge to some node
z
of
S
not on
P
.
If one of
\{v_{1}^{2},v_{3}^{2},v_{1}^{1},v_{3}^{1}\}
has an outgoing edge to a node
z^{\prime}
of
S
not on
P
, then we can extend the gluing path
P
by one and we make progress.
Hence, we assume this is not the case.
If also
z_{2}
has an outgoing edge to some node
z^{\prime}\neq z
, then we can apply
Lemma

52
and we can make progress.
In particular this case happens if
z_{2}
has an outgoing edge and
z_{1}
has outgoing edges to at least two distinct nodes of
S
not on
P
, or if
z_{2}
has outgoing edges to at least two distinct nodes of
S
not on
P
.

Hence, assume first that
z_{2}
has an outgoing edge. If
Lemma

52
can not be applied, this means that
z_{1}
and
z_{2}
are only incident to
z
.
If
z
is not a
\mathcal{C}_{4}
, we can interchange
z_{1}
with
z
and obtain another gluing path of length
k-1
, which ends at a node that is not a
\mathcal{C}_{4}
. But then we can do the same as before where we assumed that
z_{1}
is not a
\mathcal{C}_{4}
and make progress. Hence, we know that
z
is a
\mathcal{C}_{4}
.
Let
v_{1}-v_{2}-v_{3}-v_{4}-v_{1}
be the cycle of
z
and let
e_{1}
be the edge from
z_{1}
to
z
and let this edge be incident to
v_{1}
.
Further, let
e_{2}
be the edge from
z_{2}
to
z
. Recall that
e_{1}
is incident to either
v_{1}^{1}
or
v_{3}^{1}
and
e_{1}
is incident to either
v_{1}^{2}
or
v_{3}^{2}
.
If
e_{2}
is incident to either
v_{2}
or
v_{4}
, we obtain a longer gluing path and hence make progress.
Otherwise,
e_{2}
is incident to either
v_{1}
or
v_{3}
.
Since
P_{2k}
together with
e_{2}
is also a gluing path of length
k-1
, we know that we can make progress, or w.l.o.g.
v_{2}
is also incident to
v_{2}^{3}
, by applying
Lemma

48
and the previous argumentation in this proof for
z_{1}
.
Since we can not apply
Lemma

52
, none of the nodes of
z_{1},z_{2},z
is incident to some other component of
S
not on
P
. But then
v_{2}^{3}
is a cut vertex, a contradiction.

Hence,
z_{2}
does not have an outgoing edge to some node of
S
not on
P
, which implies that
z_{2}
is only incident to
v_{2}^{3}
and
z_{1}
.
Let
y_{1},...,y_{j}
be the nodes of
S
to which
z_{1}
is adjacent to.
Note to each
y_{i}
there is a gluing path of length
k-1
using
P_{3k}
,
e
and the edge from
z_{1}
to
y_{i}
.
Hence, similar to before we can argue that each
y_{i}
has to be a
\mathcal{C}_{4}
, as otherwise we can make progress.
But now we can apply the same argumentation that we did for
z_{2}
or
z_{1}
, respectively, to each
y_{i}
.
In particular, each
y_{i}
is only incident to
v_{2}^{3}
and
z_{1}
, as otherwise we either have a longer gluing path, a good merge or we can apply
Lemma

52
and make progress.
But then this implies that
v_{2}^{3}
is a cut vertex, a contradiction.
This finishes the proof.
∎

Lemma 54
.

Let
P=z_{k}-z_{k-1}-...-z_{3}-z_{2}-z_{1a}
be a gluing path of length
k-1\geq 4
and let
P^{\prime}=z_{k}-z_{k-1}-...-z_{3}-z_{2}-z_{1b}
be another gluing path of length
k-1\geq 4
such that the first
k-1
nodes are the same in
P
and
P^{\prime}
but the remaining
2
nodes are distinct, i.e.,
z_{1a}\neq z_{1b}
.
Then, we can make progress.

Proof.

Let
S
be the segment containing
P
and
P^{\prime}
.
Throughout the proof we assume we can not make progress and obtain some properties, which we highlight in a bullet point list. We first apply
Lemma

48
to both
z_{1a}
and
z_{1b}
to obtain the edges
e_{a}=v_{2}^{1b}u_{a}
and
e_{b}=v_{2}^{1b}u_{b}
, where
u_{a}\in\{v_{2}^{3},v_{4}^{3}\}
and
u_{b}\in\{v_{2}^{3},v_{4}^{3}\}
.
An illustration is given in
Figure

8
.

By
Lemma

53
we know that
z_{3}
is a
\mathcal{C}_{4}
, as otherwise we can make progress.
We next claim that is a
\mathcal{C}_{4}
.
Next, assume that
z_{2}
is not a
\mathcal{C}_{4}
.
By
Lemma

49
,
z_{2}
is large or complex. If
z_{2}
is a large component, then there is a cycle
z_{3}-z_{1a}-z_{2}-z_{1b}-z_{3}
that is a single
(4,1)
-merge, a contradiction. Hence, assume that
z_{2}
is complex.
Then either
z_{3}-z_{2}-z_{1b}
is a short heavy cycle (if
v_{1}^{2}
or
v_{2}^{2}
do not belong to the same block) or
z_{3}-z_{1a}-z_{2}-z_{1b}-z_{3}
is a single
(4,1)
-merge (if
v_{1}^{2}
and
v_{2}^{2}
belong to the same block) and hence, in either case, we make progress.
Furthermore, by
Lemma

49
,
z_{1a}
and
z_{1b}
are either a
\mathcal{C}_{4}
, large or complex. Therefore, we have the first properties:

•

z_{2}
and
z_{3}
are
\mathcal{C}_{4}
.

•

z_{1a}
and
z_{1b}
are either a
\mathcal{C}_{4}
, large or complex.

Figure 8
:
Example for situation in
Lemma

54
.

Next, we show that neither
z_{3}
nor
z_{2}
can be a cut node in
\bm{\hat{}}{G}_{H}
. Otherwise, assume w.l.o.g. that
z_{2}
is a cut node and let
S_{2}\neq S
be another segment of which
z_{2}
is part of.
Note that since
G
is structured and
z_{2}
is a
\mathcal{C}_{4}
and a cut node, the number of components in
S_{2}
is at most
4
. Otherwise
z_{2}
is a large cut
\mathcal{C}_{4}
since
S
contains a huge component by assumption, a contradiction.
Furthermore, there is no other segment besides
S_{2}
containing any of the nodes of
S_{2}
. Otherwise, either there is a segment containing only two
\mathcal{C}_{4}
’s (which implies a merge of two
\mathcal{C}_{4}
’s into one
\mathcal{C}_{8}
, by the
3
-Matching
Lemma

4
), or again
z_{2}
is a large cut
\mathcal{C}_{4}
).
If
S_{2}
contains a complex component, then there exists a cycle of length at most
4
in
S_{2}
that covers at least one bridge of the complex component. Hence, by
Lemma

39
, we can make progress.
Similarly we can make progress if
S_{2}
contains a component that is a
\mathcal{C}_{i}
for
5\leq i\leq 8
.
Hence,
S_{2}
only consists of components that are large or a
\mathcal{C}_{4}
.
Note that
P_{13}\cup e_{a}
is a single
(3,1)
-merge containing
z_{2}
.
But then by
Lemma

41
, we can find a double
(k,j)
-merge containing
z_{2}
for some
k\geq 5
and
k-j\geq 4
.
Hence, we can make progress according to
Lemma

39
.
Therefore, we have the following additional property.

•

Neither
z_{3}
nor
z_{2}
can be a cut node in
\bm{\hat{}}{G}_{H}
.

Let
S_{1}
be the subset of nodes of
S
not on
P\setminus\{z_{1a}\}
that are adjacent to
\{v_{2}^{2},v_{4}^{2}\}
. Note that
z_{1a},z_{1b}\in S_{1}
.
We next show that the set
\{v_{2}^{2},v_{4}^{2},v_{2}^{3},v_{4}^{3}\}
separates
S_{1}
from
P\setminus S_{1}
.
We make a case distinction on where
z_{1a}
can be incident to. The statement for all other nodes in
S_{1}
is analogous.

First, assume
z_{1a}
has an outgoing edge
e
to some node
z\in S
with
z\notin P\cup S_{1}
.
If
e
is incident to
v_{2}^{1a}
or
v_{4}^{1a}
, we can clearly extend
P
to a longer gluing path
P^{\prime\prime}
using
e
. Otherwise, if
e
is incident to
v_{1}^{1a}
or
v_{3}^{1a}
, then there are two gluing paths
P_{1}=z_{k}-z_{k-1}-...-z_{3}-z_{1b}-z_{2}
and
P_{2}=z_{k}-z_{k-1}-...-z_{3}-z_{1a}-z
of length
k-1
that satisfy the requirements of
Lemma

52
, and hence we can make progress.
Hence, both
z_{1a}
and
z_{1b}
are only incident in
S
to nodes of
P
.

If
z_{1a}
is incident to a node
z_{i}\in P
with
i\geq 5
, then this edge together with
P_{1ai}
implies a single
(5,j)
-merge for some
j\leq 2
, and hence by
Lemma

39
, we can make progress.
Let
C_{b}
be the cycle
P^{\prime}_{1b3}\cup\{e_{b}\}
.
If
z_{1a}
is incident to
z_{4}
via some edge
e
, we consider two cases:
If
e
is incident to one of
\{v_{1}^{1a},v_{3}^{1a}\}
then define
C_{a}=P_{34}\cup\{e,e_{a}\}
and observe that
X=C_{a}\cup C_{b}
defines a double
(5,1)
-merge. Hence, we can make progress according to
Lemma

39
.
Otherwise, if
e
is incident to
\{v_{2}^{1a},v_{4}^{1a}\}
then define
C_{a}=P_{34}\cup\{e,P_{12}\}
and observe that
X=C_{a}\cup C_{b}
defines a double
(5,1)
-merge. Hence, we can make progress according to
Lemma

39
.

If
z_{1a}
is incident to either
v_{1}^{3}
or
v_{3}^{3}
via some edge
e
, we show that we can make progress:
If
z_{1a}
is large then
P_{1a3}\cup e
is a single
(3,0)
-merge.
Assume
z_{1a}
is complex.
P_{1a3}\cup e_{a}
is not a short heavy cycle if the edge of
z_{2}z_{1a}
and the edge
e_{a}
are incident to the same block of
z_{1a}
. But then
P_{1a3}\cup e
is either a short heavy cycle or a single
(3,0)
-merge.
Hence, in either case,
Lemma

39
implies that we can make progress.
Otherwise,
z_{1a}
is a
\mathcal{C}_{4}
.
If
e
is incident to one of
\{v_{1}^{1a},v_{3}^{1a}\}
, then either the two edges
e
and
e_{a}
can be used to turn
z_{3}
and
z_{1a}
into one
\mathcal{C}_{8}
(if
e
is incident to
v_{1}^{1a}
or
v_{3}^{1a}
), a contradiction, or there is a single
(3,0)
merge (if
e
is incident to
v_{2}^{1a}
or
v_{4}^{1a}
).

Finally, if
z_{1a}
is incident to either
v_{1}^{2}
or
v_{3}^{2}
via some edge
e
, we show that we can make progress and find a longer gluing path
z_{k}-...-z_{3}-z_{1b}-z_{2}-z_{1a}
using the edges
e_{b}
and
e
.

Hence, we have the following additional property:

•

No node of
S_{1}
has an edge to a node of
S
not in
P\cup S_{1}
. Furthermore, the set
\{v_{2}^{2},v_{4}^{2},v_{2}^{3},v_{4}^{3}\}
separates
S_{1}
from
P\setminus S_{1}
.

We next show that
N(v_{1}^{2}),N(v_{3}^{2})\subseteq\{v_{2}^{2},v_{4}^{2},v_{2}^{3},v_{4}^{3}\}
, where
N(v)
is the set of neighbors in
G
of some vertex
v\in V(G)
.

Assume there is an edge
e
incident to one of
\{v_{3}^{2},v_{1}^{2}\}
which is incident to a vertex
u\notin\{v_{2}^{2},v_{4}^{2},v_{2}^{3},v_{4}^{3}\}
.
Recall that
z_{2}
is not a cut node, hence
u
is a vertex of
S
.
If
u
is part of a node
z
of
S
not on
P
or
P^{\prime}
, then we have a longer gluing path
z_{k}-...-z_{3}-z_{1a}-z_{2}-z
or
z_{k}-...-z_{3}-z_{1b}-z_{2}-z
. Hence, this is not the case.
If
u\in\{v_{1}^{3},v_{3}^{3}\}
, then there is a single
(3,0)
-merge using
P_{1a2}
,
e_{a}
and
e
.
If
u\in V(z_{i})
for some
i\geq 4
, there is a single
(i,1)
-merge.
Hence, in any case we can make progress according to
Lemma

39
.

Finally, assume
e=v_{1}^{2}v_{3}^{2}\in E(G)
.
W.l.o.g. we assume that the edge
e_{a}^{1}=z_{2}z_{1a}
of
P
is incident to
v_{2}^{2}
.
If the edge
e_{b}^{1}=z_{2}z_{1b}
of
P^{\prime}
is incident to
v_{2}^{4}
, then there is a single
(4,1)
-merge using the edges
e_{a},e_{a}^{1},e_{b}^{1},e_{b}
and we can make progress according to
Lemma

39
(we can replace
z_{2}
by
v_{2}^{2}-v_{1}^{2}-v_{3}^{2}-v_{4}^{2}
).
Hence,
e_{b}^{1}
has to be incident to
v_{2}^{2}
.
There has to be an edge
e_{2}^{4}
incident to
v_{2}^{4}
and incident to either
z_{1a}
or
z_{1b}
.
Otherwise
\{v_{2}^{2},v_{2}^{3},v_{4}^{3}\}
is a large
3
-vertex cut, a contradiction to
G
being structured.
W.l.o.g. assume that
e_{2}^{4}
is incident to
z_{1b}
.
If
e_{2}^{4}
is incident to one of
\{v_{2}^{1b},v_{4}^{1b}\}
then we can merge the two
\mathcal{C}_{4}

z_{2}
and
z_{1b}
into a single
\mathcal{C}_{8}
, a contradiction.
Hence,
e_{2}^{4}
is incident to one of
\{v_{1}^{1b},v_{3}^{1b}\}
.
But then there is a single
(4,1)
-merge using the edges
e_{2}^{4},e_{b},e_{a},e_{a}^{1}
and using the Hamiltonian Path
v_{2}^{2}-v_{3}^{2}-v_{1}^{2}-v_{4}^{2}
inside
z_{2}
. Hence, we can make progress according to
Lemma

39
.

Hence, we have the following additional property:

•

We have
N(v_{1}^{2}),N(v_{3}^{2})\subseteq\{v_{2}^{2},v_{4}^{2},v_{2}^{3},v_{4}^{3}\}
.

Let
Y=\{v_{2}^{2},v_{4}^{2},v_{2}^{3},v_{4}^{3}\}
.
By the above properties, we have that
Y
is a separator separating
V_{1}
from
V_{2}\coloneqq V(G)\setminus(V_{1}\cup Y)
, where
V_{1}
contains all vertices of
V(G)\setminus Y
that are in some connected component of
G\setminus Y
containing at least one vertex of
V(S_{1})\cup\{v_{1}^{2},v_{3}^{2}\}
.
Furthermore, let
V_{1}^{\prime}\coloneqq V_{1}\setminus\{v_{1}^{2},v_{3}^{2}\}
.
From the condition of this section, we know that
S
contains at least
5
nodes and at least one component
L
that is huge.
We first assume that this
L
is contained in
V_{2}
. We later show how the case that
L
is contained in
V_{1}
is essentially the same.

Since
Y
is a
4
-vertex cut and
G
does not contain large
4
-vertex cuts, we know that
|V_{1}|\leq 23
, since
|V_{2}|\geq 32
as it contains
L
.
Hence, in
V_{1}^{\prime}
there are at most
5
components if every component is a
\mathcal{C}_{4}
or at most 4 components if one of them is complex or large.
If
V_{1}
contains a complex component, then it is easy to see that there must be a short heavy cycle covering a bridge of that complex component: To see this, observe first that
V_{1}^{\prime}
contains at most
4
components and
V_{1}^{\prime}
is only connected to components of
V_{1}^{\prime}
and
z_{2}
and
z_{3}
.
Further,
\{z_{2},z_{3}\}
is a
2
-node cut in
S
and there is an edge between
z_{2}
and
z_{3}
in
S
.
Hence, any simple cycle covering a bridge of the complex component has length at most 6 (and hence is short). Since there is at least one such cycle within the component graph induced by the components of
V_{1}\cup V(z_{2})\cup V(z_{3})
covering a bridge, we can make progress by
Lemma

39
.
Hence,
V_{1}
only contains components that are large or
\mathcal{C}_{4}
.

We now show that
Y
is a large
4
-vertex cut (cf.
Definition

23
), a contradiction to
G
being structured.
We make a case distinction on whether
V_{1}^{\prime}
contains only
2
components that are
\mathcal{C}_{4}
(in which case
z_{1a}
and
z_{1b}
are
\mathcal{C}_{4}
), or not.
Note that in the latter case, in which
V_{1}^{\prime}
either contains
3
components or
2
components of which at least one is large, we have
|V_{1}^{\prime}|\geq 12
.

Case 1:
First consider the latter case in which
|V_{1}^{\prime}|\geq 12
.
Let
\mathsf{OPT}_{1}^{\prime}
be an optimum solution for
G[V_{1}^{\prime}\cup Y]|Y
and let
\mathsf{OPT}_{1}
be an optimum solution for
G[V_{1}\cup Y]|Y
.
Note that any optimum solution must pick at least
|\mathsf{OPT}_{1}^{\prime}|+4\geq 17
edges from within
G[V_{1}\cup Y]|Y
and
|\mathsf{OPT}_{1}|\geq 17
,
since
G[V_{1}]
has at least three connected components:
V_{1}^{\prime}
,
v_{1}^{2}
, and
v_{3}^{2}
.
Since
|V_{1}^{\prime}|\geq 12
, any feasible solution must pick at least
13
edges from within
G[V_{1}^{\prime}\cup Y]\mid Y
, and two edges incident to
v_{1}^{2}
, and
v_{3}^{2}
, respectively.
These are disjoint, and hence this sums up to
|\mathsf{OPT}_{1}^{\prime}|+4\geq 17
edges, since
|V_{1}^{\prime}|\geq 12
.
Recall that
\{v_{2}^{2},v_{4}^{2}\}\subseteq N(v_{3}^{2})
and
\{v_{2}^{2},v_{4}^{2},v_{2}^{3}\}\subseteq N(v_{1}^{2})
.
We now let
Z_{2}=\{v_{3}^{2}v_{2}^{2},v_{3}^{2}v_{4}^{2},v_{1}^{2}v_{4}^{2},v_{1}^{2}v_{2}^{3}\}
.
Note that
Z=OPT_{1}^{\prime}\cup Z_{2}
contains at most
2
connected components on the vertex set
V_{Z}=V_{1}\cup Y
, since
\{v_{2}^{2},v_{4}^{2},v_{2}^{3}\}
is connected via
v_{3}^{2}
and
v_{1}^{2}
and
V_{1}^{\prime}
is connected using the edges of
\mathsf{OPT}_{1}^{\prime}
and also connected to at least one vertex of
Y
.
Let
C_{1}
be the connected component containing
\{v_{2}^{2},v_{4}^{2},v_{2}^{3}\}
and
C_{2}
be the connected component containing
v_{4}^{3}
.
If
C_{2}=\{v_{4}^{3}\}
, there must be an edge from
C_{1}
to
C_{2}
in
G
as otherwise
Y\setminus\{v_{4}^{3}\}
is a large
3
-vertex cut, a contradiction. If
C_{2}\neq\{v_{4}^{3}\}
there must be an edge from
C_{2}
to
C_{1}
as otherwise
v_{4}^{3}
is a cut vertex, a contradiction.
In either case, let
e
be such an edge.
Now observe that adding
e
to
Z
, makes
V_{1}
and
Y
connected.
Now let
X_{2}
be any solution for
(G\setminus V_{1})|Y
. Note that in the same way as before (and as in the proof of
Lemma

97
), there is an edge set
Z^{\prime}
with
|Z^{\prime}|\leq 3
such that
X_{2}\cup Z\cup Z^{\prime}\cup\{e\}
is 2EC.
However, since
|Z^{\prime}\cup\{e\}|\leq 4
,
|Z|\geq 17
and
|\mathsf{OPT}_{1}|\geq|Z|+4
, we have
|Z\cup Z^{\prime}\cup\{e\}|\leq\alpha\mathsf{OPT}_{1}
, which implies that
Y
is a large
4
-vertex cut (cf. second case of
Definition

23
), a contradiction.

Case 2:
Now consider the other case, i.e.,
z_{1a}
and
z_{1b}
are
\mathcal{C}_{4}
.
We use the same definitions we used in the previous case.
The main difference is that we only have
|\mathsf{OPT}_{1}^{\prime}|\geq 9
and hence any optimum solution must pick at least
|\mathsf{OPT}_{1}^{\prime}|+4\geq 13
edges from within
G[V_{1}\cup Y]
.
We do mostly the same as in the previous case, but need to argue that we can find an edge set
Z
with
|Z|\leq 13+3=16
edges such that
X_{2}\cup Z
is 2EC.
This then implies that
Y
is a large
4
-vertex cut (cf. second case of
Definition

23
),, a contradiction, as desired.
Recall that
e_{a}^{1}
is incident to
v_{1}^{1a}
and
z_{2}
,
e_{a}
is incident to
v_{2}^{1a}
and
z_{3}
,
e_{b}^{1}
is incident to
v_{1}^{1b}
and
z_{2}
and
e_{b}
is incident to
v_{2}^{1b}
and
z_{3}
.
W.l.o.g. we assume that
e_{a}^{1}
is incident to
v_{2}^{2}
. We make a case distinction on where
e_{a},e_{b}
and
e_{b}^{1}
are incident to.

Case 2.1:
If
e_{a}
and
e_{b}
are both incident to
v_{2}^{3}
, then set

Z_{1}=\Big(E(z_{1a})\setminus\{v_{1}^{1a}v_{2}^{1a}\}\Big)\cup\Big(E(z_{1b})\setminus\{v_{1}^{1b}v_{2}^{1b}\}\Big)\cup\{e_{a},e_{b},e_{a}^{1},e_{b}^{1},v_{3}^{2}v_{2}^{2},v_{3}^{2}v_{4}^{2},v_{1}^{2}v_{4}^{2},v_{1}^{2}v_{2}^{3}\}

and observe that
|Z_{1}|=14
and that all vertices of
(V_{1}\cup Y)\setminus v_{4}^{3}
are in a single 2EC component of
Z_{1}
.
Similar to before, there must be an edge
e
incident to
v_{4}^{3}
and
V_{1}
.
Since
(V_{1}\cup Y)\setminus v_{4}^{3}
is already 2EC, one can easily check that there is an edge set
Z_{2}
with
|Z_{2}|\leq 1
such that
X_{2}\cup Z_{1}\cup\{e\}\cup Z_{2}
is 2EC.
Further, observe that
|Z_{1}\cup\{e\}\cup Z_{2}|\leq 16
as desired.

Case 2.2:
If
e_{a}
and
e_{b}
are both incident to
v_{4}^{3}
, then set

Z_{1}=\Big(E(z_{1a})\setminus\{v_{1}^{1a}v_{2}^{1a}\}\Big)\cup\Big(E(z_{1b})\setminus\{v_{1}^{1b}v_{2}^{1b}\}\Big)\cup\{e_{a},e_{b},e_{a}^{1},e_{b}^{1}\}\cup E(z_{2})

and observe that
|Z_{1}|=14
and that all vertices of
(V_{1}\cup Y)\setminus v_{2}^{3}
are in a single 2EC component of
Z_{1}
.
Now following the analogous steps as in Case 2.1, we obtain the result.

Case 2.3:
If neither Case 2.1 nor Case 2.2 applies, set

Z_{1}=\Big(E(z_{1a})\setminus\{v_{1}^{1a}v_{2}^{1a}\}\Big)\cup\Big(E(z_{1b})\setminus\{v_{1}^{1b}v_{2}^{1b}\}\Big)\cup\{e_{a},e_{b},e_{a}^{1},e_{b}^{1},v_{3}^{2}v_{2}^{2},v_{3}^{2}v_{4}^{2},v_{1}^{2}v_{4}^{2},v_{1}^{2}v_{2}^{3}\}

and observe that
|Z_{1}|=14
and that all vertices of
Y\setminus\{v_{4}^{3}\}
are in a single 2EC component of
Z_{1}
, and that all vertices of
V_{1}\cup Y
are connected.
Hence, similar to above, there must be an edge
e
such that
X_{2}\cup Z_{1}\cup\{e\}
is 2EC.
Further, observe that
|Z_{1}\cup\{e\}|\leq 15
as desired.

It remains to consider the case that
L
is part of
V_{1}
. Assume w.l.o.g. that
z_{1a}
is huge.
Observe that no node of
S_{1}
has an edge incident to
z_{1a}
, as if such an edge
e
exists, we can extend the gluing path
P
using this edge, unless
L
is complex and
e_{a}^{1}
and
e
are incident to the same vertex
v
of the complex component
L
, and
v
is not part of any block.
But then the three edges
P_{23},e_{a}^{1}
and
e_{a}
are a short heavy cycle, since
e_{a}
and
e_{a}^{1}
are not incident to the same vertex of
L
and hence this cycle covers a bridge of
L
.

Hence, assume that there is no edge from
L
to any other node of
S_{1}
.
Now observe that
Y
also separates
L
from
Y
and
S^{\prime}\coloneqq S\setminus(Y\cup\{L\})
.
Note that
S^{\prime}
contains at least the three nodes
z_{1b}
,
z_{4}
, and
z_{5}
, which contain in total at least
12
vertices and the two vertices
v_{1}^{2}
and
v_{3}^{2}
.
Now we have the same situation as in Case 1 in which
L
is in
V_{2}
and
|V_{1}|\geq 12
. Hence, we can make progress.
This finishes the proof of the lemma.
∎

Next, we show that
z_{1}
and
z_{2}
have no outgoing edges in
S
, i.e., edges to nodes of
S\setminus P
, where
P
is the gluing path.

Lemma 55
.

Let
P=z_{k}-z_{k-1}-...-z_{3}-z_{2}-z_{1}
be a gluing path of length
k-1\geq 4
. If
z_{1}
or
z_{2}
has an outgoing edge, i.e., an edge to some node of
S
not on
P
, then we can make progress.

Proof.

Clearly, if there is an edge from
v_{2}^{1}
or
v_{4}^{1}
to some node not on
P
, we can make progress.
By
Lemma

48
, we know that the edge
e_{13}
has to exist from
v_{2}^{1}
to one of
\{v_{2}^{3},v_{4}^{3}\}
.
Hence, if there is an edge from
v_{3}^{1}
or
v_{1}^{1}
to some node
z
not on
P
, we have two gluing paths
P_{1}=z_{k}-...-z_{3}-z_{1}-z
of length
k-1
and
P_{2}=z_{k}-...-z_{3}-z_{1}-z_{2}
of length
k-1
and hence
Lemma

54
implies that we can make progress.
Therefore,
z_{1}
has no outgoing edge to some node not on
P
.

Similarly, there can not be an edge from
v_{1}^{2}
or
v_{3}^{2}
to some node
z
not on
P
since we then have the longer gluing path
z_{k}-...-z_{3}-z_{1}-z_{2}-z
.
If there is an edge from
v_{2}^{2}
or
v_{4}^{2}
to some node
z
not on
P
, then we again have two gluing paths of length
k-1
that satisfy the conditions of
Lemma

54
and hence we can make progress.
∎

Figure 9
:
Example for situation in
Lemma

56
.

Lemma 56
.

Let
P=z_{k}-z_{k-1}-...-z_{3}-z_{2a}-z_{1a}
be a gluing path of length
k-1\geq 4
and let
P^{\prime}=z_{k}-z_{k-1}-...-z_{3}-z_{2b}
be another gluing path of length
k-2\geq 3
such that the first
k-2
nodes are the same in
P
and
P^{\prime}
but the remaining
3
nodes are all distinct, i.e.,
z_{1a}\neq z_{2b}\neq z_{2a}
.
Then, we can make progress.

Proof.

By
Lemma

48
, we know that the edge
e_{13}
has to exist from
v_{2}^{1a}
to one of
\{v_{2}^{3},v_{4}^{3}\}
.
Either
v_{2}^{2b}
or
v_{4}^{2b}
must have an edge
e
to a node
z\in S
with
z\neq z_{2b}
, by the 3-Matching
Lemma

4
. W.l.o.g. assume this edge is incident to
v_{2}^{2b}
.
If
z\notin P
, we have two gluing paths of length
k-1
satisfying the conditions of
Lemma

52
and we can make progress.
If
z=z_{i}
for some
i\geq 4
, we can make progress as follows:
We take
P_{1a3}
and
e_{13}
together with
e
and
P^{\prime}_{2ai}
and observe that this forms a double
(i+1,j)
-merge for some
j\leq 1
and hence by
Lemma

39
, we can make progress.

Hence,
e
is incident to
z_{3}
, since neither
z_{1}
nor
z_{2}
have an outgoing edge by
Lemma

55
(or we can make progress).
If
e
is incident to either
v_{3}^{3}
or
v_{1}^{3}
, then there is a double
(4,0)
-merge using
P_{1a3}
and
e_{13}
together with
P^{\prime}_{2b3}
and
e
, and hence, by
Lemma

39
, we can make progress.
Hence,
e
is incident to either
v_{2}^{3}
or
v_{4}^{3}
.
An illustration of this case is given in
Figure

9
.

Since
z_{2b}
can not be only connected to
z_{3}
in
S
(as then
z_{2b}
is not in the segment
S
we currently consider), either
v_{1}^{2b}
or
v_{3}^{2b}
must have an edge
e^{\prime}
to some node
z^{\prime}
.
Again, note that
z_{1a}\neq z^{\prime}\neq z_{2a}
by
Lemma

55
.

Let
e^{\prime\prime}
be the edge from
z_{3}
to
z_{2b}
on
P^{\prime}
. Observe that we can swap
e
with
e^{\prime\prime}
and we have the same conditions as in this lemma statement but
P^{\prime}
ends at
v_{2}^{2b}
instead of
v_{1}^{2b}
.
Let this other path be
P^{\prime\prime}
.
There must be an edge
f
to some node of
S
that is not
z_{3}
, otherwise
z_{2b}
is not in
S
.
Since we can interchange
P^{\prime}
and
P^{\prime\prime}
, no matter where
f
is incident to at
z_{2b}
, we can do the exact same case distinction as for
e
and observe that in any case we can make progress.
This finishes the proof.
∎

Next, we argue that a certain edge from
z_{1}
to
z_{4}
on the gluing path has to exist.

Lemma 57
.

Let
P=z_{k}-z_{k-1}-...-z_{1}
be a gluing path of length
k
. Then either
v_{3}^{1}
or
v_{1}^{1}
is adjacent to one of
\{v_{2}^{4},v_{4}^{4}\}
or we can make progress.

Proof.

By the previous lemmas, we know that the edge
e_{13}
from
v_{2}^{1}
to either
v_{2}^{3}
or
v_{4}^{3}
exists.
Further, neither
z_{1}
nor
z_{2}
has an edge to some node in
S
but not on
P
.

We first consider
z_{1}
.
If
z_{1}
is incident to an edge
e
that is incident to
z_{i}
on
P
for
i\geq 5
, then
P_{1i}
together with
e
forms a single
(i,j)
-merge for some
j\leq 2
, and hence we can make progress according to
Lemma

39
.
Assume
z_{1}
has an edge to
z_{4}
. Clearly, if
v_{3}^{1}
or
v_{1}^{1}
is adjacent to one of
\{v_{2}^{4},v_{4}^{4}\}
, we are done.
If
v_{3}^{1}
or
v_{1}^{1}
is adjacent to one of
\{v_{1}^{4},v_{3}^{4}\}
via some edge
e
, then
P_{14}
together with
e
forms a single
(4,1)
-merge and we can make progress according to
Lemma

39
.
If
v_{2}^{1}
or
v_{4}^{1}
is adjacent to
z_{4}
via some edge
e
, then
P_{14}
together with
e
forms a single
(4,1)
-merge, and we can make progress according to
Lemma

39
.

Next, consider
z_{2}
.
If
z_{2}
is incident to an edge
e
that is incident to
z_{i}
on
P
for
i\geq 5
, then
P_{1i}
together with
e
and
e_{13}
forms a double
(i,j)
-merge for some
j\leq 2
, and hence we can make progress according to
Lemma

39
.

Consider the case that
z_{2}
has an edge
e
to
z_{4}
.
Assume
e
is incident to
v_{2}^{2}
or
v_{2}^{4}
.
If
e
is incident to one of
\{v_{2}^{4},v_{4}^{4}\}
, then we can rearrange
P
to obtain the desired edge:
Consider
P^{\prime}=z_{k}-...-z_{4}-z_{3}-z_{1}-z_{2}
using the edge
e_{13}
from
z_{3}
to
z_{1}
.
Observe that
P^{\prime}
and
e
satisfy the lemma statement.
If
e
is incident to one of
\{v_{2}^{3},v_{4}^{1}\}
, then
P_{24}
together with
e
form a single
(3,0)
-merge, and we can make progress according to
Lemma

39
.
Next, assume
e
is incident to
v_{1}^{2}
or
v_{3}^{2}
.
If
e
is incident to one of
\{v_{1}^{4},v_{3}^{4}\}
via some edge
e
, then
P_{34}
,
e_{13}
,
P_{12}
and
e
together form a single
(4,1)
-merge and we can make progress according to
Lemma

39
.

Hence, if neither of the above conditions is met, then
z_{3}
is a cut node, a contradiction to
P
being a gluing path on the
2
-node-connected segment
S
containing
z_{1}
and
z_{2}
.
∎

Hence, from now on we assume that
v_{3}^{1}
is adjacent to one of
\{v_{2}^{4},v_{4}^{4}\}
.

Lemma 58
.

Let
P=z_{k}-z_{k-1}-...-z_{3}-z_{2}-z_{1}
be a gluing path of length
k-1\geq 4
.
Then either we can make progress or in
G
no vertex of the nodes
z_{1},z_{2},z_{3}
has an edge to a node
z
not on
P
.

Proof.

Let
e_{13}
and
e_{14}
