∎

Lemma 97
.

Let
k=4
.
If the condition in
Algorithm

5
in
Algorithm

5
holds and if both
H_{1}=\mathsf{RED}(G^{\prime}_{1})
and
H_{2}=\mathsf{RED}(G^{\prime}_{2})
are 2EC spanning subgraphs of
G^{\prime}_{1}
and
G^{\prime}_{2}
, respectively,
then there exists
F\subseteq E
with
|F|\leq 2k-2
such that
H=H_{1}\cup H_{2}\cup F
is a 2EC spanning subgraph of
G
.

Proof.

We show the existence of the desired edges
F=F_{1}\cup F_{2}
inductively.

We treat
H_{1}
as a subgraph of
G_{1}
and assume that
V(G_{1})=V(H_{1})
.
Start with
F_{1}=\emptyset
. Note that
H_{1}\cup F_{1}
contains at most
k
connected components, because
H_{1}
is 2EC for
G^{\prime}_{1}
.
For each
v_{i}\in\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{k}\}
, let
H_{1}(v_{i})
denote the connected component of
H_{1}\cup F_{1}
that contains
v_{i}
. Note that
H_{1}(v_{i})
can be composed of the single vertex
v_{i}
.

If
H_{1}\cup F_{1}
is not connected, let
v_{i}\in\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{k}\}
such that
H_{1}(v_{i})\neq(H_{1}\cup F_{1})
, and suppose that there is no edge between
H_{1}(v_{i})
and
(H_{1}\cup F_{1})\setminus H_{1}(v_{i})
in
G
.
If
H_{1}(v_{i})
is composed of the single vertex
v_{i}
, then
\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{k}\}\setminus\{v_{i}\}
is a large
3
-vertex cut in
G
, a contradiction to
Proposition

95
. Otherwise, that is,
H_{1}(v_{i})
consists of at least two vertices, then
v_{i}
is a cut vertex in
G
that separates
V(H_{1}(v_{i}))\setminus\{v_{i}\}
from
V\setminus V((H_{1}\cup F_{1})\setminus H_{1}(v_{i}))
, a contradiction to
Proposition

95
.
Thus, there is an edge
e
between
H_{1}(v_{i})
and
(H_{1}\cup F_{1})\setminus H_{1}(v_{i})
in
G
.
We now add
e
to
F_{1}
, and observe that this reduces the number of connected component of
H_{1}\cup F_{1}
by one.

Thus, after repeating this argument by at most
k-1
times, we showed the existence of a set of edges
F_{1}
with
|F_{1}|\leq k-1
such that
H_{1}\cup F_{1}
is connected and spans
V(G_{1})
.

By symmetry, we can prove the existence of edges
F_{2}
with
|F_{2}|\leq k-1
such that
H_{2}\cup F_{2}
is connected and spans
V(G_{2})
.
Finally, we set
F=F_{1}\cup F_{2}
, and have that
H_{1}\cup H_{2}\cup F
is 2EC and spans
V(G)
.
∎

Lemma 98
.

If the condition in
Algorithm

5
in
Algorithm

5
for
k=4
holds and if
H_{2}=\mathsf{RED}(G^{\prime}_{2})
is a 2EC spanning subgraph of
G^{\prime}_{2}
,
then
H^{\prime}=H_{2}\cup Z
is a 2EC spanning subgraph of
G
.

Proof.

Since for the
k
-vertex cut, Condition 2 of
Definition

23
applies and
H_{2}
is a 2ECSS of
G^{\prime}_{2}
, we conclude that
H^{\prime}
is a 2ECSS of
G
.
∎

Lemma 99
.

Let
G
be a 2ECSS instance. If every recursive call to
\mathsf{RED}(G^{\prime})
in
Algorithm

5
for
k=4
on input
G
satisfies (
1
), then (
1
) holds for input
G
.

Proof.

By
Proposition

95
, we have
|V(G)|\geq\frac{8}{\varepsilon}
. Thus, we need to show that

\mathsf{red}(G)\leq\alpha\cdot\mathsf{opt}(G)+f(G)=\alpha\cdot\mathsf{opt}(G)+4\varepsilon\cdot|V(G)|-16\ .

We first consider the case where the condition in
Algorithm

5
holds, that is,
there exists a partition
(V_{1},V_{2})
of
V\setminus\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{k}\}
with
E[V_{1},V_{2}]=\emptyset
and

\lceil\frac{2k-2}{\alpha-1}\rceil-1\leq|V_{1}|\leq|V_{2}|
.
Note that
\mathsf{opt}(G^{\prime}_{1})+\mathsf{opt}(G^{\prime}_{2})\leq\mathsf{opt}(G)
and
|V(G^{\prime}_{1})|+|V(G^{\prime}_{2})|\leq|V(G)|
.
We distinguish three cases.

(
|V_{2}|\leq\frac{8}{\varepsilon}
and
|V_{1}|\leq\frac{8}{\varepsilon}
).

By (
1
), we have
H_{i}=\mathsf{opt}(G^{\prime}_{i})
for all
i\in\{1,2\}
. Thus

\displaystyle\mathsf{red}(G)

\displaystyle=|H_{1}|+|H_{2}|+|F|

\displaystyle\leq\mathsf{opt}(G^{\prime}_{1})+\mathsf{opt}(G^{\prime}_{2})+(2k-2)

\displaystyle\leq\alpha\mathsf{opt}(G)+4\varepsilon\cdot|V(G)|-16=\alpha\mathsf{opt}(G)+f(G)\ ,

because
\alpha\geq 1
and
|V(G)|\geq\frac{8}{\varepsilon}
gives
2k-2\leq 16\leq 4\varepsilon\cdot|V(G)|-16
.

(
|V_{2}|>\frac{8}{\varepsilon}
and
|V_{1}|\leq\frac{8}{\varepsilon}
).

By (
1
), we have
H_{1}=\mathsf{opt}(G^{\prime}_{1})
and
|H_{2}|\leq\alpha\cdot\mathsf{opt}(G^{\prime}_{2})+f(G^{\prime}_{2})
. Then,

\mathsf{red}(G)=|H_{1}|+|H_{2}|+|F|\leq\mathsf{opt}(G^{\prime}_{1})+\alpha\cdot\mathsf{opt}(G^{\prime}_{2})+f(G^{\prime}_{2})+(2k-2)\leq\alpha\cdot\mathsf{opt}(G)+f(G)\ ,

where we use that
f(G^{\prime}_{2})\leq f(G)
and
\mathsf{opt}(G^{\prime}_{1})\geq\frac{2k-2}{\alpha-1}
(
Lemma

96
).

(
|V_{2}|>\frac{8}{\varepsilon}
and
|V_{1}|>\frac{8}{\varepsilon}
).

By (
1
), we have

\displaystyle\mathsf{red}(G)

\displaystyle=|H_{1}|+|H_{2}|+|F|

\displaystyle\leq\alpha\cdot\mathsf{opt}(G^{\prime}_{1})+4\varepsilon\cdot|V(G^{\prime}_{1})|-16+\alpha\cdot\mathsf{opt}(G^{\prime}_{2})+4\varepsilon\cdot|V(G^{\prime}_{2})|-16+(2k-2)

\displaystyle\leq\alpha\cdot\mathsf{opt}(G)+4\varepsilon\cdot|V(G)|-16=\alpha\cdot\mathsf{opt}(G)+f(G)\ ,

because
2k-2\leq 16
.

Next, we consider the case where the condition in
Algorithm

5
holds.
Note that
\mathsf{opt}(G^{\prime}_{1})+\mathsf{opt}(G^{\prime}_{2})\leq\mathsf{opt}(G)
and
|V(G^{\prime}_{1})|+|V(G^{\prime}_{2})|\leq|V(G)|
.
Since Condition 1 in
Definition

23
does not hold,
|V_{1}|\leq\lceil\frac{2k-2}{\alpha-1}\rceil-2\leq\frac{8}{\varepsilon}
as
\varepsilon\leq\frac{1}{100}
. Thus, we distinguish two cases.

(
|V_{2}|\leq\frac{8}{\varepsilon}
and
|V_{1}|\leq\frac{8}{\varepsilon}
).

We have
|Z|\leq\alpha\cdot\mathsf{opt}(G^{\prime}_{1})
, and by (
1
), we have
H_{2}=\mathsf{opt}(G^{\prime}_{2})
. Thus,

\displaystyle\mathsf{red}(G)

\displaystyle=|Z|+|H_{2}|\leq\alpha\mathsf{opt}(G^{\prime}_{1})+\mathsf{opt}(G^{\prime}_{2})

\displaystyle\leq\alpha\mathsf{opt}(G)+4\varepsilon\cdot|V(G)|-16=\alpha\mathsf{opt}(G)+f(G)\ ,

because
\alpha\geq 1
and
|V(G)|\geq\frac{8}{\varepsilon}
gives
0\leq 4\varepsilon\cdot|V(G)|-16
.

(
|V_{2}|>\frac{8}{\varepsilon}
and
|V_{1}|\leq\frac{8}{\varepsilon}
).

We have
|Z|\leq\alpha\cdot\mathsf{opt}(G^{\prime}_{1})
, and by (
1
), we have
|H_{2}|\leq\alpha\cdot\mathsf{opt}(G^{\prime}_{2})+f(G^{\prime}_{2})
. Thus,

\displaystyle\mathsf{red}(G)

\displaystyle=|Z|+|H_{2}|\leq\alpha\cdot\mathsf{opt}(G^{\prime}_{1})+\alpha\cdot\mathsf{opt}(G^{\prime}_{2})+f(G^{\prime}_{2})

\displaystyle\leq\alpha\cdot\mathsf{opt}(G)+f(G)\ ,

where we use that
f(G^{\prime}_{2})\leq f(G)
.

This completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

A.3
Proof of
Lemma

3

In this final subsection, we prove
Lemma

3
. The statement is implied by the following three lemmas.
We first show that
\mathsf{RED}(G)
runs in polynomial time. The proof is similar to the corresponding proof in
[
GGJ23
]
.

Lemma 100

(running time)
.

For any 2ECSS instance
G
,
\mathsf{RED}(G)
runs in polynomial time in
|V(G)|
if
\mathsf{ALG}(G)
runs in polynomial time in
|V(G)|
.

Proof.

Let
n=|V(G)|
and
m=|V(G)|
, and
\sigma=n^{2}+m^{2}
be the size of the problem. We first argue that every non-recursive step of
Algorithms

1
,
2
,
3
,
4
and
5
can be performed in time polynomial in
\sigma
.
Clearly, all computations performed in
Algorithm

1
can be performed in polynomial time in
\sigma
. In particular, it is possible to check in polynomial-time whether
G
contains an
\alpha
-contractible subgraph with at most
\frac{8}{\varepsilon}
vertices: Enumerate over all subsets
W
of vertices of the desired size; for each such
W
, one can compute in polynomial time a minimum-size 2ECSS
\mathsf{OPT}^{\prime}
of
G[W]
, if any such subgraph exists. Furthermore, one can compute in polynomial time a maximum cardinality subset of edges
F
with endpoints in
W
such that
G\setminus F
is 2EC. Then, it is sufficient to compare the size of
\mathsf{OPT}^{\prime}
, if exists, with
|E(G[W])\setminus F|
.

We now consider
Algorithm

3
.
We already argued that the computation in
Algorithm

3
can be executed in polynomial time in
\sigma
. Moreover, determining the
\mathsf{C2}
subcases in
Algorithms

3
,
3
and
3
and selecting a desired
\mathsf{OPT}_{1}^{\mathsf{C2}}
solution in
Algorithms

3
and
3
and
\mathsf{OPT}_{1}^{\mathsf{C3}}
solution in
Algorithm

3
can be done in constant time, as
G_{1}
is of constant size in this case.
Finding the corresponding sets of edges
F
in
Algorithm

3
can also be done in polynomial time in
\sigma
by enumeration, because
Lemmas

82
,
83
,
84
,
85
,
86
,
87
,
88
and
89
guarantee that there exists a desired set of edges
F
of constant size.
The same arguments can also be used to argue that
Algorithm

2
can be implemented in polynomial time.
Finally,
Algorithms

4
and
5
can clearly also be implemented in polynomial time. In particular, checking whether
G
contains a large
4
-vertex cut by
Definition

23
(2) can be done in polynomial time by enumerating all possible connectivity configurations of
H_{2}
, similar to enumerating the different solution types of 3-vertex cuts explained above.

To bound the total running time including recursive steps, let
T(\sigma)
be the running time of
Algorithm

1
as a function of
\sigma
. In each step, starting with a problem of size
\sigma
, we generate either a single subproblem of size
\sigma^{\prime}<\sigma
(to easily see this in
Algorithms

2
,
3
,
4
and
5
, as we assume that the cuts are sufficiently large) or two subproblems of size
\sigma^{\prime}
and
\sigma^{\prime\prime}
such that
\sigma^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime\prime}<\sigma
and
\sigma^{\prime}+\sigma^{\prime\prime}\leq\sigma
. Thus,
T(\sigma)\leq\max\{T(\sigma^{\prime}),T(\sigma^{\prime})+T(\sigma^{\prime\prime})\}+\mathrm{poly}(\sigma)
with
\sigma^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime\prime}<\sigma
and
\sigma^{\prime}+\sigma^{\prime\prime}\leq\sigma
. Therefore,
T(\sigma)\leq\sigma\cdot\mathrm{poly}(\sigma)
.
∎

Next, we show that
\mathsf{RED}(G)
returns a feasible solution to 2ECSS on
G
.

Lemma 101

(correctness)
.

For any 2ECSS instance
G
,
\mathsf{RED}(G)
returns a 2EC spanning subgraph of
G
.

Proof.

The proof is by induction over the tuple
(\lvert V(G)\rvert,\lvert E(G)\rvert)
. The base cases are given by
Algorithms

1
and
1
of
Algorithm

1
.
If we enter
Algorithm

1
, the claim trivially holds. If we call
\mathsf{ALG}
in
Algorithm

1
, the claim follows by the correctness of
\mathsf{ALG}
for
(\alpha,\varepsilon)
-structured instances.

If the condition in
Algorithm

1

applies, let
G_{i}=G[V_{i}\cup\{v\}]
. Note that both
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
, which must be 2EC since
G
is 2EC, each contain at most
|V(G)|-1
vertices, hence by induction hypothesis
\mathsf{RED}(G_{1})
and
\mathsf{RED}(G_{2})
are 2EC spanning subgraphs of
V_{1}\cup\{v\}
and
V_{2}\cup\{v\}
, respectively. Thus, their union is a 2EC spanning subgraph of
G
.
If the conditions in
Algorithm

1
or
Algorithm

1
apply,
G^{\prime}\coloneq G\setminus\{e\}
must be 2EC by
Lemmas

64
and
65
, respectively, and
|V(G^{\prime})|=|V(G)|
and
|E(G^{\prime})|=|E(G)|-1
. Hence, by induction hypothesis
\mathsf{RED}(G^{\prime})
is a 2EC spanning subgraph of
G^{\prime}
, and thus also for
G
.
If the condition in
Algorithm

1
applies, the claim follows from
61
.

It remains to consider the cases when
Algorithms

2
,
3
,
4
and
5
are executed.
Observe that in these cases,
G
is 2VC since the condition of
Algorithm

1
is checked before. Moreover, note that all recursive calls to
\mathsf{RED}
made in
Algorithms

2
,
3
and
4
consider graphs with strictly fewer vertices than
G
, hence the induction hypothesis applies.
If
Algorithm

1
executes
Algorithm

2
,
the correctness follows from the induction hypothesis,
Corollary

70
and
Lemmas

72
,
73
and
74
.
If
Algorithm

1
executes
Algorithm

3
,
the correctness follows from the induction hypothesis,
Corollary

70
and
Lemmas

83
,
84
,
85
,
86
,
87
,
88
and
89
.
If
Algorithm

1
executes
Algorithm

4
, the correctness follows from the induction hypothesis and
Lemma

93
, and if
Algorithm

1
executes
Algorithm

5
, the correctness follows from the induction hypothesis and
Lemmas

97
and
98
.
∎

The following lemma implies
\mathsf{red}(G)\leq(\alpha+4\varepsilon)\mathsf{opt}(G)
using the trivial bound
\mathsf{opt}(G)\geq|V(G)|
.

Lemma 102

(approximation guarantee)
.

For any 2ECSS instance
G
, (
1
) is satisfied. That is,

\mathsf{red}(G)\leq\begin{cases}\mathsf{opt}(G)&\quad\text{if }\lvert V(G)\rvert\leq\frac{8}{\varepsilon}\ ,\text{ and}\\
\alpha\cdot\mathsf{opt}(G)+4\varepsilon\cdot\lvert V(G)\rvert-16&\quad\text{if }\lvert V(G)\rvert>\frac{8}{\varepsilon}\ .\end{cases}

Proof.

The proof is by induction over the tuple
(\lvert V(G)\rvert,\lvert E(G)\rvert)
. The base cases are given by
Algorithms

1
and
1
of
Algorithm

1
.
If we enter
Algorithm

1
, the stated bound trivially holds.
If the condition in
Algorithm

1
or
Algorithm

1
applies, we consider a graph
G^{\prime}\coloneq G\setminus\{e\}
with the same number of vertices as
G
and the same optimal size of a 2ECSS by
Lemma

64
and
Lemma

65
, respectively. Thus, the claim follows from the induction hypothesis. If we call
\mathsf{ALG}
in
Algorithm

1
, the claim follows because we assume that
\mathsf{ALG}(G)
returns an
\alpha
-approximate solution to 2ECSS on
G
and
G
is in this case
(\alpha,\varepsilon)
-structured by the correctness of
\mathsf{RED}
(cf.
Lemma

101
).

If the condition in
Algorithm

1
applies, recall that by
Lemma

63
we have
\mathsf{opt}(G)=\mathsf{opt}(G_{1})+\mathsf{opt}(G_{2})
, where
G_{i}\coloneq G[V_{i}\cup\{v\}]
for
i\in\{1,2\}
. We distinguish a few cases depending on the sizes of
V_{1}
and
V_{2}
. Assume w.l.o.g.
|V_{1}|\leq|V_{2}|
.
If
|V_{2}|\leq\frac{8}{\varepsilon}
, then
\mathsf{red}(G)=\mathsf{opt}(G_{1})+\mathsf{opt}(G_{2})=\mathsf{opt}(G)\leq\alpha\cdot\mathsf{opt}(G)+4\varepsilon|V(G)|-16
since
\alpha\geq 1
and
|V(G)|\geq\frac{8}{\varepsilon}
.
Otherwise,
|V_{2}|\geq\frac{8}{\varepsilon}
.
If
|V_{1}|\leq\frac{8}{\varepsilon}
, then by the induction hypothesis we have
\mathsf{red}(G)\leq\mathsf{opt}(G_{1})+\alpha\mathsf{opt}(G_{2})+f(G_{2})\leq\alpha\mathsf{opt}(G)+f(G)
using
\alpha\geq 1
and
f(G_{2})\leq f(G)
.
Otherwise,
|V_{2}|\geq|V_{1}|\geq\frac{8}{\varepsilon}
, and the induction hypothesis gives

\mathsf{red}(G)\leq\alpha\mathsf{opt}(G_{1})+\alpha\mathsf{opt}(G_{2})+4\varepsilon(|V(G_{1})|+|V(G_{2})|)-16\leq\alpha\mathsf{opt}(G)+4\varepsilon|V(G)|+4\varepsilon-16\leq\alpha\mathsf{opt}(G)+f(G)
, because
4\varepsilon\leq 16
.

If the condition in
Algorithm

1
applies,
we have
\mathsf{opt}(G)=|\mathsf{OPT}(G)\cap{V(H)}^{2}|+|\mathsf{OPT}(G)\setminus{V(H)}^{2}|\geq\frac{1}{\alpha}|H|+\mathsf{opt}(G|H)
by the definition of a contractible subgraph
H
and observing that
\mathsf{OPT}(G)\setminus{V(H)}^{2}
induces a feasible 2EC spanning subgraph of
G|H
.
If
|V(G|H)|\leq\frac{8}{\varepsilon}
one has
\mathsf{red}(G)=|H|+\mathsf{opt}(G|H)\leq\alpha\mathsf{opt}(G)\leq\alpha\mathsf{opt}(G)+4\varepsilon|V(G)|-16
since
|V(G)|\geq\frac{8}{\varepsilon}
whenever
Algorithm

1
reaches
Algorithm

1
.
Otherwise,
|V(G|H)|>\frac{8}{\varepsilon}
, we conclude that
\mathsf{red}(G)\leq|H|+\alpha\mathsf{opt}(G|H)+4\varepsilon|V(G|H)|-4\leq\alpha\mathsf{opt}(G)+4\varepsilon|V(G)|-16
since
|V(G)|\geq|V(G|H)|
.

Finally, if
Algorithm

1
executes
Algorithms

2
,
3
,
4
and
5
, we have that
|V(G)|>\frac{8}{\varepsilon}
. Thus, in these cases the statement follows from the induction hypothesis and
Lemma

75
,
Lemma

90
,
Lemma

94
, or
Lemma

99
, respectively.
∎

Appendix B
Canonical 2-Edge Cover

See
6

Proof.

We first compute a triangle-free
2
-edge cover
H^{\prime}
, which can be done in polynomial time
[
KN23
,
Har24
]
, and show how to modify it to obtain a canonical
2
-edge cover with the same number of edges.
As in
[
Bos+25
]
, we can assume that in
H^{\prime}
, there is a component which has at least
32
vertices.
This can be achieved by guessing a subset of
31
edges in an optimal 2ECSS, which induce a connected graph.
We can fix these
31
edges to be in
H^{\prime}
by subdividing each edge into a path of length
2
so that any
2
-edge cover will include these paths.

Since
H^{\prime}
is a triangle-free
2
-edge cover, each component is either a 2EC component that contains at least
4
edges, or complex.

If
H^{\prime}
is canonical, we are done. Otherwise, we apply the following algorithm to
H^{\prime}
:
While there exist sets of edges
F_{A}\subseteq E\setminus H^{\prime}
and
F_{R}\subseteq H^{\prime}
with
|F_{A}|\leq|F_{R}|\leq 2
such that
H^{\prime\prime}\coloneq(H^{\prime}\setminus F_{R})\cup F_{A}
is a triangle-free
2
-edge cover and either
|H^{\prime\prime}|<|H^{\prime}|
, or
|H^{\prime\prime}|=|H^{\prime}|
and
H^{\prime\prime}
contains strictly less connected components than
H^{\prime}
, or
H^{\prime\prime}
contains strictly less bridges than
H^{\prime}
, or
H^{\prime\prime}
contains less cut vertices in 2EC components than
H^{\prime}
, we update
H^{\prime}\coloneq H^{\prime\prime}
.

Clearly, each iteration of this algorithm can be executed in polynomial time. Moreover, there can only be polynomially many iterations.
Thus, let
H
be equal to
H^{\prime}
when the algorithm terminates.
We prove that
H
is a canonical 2-edge cover of
G
. This proves the lemma because the algorithm does not increase the number of edge compared to a minimum triangle-free
2
-edge cover of
G
. To this end, we consider the following cases.

Case 1:

H
contains a 2EC component
C
such that
4\leq|E(C)|\leq 8
and
C
is not a cycle on
|E(C)|
vertices. We distinguish three exhaustive cases, and prove in any case that Case 1 cannot occur.

Case 1.1:

C
has a cut vertex
v
. Observe that
v
must have degree at least
4
in
C
.
Furthermore, each connected component in
C\setminus\{v\}
contains at least
2
vertices, as otherwise
C
contains parallel edges, a contradiction to
G
being structured.
Assume that there is a component
K_{1}
in
C\setminus\{v\}
with
2
vertices and let
u_{1}
and
u_{2}
be these vertices.
Note that the three edges
\{vu_{1},u_{1}u_{2},u_{2}v\}
must be in
C
as otherwise
G
is not structured since it must contain parallel edges.
If there exists an edge
u_{1}w\in E\setminus H
(or
u_{2}w\in E\setminus H
by symmetry) for
w\in V\setminus V(C)
, then we can remove
vu_{1}
from
H
and add
u_{1}w
to obtain a triangle-free
2
-edge cover
H^{\prime\prime}
with strictly less components and
|H|=|H^{\prime\prime}|
, a contradiction to the algorithm.
If there exists an edge
u_{1}w\in E\setminus H
(or
u_{2}w\in E
by symmetry) for
w\in V(C)\setminus\{v,u_{1},u_{2}\}
, then we can remove
vu_{1}
from
H
and add
u_{1}w
to obtain a triangle-free
2
-edge cover
H^{\prime\prime}
with
|H^{\prime\prime}|=|H|
in which
v
is not a cut vertex anymore.
If neither of these two cases apply, we conclude
\deg_{G}(u_{i})=2
for
i=1,2
. Thus,
v-u_{1}-u_{2}-v
is a contractible cycle, a contradiction to
G
being structured.
Now we assume each connected component in
C\setminus\{v\}
has at least
3
vertices (and at least
2
edges).
Since the degree of
v
is at least
4
in
C
and
|E(C)|\leq 8
, the only possible case is that
C
consists of two
4
-cycles intersecting only at one vertex
v
, say,
v-x_{1}-x_{2}-x_{3}-v
and
v-y_{1}-y_{2}-y_{3}-v
.
Note
x_{1},y_{1},x_{3},y_{3}
are symmetric.
We focus on
x_{1}
.
If there exists some edge
x_{1}w
for some
w\in V\setminus V(C)
,
then we can remove
x_{1}v
from
H
and add
x_{1}w
to obtain a triangle-free
2
-edge cover
H^{\prime\prime}
with
|H^{\prime\prime}|=|H|
with fewer components.
if
x_{1}w\in E(G)
for some
w\in\{y_{1},y_{2},y_{3}\}
, then we can remove
x_{1}v
from
H
and add
x_{1}w
to obtain a triangle-free
2
-edge cover
H^{\prime\prime}
with
|H^{\prime\prime}|=|H|
in which
v
is not a cut vertex anymore.
Hence either
x_{1}
(by symmetry also
x_{3}
) has degree
2
in
G
or
x_{1}x_{3}\in E(G)
.
In the former case,
x_{1}-x_{2}-x_{3}-v
is contractible.
The case for
y_{1},y_{3}
is symmetric.
So the only remaining case is that the degrees of
x_{1},x_{3},y_{1},y_{3}
in
G
are exactly
3
in
G
and
x_{1}x_{3},y_{1}y_{3}\in E(G)
.
Now we consider
x_{2}
.
There must be some edge
x_{2}w\in E
with
w\in V\setminus\{x_{1},x_{3},v\}
, as otherwise
v
is a cut vertex of
G
.
We add
x_{2}w
and
x_{1}x_{3}
to
H
, and remove
x_{1}v,x_{2}x_{3}
to obtain a triangle-free
2
-edge cover
H^{\prime\prime}
with
|H^{\prime\prime}|=|H|
.
If
w\notin C
,
H^{\prime\prime}
has fewer connected components.
If
w\in C
,
H^{\prime}
has the same number of components but fewer cut vertices.

Case 1.2:

C
contains no 2-vertex cut (and, thus,
C
is 3-vertex-connected ). This implies that every vertex has degree at least 3 in
C
, as otherwise the neighboring vertices of a vertex of
C
with degree
2
form a
2
-vertex cut.
But then it must hold that
4\leq|V(C)|\leq 5
, since
|V(C)|\leq 3
is not possible with the above degree constraint and if
|V(C)|\geq 6
, then the number of edges in
C
is at least
9
, a contradiction.
But if
|V(C)|=4
and
C
is
3
-vertex-connected,
C
must be a complete graph on
4
vertices. Therefore, there is an edge
e\in E(C)
such that
H\setminus\{e\}
is a triangle-free 2-edge cover, a contradiction to the algorithm.
Similarly, if
|V(C)|=5
, there must be exactly
8
edges and
4
vertices with degree
3
and
1
vertex with degree
4
.
Since
C
is
3
-vertex-connected, removing the only vertex with degree
4
from
C
results in a connected graph with every vertex having degree
2
, which must be a
\mathcal{C}_{4}
.
Hence
C
is
\mathcal{C}_{4}
plus one vertex connected to each vertex of the
\mathcal{C}_{4}
.
Therefore, there is an edge
e\in E(C)
such that
H\setminus\{e\}
is a triangle-free 2-edge cover, a contradiction to the algorithm.

Case 1.3:

C
contains a 2-vertex cut
\{v_{1},v_{2}\}
.
Note that each connected component of
C\setminus\{v_{1},v_{2}\}
must have an edge to both
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
, as otherwise
C
contains a cut vertex and we are in Case 1.1.
Hence, there are at most
4
connected components in
C\setminus\{v_{1},v_{2}\}
, as otherwise the number of edges in
C
is at least 10.

First, assume that
C\setminus\{v_{1},v_{2}\}
contains exactly
4
connected components.
Then each of the connected components in
C\setminus\{v_{1},v_{2}\}
must be a single vertex and we label them as
u_{1},u_{2},u_{3},u_{4}
.
If there is some edge between
u_{i}w
for some
i
and
w\in V\setminus V(C)
, then we can remove
v_{1}u_{i}
from
H
and add
u_{i}w
to obtain a triangle-free
2
-edge cover
H^{\prime\prime}
with strictly fewer components and
|H|=|H^{\prime\prime}|
, a contradiction to the algorithm.
If there is an edge between
u_{i}
and
u_{j}
in
G
for some
1\leq i<j\leq 4
, then we can remove
u_{i}v_{2},u_{j}v_{1}
from
H
and add
u_{i}u_{j}
to obtain a triangle-free 2-edge cover with fewer edges, which contradicts the algorithm.
Hence
u_{i}
has degree
2
in
G
for each
i
.
This implies that
C
is contractible since any optimal solution includes the incident edges of
u_{i}
and in total at least
8
edges in
C
.

Second, assume that
C\setminus\{v_{1},v_{2}\}
contains exactly
3
connected components.
Observe that each connected component of
C\setminus\{v_{1},v_{2}\}

has at most
3
vertices.
Otherwise
|E(C)|>8
.
If there is some connected component of
C\setminus\{v_{1},v_{2}\}
with
3
vertices (a path
u_{1}-u_{2}-u_{3}
),
then the other two connected components of
C\setminus\{v_{1},v_{2}\}
must consist of a single vertex, say,
u_{4}
and
u_{5}
.
In this case,
both
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
have some incident edge with the other endpoint in
\{u_{1},u_{2},u_{3}\}
.
Otherwise, either
v_{1}
or
v_{2}
is a cut vertex in
C
and we are in Case 1.1.
Further, since both
u_{1}
and
u_{3}
have at least
2
incident edges in
C
, both
u_{1}
and
u_{3}
have some incident edge with the other endpoint in
{v_{1},v_{2}}
.
Hence we can assume that
u_{1}v_{1}
and
u_{3}v_{2}
are in
E(C)
and
E(C)=\{u_{1}u_{2},u_{2}u_{3},u_{1}v_{1},u_{3}v_{2},v_{1}u_{4},v_{1}u_{5},v_{2}u_{4},v_{2}u_{5}\}
.
Similar to the previous arguments in the last paragraph, there is no edge
u_{i}w
in
G
for
i\neq 2
and
w\in V\setminus V(C)
, no edge between
\{u_{1},u_{3}\}
and
\{u_{4},u_{5}\}
, and
u_{4}u_{5}\notin E(G)
.
So the optimal solution has to include at least
2
incident edges of
u_{1},u_{3},u_{4},u_{5}
and hence at least
7
edges in
G[C]
, which implies
C
is contractible.
Now consider the case where each connected component of
C\setminus\{v_{1},v_{2}\}
has at most
2
vertices.
By the same arguments in the previous case, there is no edge between these connected component of
C\setminus\{v_{1},v_{2}\}
in
G
and there is no edge between these components and
V\setminus V(C)
.
Hence we can argue that each vertex in
C\ \{v_{1},v_{2}\}
has degree
2
in
G
and hence
C
is contractible.

Hence, assume that
C\setminus\{v_{1},v_{2}\}
contains exactly 2 connected components
K_{1}
and
K_{2}
and without loss of generality assume
|E(K_{1})|\geq|E(K_{2})|
.
Recall that each connected component of
C\setminus\{v_{1},v_{2}\}
must have an edge to both
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
.
Hence,
\{v_{1},v_{2}\}
is incident to at least 4 edges in
C
. Thus,
|E(K_{2})|\leq|E(K_{1})|\leq 3
.
If
|E(K_{1})|=3
,
K_{1}
is a path on 4 vertices, or a triangle on 3 vertices, or a star on 4 vertices (one center and 3 leaves).
If
K_{1}
is a triangle, then either there is an edge
e
in
C
such that
H\setminus\{e\}
is a triangle-free
2
-edge cover (a contradiction to the algorithm) or one vertex in triangle is a cut vertex of
C
(we are in Case 1.1).
If
K_{1}
is a star on 4 vertices, then there must be 3 edges from the leaves of
K_{1}
to
\{v_{1},v_{2}\}
, as otherwise
H
is not a 2-edge cover.
Then
C
contains exactly 8 edges.
Let
u_{1}
be the center of the star
K_{1}
, and let
u_{2},u_{3},u_{4}
be the leaves of the star.
Let
u_{5}
be the only vertex in
K_{2}
.
Then by symmetry we can assume that

E(C)=\{u_{1}u_{2},u_{1}u_{3},u_{1}u_{4},v_{1}u_{2},v_{1}u_{3},v_{2}u_{4},v_{1}u_{5},v_{2}u_{5}\}
.
By similar arguments in the previous cases, there is no edge in
G
between
\{u_{2},u_{3},u_{4},u_{5}\}
and
V\setminus V(C)
.
Then we claim that
G[\{u_{2},u_{3},u_{4},u_{5}\}]
either has no edge or has only one edge
u_{4}u_{5}
, which implies that any optimal solution has to include at least
7
edges in
G[C]
and
C
is contractible.
To prove the claim, if
u_{2}u_{3}\in G
, then we can remove
v_{1}u_{2},u_{1}u_{3}
from
H
and add
u_{2}u_{3}

to obtain a triangle-free
2
-edge cover with fewer edges.
If
u_{2}u_{5}\in E(G)
,
then we can remove
u_{1}u_{2},v_{1}u_{5}
from
H
and add
u_{2}u_{5}

to obtain a triangle-free
2
-edge cover with fewer edges.
The case for
u_{3}u_{5}
is symmetric.
If
u_{3}u_{4}\in E(G)
, then we can remove
v_{1}u_{3},u_{1}u_{3}
from
H
and add
u_{3}u_{4}

to obtain a triangle-free
2
-edge cover with fewer edges.
The case for
u_{2}u_{4}
is symmetric.
We conclude with the claim and that
K_{1}
cannot be a star.
Finally consider the case where
K_{1}
is a path on 4 vertices.
But then one leaf of
K_{1}
must be adjacent to
v_{1}
in
C
and the other leaf of
K_{1}
must be adjacent to
v_{2}
in
C
. Since
K_{2}
is a single vertex in this case and must be incident to both
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
,
C
contains a
\mathcal{C}_{7}
as a spanning subgraph, a contradiction.
Hence, we have that
|E(K_{2})|\leq|E(K_{1})|\leq 2
.
Since
G
does not contain parallel edges,
K_{1}
must be a simple path on
i
vertices for some
i\in\{1,2,3\}
. The same is true for
K_{2}
.
By similar reasoning as before we can observe that the leaves of
K_{1}
and
K_{2}
, respectively, must be incident to
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
(or, if
K_{1}
or
K_{2}
is a single vertex, then it must be incident to both
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
) and therefore we observe that
C
contains a spanning cycle as a subgraph, a contradiction.

Case 2:

There is some complex component
C
of
H
containing a pendant block
B
with less than 6 edges.
Assume there is a pendant block
B
with exactly 5 edges. The cases in which
B
has 3 or 4 edges is similar.
Note that
B
must contain either 4 or 5 vertices.
If
B
contains exactly
4
vertices then
B
must contain a simple cycle on 4 vertices with an additional edge
e
being a chord of this cycle, which is redundant, and, thus, a contradiction to the algorithm.
Hence, assume that
B
contains exactly
5
vertices, i.e.,
B
is a cycle
b_{1}-b_{2}-b_{3}-b_{4}-b_{5}-b_{1}
, and assume that
b_{1}
is incident to the unique bridge
f
in
C
.
If either
b_{2}
(or
b_{5}
by symmetry) is incident to an edge
e
with neighbor in
V\setminus V(B)
we can remove
b_{1}b_{2}
from
H
and add
e
to obtain a triangle-free
2
-edge cover
H^{\prime\prime}
. If
e
is incident to some vertex in
V\setminus V(C)
, the number of connected components in
H^{\prime\prime}
is strictly less compared to
H
, and otherwise, that is,
e
is incident to some vertex in
V(C)\setminus V(B)
, or
H^{\prime\prime}
has strictly less bridges compared to
H
. This is a contradiction to the algorithm. Thus, in the following we can assume that
b_{2}
and
b_{5}
are only adjacent to vertices of
V(B)
.

By
Lemma

4
,

b_{3}
and
b_{4}
both must be incident to some edge
e_{3}
and
e_{4}
going to some vertex in
V\setminus V(B)
, respectively.

If
b_{2}b_{5}\in E(G)
,
then we can remove
b_{2}b_{3}
and
b_{1}b_{5}
from
H
and add
b_{2}b_{5}
and
e_{3}
to obtain a triangle-free
2
-edge cover
H^{\prime\prime}
.
If
e_{3}
is incident to some vertex
V\setminus V(C)
, the number of connected components in
H^{\prime\prime}
is strictly less compared to
H
, and otherwise, that is,
e_{3}
is incident to some vertex
V(C)\setminus V(B)
,
H^{\prime\prime}
has strictly less bridges compared to
H
. This is a contradiction to the algorithm.

If
b_{3}b_{5}\in E(G)
,
then we can remove
b_{1}b_{5}
and
b_{3}b_{4}
from
H
from
H
and add
b_{3}b_{5}
and
e_{4}
to obtain a triangle-free
2
-edge cover
H^{\prime\prime}
.
If
e_{4}
is incident to some vertex
V\setminus V(C)
, the number of connected components in
H^{\prime\prime}
is strictly less compared to
H
, and otherwise, that is,
e_{4}
is incident to some vertex
V(C)\setminus V(B)
,
H^{\prime\prime}
has strictly less bridges compared to
H
.
By symmetry, we also have
b_{2}b_{4}\notin E(G)
.
Hence
C
is a
\mathcal{C}_{5}
with
b_{2},b_{5}
having degree
2
in
G
.

Case 3:

There is some complex component
C
of
H
containing a non-pendant block
B
that contains less than
5
edges.
If
B
has
3
edges, i.e.,
B
is a triangle.
In this case, we observe that in any case there must be an edge
e
in
B
such that
H\setminus\{e\}
is a triangle-free
2
-edge cover, a contradiction to the algorithm.
If
B
has
4
edges, i.e.,
B
is a
\mathcal{C}_{4}
.
We number the vertices as
b_{1},b_{2},b_{3},b_{4}
.
If there are two adjacent vertices
b_{i}
and
b_{i+1}
(
b_{1}
and
b_{4}
are also adjacent), then
H\setminus\{b_{i}b_{i+1}\}
is a triangle-free
2
-edge cover, a contradiction to the algorithm.
Since
B
is a non-pendant block, w.l.o.g. we assume that
b_{1}
and
b_{3}
are incident to some bridge in
C
,
and
b_{2}
and
b_{4}
are vertices of degree
2
in
C
.
If
b_{2}b_{4}\in E
, we can add the edge
b_{2}b_{4}
, and remove
b_{1}b_{4}
and
b_{2}b_{3}
to obtain a triangle-free
2
-edge cover with fewer edges, a contradiction to the algorithm.
If
b_{2}w\in E
for some
w\notin V(B)
, then we can add
b_{2}w
and remove
b_{2}b_{3}
to obtain a triangle-free
2
-edge cover
H^{\prime\prime}
with either fewer bridges (if
w\in V(C)
) or fewer connected components (if
w\in V\setminus V(C)
).
It holds the same for
b_{4}
.
Hence we can assume that
b_{2},b_{4}
are vertices of degree
2
in
G
, which implies that
B
is contractible, a contraction.

Case 4:

There are two non-complex components of
H
that are both
\mathcal{C}_{4}
s and can be merged into a single
\mathcal{C}_{8}
by removing two edges of
H
and adding two edges.
We can enumerate all pairs of
\mathcal{C}_{4}
s in
H
that are adjacent in
G
and check whether they can be merged into a
\mathcal{C}_{8}
.
Then we obtain
H^{\prime\prime}
with fewer components and
|H^{\prime\prime}|=|H|
.
∎
